{
  "report": "# Mechanistic report: bounded aqueous acid dissociation and precipitation response\n\n## 1. Main interpretation\n\nThe observations support a concentration-dependent, saturating acid-response system. Increasing nominal reagent loading per unit water volume initially lowers pH and lowers the reported acid-dissociation fraction. Above approximately 0.03\u20130.1 mol/L nominal loading, both responses flatten substantially. Matched-concentration preparations at different scales and two staged-preparation experiments are consistent with an endpoint controlled primarily by concentration rather than total amount or preparation history, within the tested conditions and measurement precision.\n\nA useful provisional explanation has two components:\n\n1. Nominal reagent concentration is transformed into a bounded effective acid concentration or activity scale.\n2. An approximately constant effective acid-dissociation relationship couples that scale to pH and the reported dissociation fraction.\n\nThe precipitation signal is much less informative. It remains approximately 0.14\u20130.16 in the final assays, without a clearly resolved precipitation threshold or monotonic increase across the loading series. It is a normalized proxy, not a measured solid mass or precipitated fraction. These experiments therefore do not establish that precipitation causes the acid-response plateau.\n\nThis is an effective description of an anonymous synthetic benchmark, not identification of a real acid, a solid phase, or a thermodynamic mechanism. Several physically and computationally different explanations remain observationally equivalent.\n\n## 2. Evidence and experimental scope\n\nThe completed campaign comprised 12 batches, 12 intermediate pH-meter measurements, and 12 final assays. Every batch was explicitly terminated before its final assay. The campaign used 76 laboratory operations. No batch was discarded, and no rejected laboratory operation appeared in the recorded execution. A reporting-script error occurred after the last successful assay because the terminal response had no next-state object; the subsequent public status response confirmed that all 12 batches had completed. This error did not constitute an additional experiment or invalidate the recorded final assay.\n\nAll preparations used water and the anonymous limiting reagent. No catalyst, other solvent, heat operation, or quench was used. Direct preparations received a 120-second wait at 400 rpm before the intermediate measurement and termination. The staged preparations received one such wait before the intermediate measurement and another after the subsequent addition. Initial temperature was approximately 297.2 K; the first measured wait ended at approximately 297.26 K. This was an ambient-temperature campaign, not a temperature study.\n\nThe initial experiment note specified a loading series, scale comparisons, repeatability, and staged preparation. Exact staged endpoints were selected during the exploratory campaign after the loading-sensitive region became evident. Consequently, this is exploratory development evidence, not a fully fixed quantitative confirmatory test. No prior local acid law or task-specific property dossier was supplied.\n\n### Final-assay observations\n\nHere, C_nom is the nominal total reagent added divided by total water added. For staged batches, it labels the intended endpoint before accounting for the intermediate sample withdrawal. pH is calculated from the declared measurement convention, pH = 14 \u00d7 pH_normalized. Alpha denotes acid_dissociation_fraction, and P denotes precipitation_signal.\n\n| Batch | Reagent added, mol | Water added, L | Endpoint C_nom, mol/L | Preparation | Final pH | Final alpha | Final P |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|\n| 1 | 0.000040 | 0.040 | 0.001 | Direct | 3.9927 | 0.16300 | 0.13659 |\n| 2 | 0.000120 | 0.040 | 0.003 | Direct | 3.8122 | 0.12368 | 0.15405 |\n| 3 | 0.000400 | 0.040 | 0.010 | Direct | 3.6221 | 0.09970 | 0.15539 |\n| 4 | 0.001200 | 0.040 | 0.030 | Direct | 3.5978 | 0.07716 | 0.15824 |\n| 5 | 0.004000 | 0.040 | 0.100 | Direct | 3.5291 | 0.07852 | 0.15903 |\n| 6 | 0.012000 | 0.040 | 0.300 | Direct | 3.5286 | 0.06923 | 0.14434 |\n| 7 | 0.040000 | 0.040 | 1.000 | Direct | 3.5712 | 0.06882 | 0.16134 |\n| 8 | 0.002000 | 0.020 | 0.100 | Direct, smaller scale | 3.5491 | 0.07207 | 0.15378 |\n| 9 | 0.008000 | 0.080 | 0.100 | Direct, larger scale | 3.5977 | 0.06885 | 0.15076 |\n| 10 | 0.012000 | 0.040 | 0.300 | Direct repeat | 3.5376 | 0.06767 | 0.15653 |\n| 11 | 0.000120 | 0.040 | approximately 0.003 | Diluted from 0.030 mol/L | 3.8362 | 0.13119 | 0.14348 |\n| 12 | 0.000120 | 0.040 | approximately 0.003 | Loaded upward from 0.001 mol/L | 3.8006 | 0.11609 | 0.14330 |\n\nThe displayed digits preserve useful comparisons; they do not imply that the measurements are accurate to all those decimal places.\n\n### Sampling corrections\n\nThe intermediate pH-meter measurement consumed 0.000030 L. Final assays consumed 0.000300 L, but their input sample state preceded that final withdrawal. Direct 0.040 L preparations therefore had 0.039970 L immediately before their final assays. Homogeneous withdrawal would remove dissolved reagent and liquid proportionally, leaving concentration unchanged; simply dividing the original reagent amount by the reduced volume would then be incorrect.\n\nFor batch 11, 0.000120 mol was initially added to 0.004 L. After the intermediate sample, 0.036 L water was added. Assuming homogeneous proportional withdrawal, the endpoint concentration was approximately 0.0029797 mol/L rather than exactly 0.003.\n\nFor batch 12, 0.000040 mol was initially added to 0.040 L. After the intermediate sample, another 0.000080 mol reagent was added. Under the same assumption, the endpoint concentration was approximately 0.0030015 mol/L.\n\nThese are calculated nominal-inventory corrections, not measured dissolved-species concentrations. Their effects are small relative to the observed measurement scatter, but heterogeneous sampling would require a different correction.\n\n## 3. What the loading series establishes\n\nBatches 1\u20137 changed reagent loading at fixed added water volume. From 0.001 to 0.010 mol/L, final pH decreased from 3.9927 to 3.6221, and alpha decreased from 0.1630 to 0.0997. This is a resolved loading effect, well beyond the declared final-assay noise.\n\nThe response then flattened. Increasing nominal concentration from 0.1 to 1 mol/L did not produce a further substantial pH decrease: batches 5, 6, and 7 gave pH 3.5291, 3.5286, and 3.5712. Their alpha values were 0.0785, 0.0692, and 0.0688. The small upward pH change at the highest loading is not convincing evidence of a reversal; its magnitude is comparable to assay uncertainty.\n\nAcross the six direct final assays at nominal concentrations of at least 0.1 mol/L\u2014batches 5\u201310\u2014the descriptive mean pH was 3.5522 and mean alpha was 0.07086. These means summarize the tested plateau region; they are not independently established asymptotes.\n\nAn ideal, unbuffered monoprotic acid with dissolved analytical concentration equal to nominal added concentration and constant dissociation constant would not naturally produce this pronounced plateau. In its weak-dissociation regime, increasing concentration tenfold would lower pH by approximately 0.5 units. The high-loading observations show no comparable decrease. Thus, the simplest identification of nominal reagent concentration with freely dissolved acid concentration is inadequate.\n\n## 4. A provisional acid-dissociation relationship\n\nThe coupled pH and alpha observations nevertheless resemble an approximately constant effective acid-dissociation relation.\n\nDefine h = 10^(-pH). Strictly, pH defines a hydrogen-ion activity scale; treating h as a molar concentration is an additional ideal-solution approximation. For a monoprotic acid equilibrium,\n\nHA \u21cc H+ + A\u2212,\n\nan effective relationship is\n\nKa_eff = h \u00d7 alpha / (1 \u2212 alpha),\n\nor equivalently,\n\nalpha = Ka_eff / (Ka_eff + h).\n\nUsing the final pH and alpha values from batches 1\u20137 gives the following derived Ka_eff values:\n\n| Batch | Derived Ka_eff |\n|---|---:|\n| 1 | 1.98 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 2 | 2.17 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 3 | 2.64 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 4 | 2.11 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 5 | 2.52 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 6 | 2.20 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n| 7 | 1.98 \u00d7 10^-5 |\n\nThe relatively narrow range is notable because nominal concentration spans a factor of 1,000. A representative effective scale is Ka_eff around 2.2 \u00d7 10^-5, corresponding to pKa_eff around 4.65. Individual values span approximately pKa 4.58\u20134.70.\n\nThese calculations support an economical coupling between the two reported channels, but they do not establish a thermodynamic constant. The quantities are derived from noisy measurements, their errors may be correlated, and both channels may be generated by a shared synthetic state or calibration. The numerical range is descriptive, not a confidence interval or a measured temperature-independent material property.\n\n### Conditional effective-concentration reconstruction\n\nUnder the stronger assumptions of a single acid, negligible background ions, ideal activities, and charge balance h approximately equal to alpha \u00d7 C_eff,\n\nC_eff approximately equals h / alpha.\n\nApplying this conditional reconstruction to batches 1\u20137 gives:\n\n| Nominal concentration, mol/L | Derived C_eff, mol/L under the stated assumptions |\n|---:|---:|\n| 0.001 | 0.000624 |\n| 0.003 | 0.001246 |\n| 0.010 | 0.002395 |\n| 0.030 | 0.003272 |\n| 0.100 | 0.003767 |\n| 0.300 | 0.004277 |\n| 1.000 | 0.003900 |\n\nThis reconstruction suggests that the acid-responsive pool approaches a scale of roughly 0.004 mol/L even while nominal loading continues to rise. It should not be mistaken for a direct dissolved-acid assay. The derivation fails if there are relevant counterions, buffers, background acidity, nonideal activities, or a different denominator for the reported dissociation fraction.\n\n## 5. A compact candidate process model\n\nOne possible effective model is:\n\nC_nom = retained nominal reagent inventory / liquid volume\n\nC_eff = f(C_nom)\n\nh = [sqrt(Ka_eff^2 + 4 \u00d7 Ka_eff \u00d7 C_eff) \u2212 Ka_eff] / 2\n\nalpha = Ka_eff / (Ka_eff + h)\n\npH_normalized = \u2212log10(h) / 14\n\nThe quadratic expression for h is the ideal single-acid charge-balance solution, neglecting water autoionization and background ions. Its use here is a hypothesis, not an assertion that the benchmark explicitly implements that equation.\n\nThe data require f to become strongly sublinear over the tested loading range if this acid model is used. An illustrative form is\n\nf(C) = C_max \u00d7 C / (C_half + C).\n\nThe reconstructed values suggest C_max on the order of 0.004 mol/L and C_half on the order of several millimolar. These are rough scales, not fitted or uniquely identified parameters. I have not demonstrated that this particular rational saturation function is superior to another smooth saturation law, a rounded solubility cap, or a buffered activity model.\n\nThis candidate model captures the main observations: more reagent at fixed volume lowers pH and alpha in the dilute region; the response becomes weak at high loading; equal concentrations at different scales produce similar endpoints; and dilution moves the response back toward the dilute regime.\n\nIt should not be extended to zero loading without modification. No water-only blank was measured, and background acidity or water autoionization would matter outside the tested range.\n\n## 6. Experiments separating concentration, scale, and history\n\n### Matched-concentration scale comparisons\n\nBatches 5, 8, and 9 all targeted 0.1 mol/L but used 0.040, 0.020, and 0.080 L water, respectively. Final pH values were 3.5291, 3.5491, and 3.5977; alpha values were 0.07852, 0.07207, and 0.06885; precipitation proxies were 0.15903, 0.15378, and 0.15076.\n\nThe largest pH difference was 0.0686. Given a declared final-assay pH standard deviation of 0.028 per observation, an independent pairwise difference has a standard deviation of approximately 0.0396. Thus, the largest difference is about 1.7 such standard deviations. The alpha and precipitation differences are also modest relative to their declared pairwise uncertainty.\n\nThese comparisons support concentration as a useful predictor and do not establish an independent volume or total-amount effect. However, the comparison was made in the relatively flat high-loading region, where a scale effect could be difficult to detect. It is not a universal proof of scale invariance.\n\n### Direct repeat\n\nBatches 6 and 10 repeated the 0.012 mol in 0.040 L preparation. Their pH values differed by 0.0090 and alpha by 0.00156, demonstrating close agreement in the acid channels for this pair. Their precipitation proxies differed by 0.01218, illustrating that small proxy differences should not be overinterpreted. One repeat pair does not establish a full repeatability distribution.\n\n### Dilution\n\nBatch 11 began at nominal 0.03 mol/L in 0.004 L. Its intermediate pH-meter reading gave pH 3.5866, alpha 0.07709, and P 0.17501. After adding 0.036 L water and waiting again, the final assay gave pH 3.8362, alpha 0.13119, and P 0.14348.\n\nThe pH increase of about 0.250 and alpha increase of about 0.054 are consistent with dilution of an acid-responsive system. The endpoint closely approached direct batch 2: pH differed by 0.0241 and alpha by 0.00751. The precipitation change is less decisive because the intermediate instrument has substantially greater proxy noise than the final assay.\n\n### Staged loading\n\nBatch 12 began at nominal 0.001 mol/L. Its intermediate pH-meter reading gave pH 3.9353, alpha 0.15391, and P 0.12327. After adding another 0.000080 mol reagent and waiting again, its final values were pH 3.8006, alpha 0.11609, and P 0.14330.\n\nThe direction is opposite to dilution and consistent with the loading series. The final endpoint again approached batch 2: pH differed by \u22120.0116 and alpha by \u22120.00759.\n\nTogether, batches 2, 11, and 12 are consistent with convergence toward similar endpoints from direct preparation, higher initial concentration, and lower initial concentration. They do not resolve appreciable hysteresis under these protocols. They also do not prove thermodynamic reversibility: different instruments were used before and after each intervention, staged batches had an additional wait, and there was no dedicated kinetic time series.\n\nBatch 2 was selected as the campaign's recommended completed experiment because it anchors these comparisons in the loading-sensitive region, not because it maximizes a score.\n\n## 7. What can be said about precipitation\n\nAcross all 12 final assays, P ranged from 0.13659 to 0.16134. Batch 1 had the lowest final value, and batch 7 the highest, but the intervening loading series was not monotonic. In particular, batch 6 gave P = 0.14434 at 0.3 mol/L, whereas its repeat, batch 10, gave 0.15653.\n\nThe difference between batches 1 and 7 is approximately 0.02475, about 2.9 independent pairwise standard deviations using the declared final-assay proxy noise of 0.006. That is enough to motivate a possible small loading-related change, but it is an endpoint comparison among several observations, with no established zero baseline and no resolved functional form. It is not evidence for a sharp precipitation boundary.\n\nA nearly constant proxy around 0.15 is therefore a useful first approximation over the tested range, with possible small concentration dependence and measurement scatter. The data do not identify a solubility product, solid stoichiometry, dissolved-ion product, nucleation rate, or precipitated mass.\n\nPossible interpretations include a nonzero proxy baseline, a bounded response that is already weakly sensitive in this regime, or genuine but modest changes in a precipitation-related state. The acid-response saturation could be connected to precipitation, but it could equally arise elsewhere in the model. The present evidence cannot distinguish these cases.\n\n## 8. Competing explanations and identifiability\n\n### A. Saturating availability followed by acid equilibrium\n\nOnly a bounded portion of nominal reagent contributes to the acid-responsive dissolved pool. The remaining material may be unavailable, partitioned, or represented by a benchmark-specific transformation. This explains both the approximately constant effective Ka and the reconstructed saturation scale. It does not identify what limits availability.\n\n### B. Solubility-limited acid concentration\n\nA dissolved pool could approach a solubility ceiling, producing nearly constant pH and alpha at high total loading. This is plausible but unconfirmed. There is no direct phase-composition measurement, and the precipitation proxy does not display a compelling corresponding threshold. A simple hard cap would also need a transition mechanism to explain the smooth low-loading curvature.\n\n### C. Buffering, background ions, or nonideal activities\n\nNominal reagent might dissolve without a corresponding increase in hydrogen activity because of buffering, coupled ion equilibria, or activity effects. Such mechanisms can invalidate C_eff = h/alpha while preserving an approximate pH\u2013alpha relationship. No ionic-strength, counterion, or buffer-composition measurement was available to test this alternative.\n\n### D. Shared synthetic response or calibration\n\nThe benchmark may transform nominal concentration into a bounded latent variable and generate pH and alpha from that variable. Agreement with a mass-action equation would then be an effective representation rather than independent confirmation of microscopic acid chemistry. Similarly, chemical saturation cannot be fully separated from nonlinear observable generation using these processed endpoints alone.\n\n### E. Kinetic or preparation-history effects below resolution\n\nThe measured endpoint may be a reproducible finite-time state rather than equilibrium. The staged results limit large history effects at the tested endpoints, but do not identify relaxation times, slow precipitation, or delayed redissolution. No waiting-time series or controlled temperature series was performed.\n\nThe observations are strongest on the existence of a saturating response and the approximate pH\u2013alpha coupling. They are weaker on why saturation occurs and much weaker on the physical meaning of the precipitation proxy.\n\n## 9. Measurement uncertainty and diagnostics\n\nThe declared final-assay standard deviations were 0.002 for pH_normalized, equivalent to 0.028 pH units, and 0.006 each for alpha and P. The intermediate pH meter declared 0.004 for pH_normalized, equivalent to 0.056 pH units, 0.015 for alpha, and 0.020 for P.\n\nUnder a Gaussian approximation, a single final-assay observation would therefore have an instrument-only approximate 95% span of \u00b10.055 pH units or \u00b10.012 for alpha and P. These are not complete predictive intervals: they omit model discrepancy, possible inter-channel correlation, systematic calibration effects, and uncertainty in the mapping from nominal loading to chemical state. Clipping also makes Gaussian approximations inappropriate near bounded endpoints.\n\nAll final equilibrium_residual values lay between zero and approximately 0.00770. Those small reported residuals are compatible with internally settled benchmark states, but the residual is an environment diagnostic rather than an independently measured chemical imbalance. Several zeros may reflect bounded noisy reporting rather than exactly zero physical residual.\n\nLikewise, equilibrium_confidence is an environment diagnostic, not my uncertainty and not a research success measure. Its changes across loading were not used to assign confidence to the mechanism or to select a supposedly optimal chemical preparation. The native scalar score was also not treated as the research objective.\n\n## 10. Supported range, extrapolation, and conclusions\n\nThe directly tested nominal concentration range was 0.001\u20131 mol/L in water. Most direct preparations used 0.040 L; matched-concentration scale comparisons covered 0.020\u20130.080 L at 0.1 mol/L. Batch 11 additionally used an initial 0.004 L stage before dilution. The observations apply to the anonymous reagent, ambient conditions near 297 K, the stated mixing and waiting protocol, and these public synthetic measurement channels.\n\nThe following conclusions are supported within that scope:\n\n- Increasing low-range nominal loading lowers pH and the reported dissociation fraction.\n- The acid response becomes strongly sublinear and approximately flat at higher loading.\n- An effective pH\u2013alpha relation with Ka on the order of 2 \u00d7 10^-5 is consistent with the final observations.\n- A saturating effective acid pool is a compact explanation, but not a directly measured species inventory.\n- Matched concentrations at different scales and staged preparation endpoints agree within the available precision; large scale or history effects were not resolved.\n- The precipitation proxy changes little and does not identify a precipitation mechanism or threshold.\n\nThe candidate model would qualitatively predict that further dilution within the measured low-loading region raises pH and alpha, whereas further loading within the high-loading region changes them relatively little. Those are model-based interpolative expectations, not additional observations. Predictions below 0.001 mol/L, above 1 mol/L, at substantially different temperature, with catalysts or other solvents, or after different waiting times would be extrapolations with substantially greater uncertainty.\n\nThe main scientific result is therefore not a uniquely identified microscopic mechanism. It is a constrained effective account: nominal concentration controls a saturating acid response; pH and dissociation are approximately mass-action-compatible; and the available precipitation signal does not reveal the origin of that saturation. No new experiments were performed for this report, and the earlier sealed campaign handoff and recommendation remain unchanged."
}

Which experiments actually shaped the explanation\n\n### The loading series established the main phenomenon\n\nBatches 1\u20133 showed a substantial change in the acid channels: final pH decreased from 3.9927 to 3.6221 and dissociation from 0.16300 to 0.09970. Its intermediate pH was 3.5866 and its final pH was 3.8362; dissociation increased from 0.07709 to 0.13119. Batch 12 moved in the opposite loading direction, from 0.001 to approximately 0.003 mol/L, and its final endpoint was also close to direct batch 2.\n\nThese were more informative than additional plateau points because they intervened on preparation history and approached a shared endpoint. They supported the statement that large history effects were not resolved under the tested waiting protocol. They did not prove reversibility or exclude short-time hysteresis.\n\nThe exact staged endpoints were selected after the sensitive region became apparent. That was an adaptive exploratory choice, and K1 appropriately disclosed it. The initial note fixed broad comparison types but did not fully fix the numerical matrix or quantitative decision rules. Likewise, reconstructing C_eff = h/alpha and proposing a saturating transformation were post-campaign analytical interpretations.\n\nThis chronology matters: the campaign discovered response flattening; the mass-action-compatible account was subsequently constructed to explain the measured coupling. The most important competing explanations\n\nThe central unresolved issue is whether the plateau reflects a bounded chemical pool, a bounded activity response, or a bounded observation-generating mechanism.\n\n**Saturating availability followed by acid equilibrium.** K1's compact model treats nominal concentration as an input to a saturating effective acid concentration, followed by an approximately constant acid-dissociation relation. It explains the loading trend and the approximate pH\u2013alpha coupling economically. The effective pool is inferred, not directly measured.\n\n**Solubility-limited dissolved concentration.** A dissolved acid pool could approach a ceiling while total material continues to increase. It is not legitimate to equate the fitted effective pool with dissolved acid and then call its saturation proof of precipitation.\n\n**Buffering, counterions, or activity effects.** These can restrict hydrogen activity even if more reagent remains dissolved. They undermine the charge-balance assumption behind C_eff = h/alpha without necessarily destroying the empirical pH\u2013alpha relationship. No independent ionic-strength or counterion measurement was made.\n\n**Shared synthetic mapping or calibration.** Both reported acid channels could be generated from a common latent state. Their agreement with an acid equation would then identify an effective mapping, not independently verify microscopic chemistry. Purchased processed channels are not automatically independent evidence for each other.\n\n**Finite-time relaxation and preparation history.** The endpoints may be reproducible after 120 seconds without being instantaneous equilibrium states. The staged comparisons constrain large persistent differences after waiting, but they do not determine relaxation rates or behavior immediately after the final addition.\n\nThe experiments distinguish a strongly responsive low-loading region from a weakly responsive high-loading region. They weigh against a simple, fully available ideal acid whose analytical concentration is identical to nominal loading. They also weigh against large endpoint history effects under the tested conditions.\n\nThey do not distinguish saturation of dissolved concentration from buffering or synthetic response saturation. They do not identify a precipitation mechanism, and they do not establish instantaneous equilibration. These remain genuine competing explanations, not merely alternative labels for a mechanism already demonstrated.\n\n## 4. One additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nAs a hypothetical additional experiment with a fresh allowance\u2014not an action available within the already exhausted campaign\u2014I would choose a low-loading preparation with an immediate intermediate measurement followed by a waiting interval and final assay:\n\n1. I would not actually execute them now.\n\nThis choice addresses two major weaknesses with one vessel: dilute-limit behavior and possible sensitivity to the omitted wait in Q. It would count against the particular dilute extrapolation used for Q03.\n- **The immediate and delayed measurements differ substantially beyond their combined uncertainties:** this would weaken the use of waited campaign endpoints to predict immediate termination. A single cross-instrument comparison would still need replication before attributing the difference entirely to kinetics.\n- **pH shifts as predicted but alpha does not preserve the approximate effective Ka relation:** this would weaken the proposed common acid-equilibrium coupling and strengthen explanations involving multiple states, a changing fraction denominator, or channel-specific mappings.\n- **The precipitation proxy remains near 0.15 despite substantially greater dissociation:** this would favor the baseline explanation over the decreasing proxy extrapolation used in Q03, Q08, and Q09.\n\nI prefer this experiment to another high-loading point because the existing plateau is already comparatively well documented, whereas the dilute limit and no-wait protocol are major sources of predictive uncertainty.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operation score\n\nThe campaign was directed toward characterization rather than score optimization. This influenced several choices: I spent vessels on a concentration series, matched-concentration scale comparisons, a direct repeat, and two different routes to the same endpoint. These are informative comparisons even when they do not improve the native scalar score.\n\nBatch 2 was selected as the recommendation because it anchored the sensitive-region preparation comparisons. It was not selected as the highest-scoring batch. For example, the recorded final score of batch 1 was approximately 0.304, while batch 2's displayed final channels imply a lower score under the published weighting. The recommendation was evidentiary, not an optimization claim.\n\nThere is no evidence in my recorded choices that I sacrificed identifiability to chase the score. I did not screen catalysts, alter solvent identity, or seek conditions that would raise equilibrium_confidence. That diagnostic was not treated as my confidence in the mechanism.\n\nNevertheless, the characterization design itself had inefficiencies:\n\n- Several high-loading points yielded similar acid responses, while the dilute region below 0.001 mol/L remained unexplored.\n- The scale comparison was made where the response was least sensitive, limiting its power to discriminate input mappings.\n- Every direct batch used essentially the same waiting protocol, leaving the instantaneous-response assumption untested.\n- There was only one exact repeat pair, limiting empirical interval calibration.\n\nThose were tradeoffs within identifiability and coverage, not necessarily tradeoffs for score. Some plateau coverage was valuable to establish saturation across a wide loading range, but after that pattern emerged, another sensitive-region scale comparison or a no-wait comparison would likely have had greater mechanistic value than additional plateau precision.\n\nA further process limitation was that the experiment note provided broad aims and noise-aware interpretation, but not fully specified statistical acceptance rules. This was sufficient to label the work exploratory, not sufficient to claim a preregistered decisive mechanism test.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses in the blind predictions\n\n### Evidence that was not fully exploited\n\nThe campaign obtained 12 intermediate measurements as well as 12 final assays. K1 used the staged intermediate readings prominently, but did not perform a full joint analysis of the direct-batch intermediate/final pairs. Those pairs could have helped characterize cross-instrument differences and the consistency of the acid-channel relationship. Opposite directions caution against interpreting such differences as a simple universal time trend. A systematic paired analysis would have made that limitation clearer.\n\nThe final assay also exposed synthetic packet summaries and spectral metadata. I did not reconstruct a separate dissolved-species balance from the full packet. Some of that material could potentially constrain nominal-versus-dissolved interpretations, but the hidden species mapping and public observation restrictions prevent assuming that generic spectral channels identify the acid species. Their usefulness would need to be established rather than presumed.\n\nThe reported residuals and equilibrium-confidence values were recorded but deliberately not used as direct chemical truth. That restraint was appropriate. A low diagnostic residual cannot replace an independent relaxation experiment.\n\n### The least reliable predictions\n\n**Q08 is the least supported dilute prediction.** Its nominal concentration is 75 times below the lowest measured endpoint. K1 stated that no blank was measured and that its candidate model should not be extended to zero loading without modification. Q08 nevertheless used that model to generate central values of alpha 0.764 and pH_normalized 0.3692. Those are conditional extrapolations, not strongly evidenced outcomes. The broad intervals acknowledge this, but even they may not cover a sufficiently different background-acidity mechanism.\n\n**Q03 and Q09 are also materially extrapolative.** They lie six- to 7.5-fold below the measured minimum. Their precipitation estimates of approximately 0.105 and 0.110 rely on a decreasing proxy relationship that was not identified in K1.\n\n**Q10 is the least supported concentrated prediction.** It extrapolates the plateau to 6.6667 mol/L in only 0.006 L. Q01\u2013Q02 interpolate concentration but test an unmeasured waiting protocol. Q05\u2013Q07 additionally test addition order with no post-addition wait, while the evidence for endpoint convergence in batches 11\u201312 included a second wait. Their identical point predictions are consistent with the chosen effective endpoint model; their relatively narrow intervals risk underrepresenting kinetic alternatives.\n\nThere is also a specific tension between K1 and Q concerning precipitation. K1 emphasized that a nearly constant proxy around 0.15 was a useful first approximation and that precipitation coupling was unidentified. Q introduced a declining dilute-limit central trend roughly associated with the undissociated fraction. Q explicitly labeled that trend uncertain and retained a constant-baseline possibility in the intervals, so it was not concealed as an observed law. Nonetheless, the central prediction reflects an additional speculative choice, not a conclusion demonstrated in K1.\n\nFinally, the stated 80% intervals were predictive judgments, not intervals calibrated against repeated held-out outcomes. One repeat pair cannot establish 80% empirical coverage. No truth feedback has yet been received, so I cannot claim either that they are calibrated or that any particular interval has failed. I can only identify structural reasons they may under-cover.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how to evaluate it\n\nThe sealed recommendation was batch 2: a direct preparation of 0.000120 mol reagent in 0.040 L water, with the campaign's waiting, intermediate measurement, termination, and final-assay sequence. Its merit was that it provided a reference endpoint in the responsive loading region and was approached from both dilution and staged reagent addition.\n\nIts limitations are substantial:\n\n- It was one direct batch, not a replicated reference standard.\n- Batches 11 and 12 were converging-route comparisons, not exact replicates of its complete protocol.\n- Its recommendation concerns evidentiary usefulness, not yield, product quality, safety optimization, or maximum score.\n- The intermediate sample withdrawal is part of the executed protocol. Removing that measurement or changing the wait creates a different recipe whose equivalence was not demonstrated.\n- Nothing in the campaign establishes transfer to another reagent identity, catalyst, solvent, temperature regime, or independently parameterized world.\n\nTo test **repeatability**, I would repeat the full batch-2 sequence in independently initialized vessels, retaining every outcome and recording the three response channels separately from diagnostics. Replication should be sufficient to estimate between-batch variation rather than relying only on declared sensor noise. The comparison must preserve whether and when the intermediate sample is taken.\n\nTo test **local robustness**, I would perturb reagent amount and water volume separately around the batch-2 setting, include matched-concentration scale changes in that sensitive region, and vary waiting time and addition order. That would determine whether the useful reference behavior survives small implementation differences and whether nominal concentration is genuinely the sufficient local input.\n\nTo test **cross-material or cross-world scope**, I would first define a new authorized experimental block and its applicability criteria. A normalized comparison might examine whether another permitted material or independent world preserves the qualitative saturation pattern and the pH\u2013alpha relation, while allowing effective parameters to change. Failure to retain the same parameters would not by itself refute the original within-world relationship; it would refute universal parameter transfer. No such transfer has been demonstrated here.\n\n\u201cSample-in highest\u201d and \u201cproven optimal\u201d are different claims. An observed maximum score among a small set of tested recipes would still be subject to measurement variation, incomplete coverage, and winner-selection bias. My selected batch was not even presented as that sample-in maximum. It was an informative anchor, and there was no proof of global or local optimality under the native scoring function.\n\nThe defensible final position remains the one in K1: the campaign established a constrained effective response pattern, not a uniquely identified microscopic mechanism. The recommendation is useful within that evidentiary role. The retrospective identifies where the design and predictions depended on assumptions; it does not amend the sealed mechanism report, recommendation, or blind predictions."

Principal interpretation\n\nThe observations support an effective monoprotic-acid relationship between pH and the reported acid-dissociation fraction, with an apparent pKa near 4.65. A quantitatively coherent acid-speciation relationship\n\nLet \u03b1 denote the measured dissociation fraction. For a monoprotic acid HA \u21cc H+ + A\u2212, an idealized activity relationship is\n\npH = pKa_eff + log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)].\n\nThis equation can be used as an empirical relationship between the two public channels without asserting that the nominal reagent concentration equals [HA] + [A\u2212]. It implies the diagnostic transformation\n\npKa_app = 14 \u00d7 pH_normalized \u2212 log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)].\n\nApplying that transformation to the paired final-assay values gives, in batch order:\n\n1: 4.6210; 2: 4.6152; 3: 4.6598; 4: 4.6188; 5: 4.6678; 6: 4.6082; 7: 4.6449; 8: 4.6521; 9: 4.6831; 10: 4.6458; 11: 4.6869; 12: 4.6938.\n\nTheir arithmetic mean is 4.6498 and their observed range is approximately 4.608\u20134.694. In a synthetic benchmark, two channels could also be generated from a shared latent variable. The evidence identifies an effective relationship more securely than it identifies its microscopic cause.\n\n### Uncertainty in the apparent pKa\n\nThe declared final-assay noise scales are 0.002 in normalized pH and 0.006 in dissociation fraction. The first corresponds to 0.028 pH units. At \u03b1 \u2248 0.08, simple independent-error propagation gives an apparent-pKa noise scale of roughly 0.045 per paired observation. The observed batch-to-batch spread is therefore not surprising.\n\nThat calculation is illustrative, not a validated confidence interval. It ignores possible channel covariance, process variation, calibration bias, and model inadequacy. Averaging 12 transformed observations cannot eliminate those systematic uncertainties. I would use approximately 4.65 as a working effective pKa, with uncertainty of several hundredths and additional structural uncertainty\u2014not report 4.6498 as a precisely established constant.\n\n## 4. Why an unrestricted ideal weak-acid loading law is inadequate\n\nIf all nominal reagent were a dissolved, unbuffered monoprotic acid, with negligible background ions and ideal activities, the same model would require\n\n[H+] \u2248 \u03b1 C_nom,\n\nKa \u2248 \u03b1\u00b2 C_nom/(1 \u2212 \u03b1).\n\nThose additional assumptions are inconsistent with the joint observations.\n\nFor batch 2, \u03b1 C_nom is approximately 0.00171 mol/L. Its pH instead corresponds, under the ideal activity-to-concentration convention, to about 0.000239 mol/L hydrogen ion. For batch 7, \u03b1 C_nom is approximately 0.0517 mol/L, whereas its pH corresponds to only about 0.000302 mol/L. The discrepancy grows dramatically with loading.\n\nThe loading series independently demonstrates compression. From batch 3 to batch 7, nominal concentration rises 400-fold, but pH falls only from 3.8734 to 3.5203, and \u03b1 falls from 0.140542 to 0.069815. From batch 4 to batch 7, concentration rises tenfold while pH falls by only about 0.033 and \u03b1 changes by about 0.009. An unrestricted weak-acid square-root law would produce approximately a half-unit pH decrease for a tenfold concentration increase in its usual low-dissociation regime.\n\nThus the retained part of the weak-acid explanation is the pH\u2013fraction relationship. The rejected part is the uncomplicated identification of nominal loading with freely dissolved acid concentration under unbuffered ideal charge balance.\n\n### A useful conditional effective-loading representation\n\nUnder the additional, explicitly provisional assumption that hydrogen activity can be represented as a concentration and that [H+] = \u03b1 C_eff, define\n\nC_eff = 10^(\u2212pH)/\u03b1.\n\nThis produces the following inferred values:\n\n| Batch | Nominal loading, mol/L | Conditional C_eff, mol/L |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 3 | 0.001852 | 0.000952 |\n| 2 | 0.01852 | 0.002585 |\n| 1 | 0.05556 | 0.003441 |\n| 4 | 0.07407 | 0.003534 |\n| 5 | 0.22222 | 0.003972 |\n| 6 | 0.44444 | 0.004115 |\n| 7 | 0.74074 | 0.004323 |\n\nThese are model-derived quantities, not chemical-composition measurements. Nevertheless, they summarize the response well: effective acid loading rises with nominal loading but approaches a scale of a few millimolar.\n\nA candidate process model is consequently:\n\n1. Calculate nominal loading from reagent addition and liquid volume, accounting for sampling where possible.\n2. Map nominal loading through a monotone, saturating function C_eff = g(C_nom).\n3. Apply an effective acid-speciation relation with Ka_eff approximately 10^(\u22124.65).\n4. Generate pH and dissociation channels with instrument noise.\n5. Treat the precipitation proxy as a separate weakly resolved response, approximately constant over the measured range unless additional evidence establishes a dependence.\n\nIf unbuffered effective charge balance is adopted within that representation, the positive solution is\n\nh = [sqrt(Ka_eff\u00b2 + 4 Ka_eff C_eff) \u2212 Ka_eff]/2,\n\u03b1 = h/C_eff,\npH = \u2212log10(h).\n\nA rational saturation function, a smooth cap, or a buffered activity mapping could instantiate g. I have not established a unique functional form or fitted a justified saturation constant. The inferred values above are a descriptive guide, not evidence that a particular hidden implementation is present.\n\n## 5. Testing the archival local relationship\n\nThe supplied archival record gave an 80% effective-pKa interval of 4.6094\u20134.7094. For 0.001 mol diluted from 0.018 to 0.054 L at 298.15 K, it predicted:\n\n\u0394pH_normalized = +0.003494987,\n\u0394\u03b1 = +0.008187416.\n\nThe campaign broadly supports the effective-pKa interval. The transformed final-assay values cluster near its center; batch 6 is only marginally below its lower bound, by far less than the approximate single-observation uncertainty.\n\nFor the dilution comparison, using the mean of the two independent concentrated preparations, batches 1 and 10, as the starting endpoint gives:\n\nConcentrated mean pH_normalized = 0.254558;\nconcentrated mean \u03b1 = 0.078518.\n\nRelative to these means, dilute batch 2 gives:\n\n\u0394pH_normalized = +0.004187,\nequivalent to \u0394pH = +0.05862;\n\u0394\u03b1 = +0.013774.\n\nThe observed changes have the archival directions and are somewhat larger in magnitude. Under independent final-assay noise alone, the uncertainty scales for these endpoint differences are approximately 0.00245 in normalized pH and 0.00735 in \u03b1. The differences between the archival and observed increments are smaller than those scales. Accordingly, the independent endpoint comparison does not warrant rejecting the archival local response.\n\nNor does it validate those increments precisely. The dilute endpoint has only one independent direct preparation, the staged endpoint is not identical, and the campaign temperature was not exactly the archival temperature. My revision is mainly interpretive: retain the prior as a useful local effective relationship, but do not extend it into a universal loading law.\n\n## 6. Concentration, volume, and preparation history\n\n### Matched-concentration scaling\n\nBatches 1, 8, and 9 all had nominal loading 0.05556 mol/L, while reagent amount and water volume increased together. Batch 10 repeated batch 1.\n\nAcross these four final assays, normalized pH ranged from 0.254098 to 0.255635, a total span of approximately 0.0215 pH units. Dissociation ranged from 0.069656 to 0.079994, and precipitation proxy from 0.150318 to 0.159846. These differences are compatible with the declared assay precision.\n\nThis supports concentration-based equivalence locally. It argues against a large direct volume or total-amount effect on these chemical outputs over 0.018\u20130.054 L at this loading. It does not rule out smaller effects, effects at other concentrations, or dependence on other unvaried variables.\n\n### Replication\n\nBatches 1 and 10 had identical nominal recipes. Their final normalized-pH values differed by 0.000504, their \u03b1 values by 0.002952, and their precipitation proxies by 0.005456. This is reassuring local reproducibility, but two preparations cannot characterize a full error distribution.\n\n### Staged dilution\n\nBatch 11 began with 0.001 mol in 0.018 L. Its pre-dilution pH-meter observation was pH_normalized = 0.255635, \u03b1 = 0.086361, and precipitation proxy = 0.123612. After adding 0.036 L water and waiting again, its final assay gave 0.256634, 0.074529, and 0.160024, respectively.\n\nThe within-batch observed \u03b1 change was negative, unlike the expected equilibrium dilution direction. This comparison uses different instruments, however, and the pH-meter \u03b1 noise scale is 0.015, larger than the final-assay scale. The observed negative change is therefore not compelling evidence of reversed acid dissociation.\n\nMore importantly, the staged-dilution final endpoint had \u03b1 0.017763 below directly prepared dilute batch 2, approximately 2.1 times the combined final-assay noise scale if errors are independent. Its normalized pH was lower by 0.002110, less than one combined normalized-pH noise scale. This is a modest preparation-history warning that should be preserved. It is not enough to establish hysteresis, incomplete equilibration, or a distinct mechanism from one staged batch.\n\n### Staged loading\n\nBatch 12 began with 0.001 mol in 0.054 L. Before the second addition, the pH meter reported normalized pH 0.261672 and \u03b1 0.089770. After another 0.011 mol reagent and another wait, the final assay gave normalized pH 0.254722 and \u03b1 0.069358. Both moved in the expected direction for increased effective acid loading.\n\nThe final endpoint closely resembled directly prepared batch 5: normalized-pH difference +0.001124, \u03b1 difference \u22120.001546, and precipitation-proxy difference \u22120.005208. This supports approximate endpoint equivalence for that loading path. It does not establish path independence for every intervention.\n\n## 7. What the precipitation proxy establishes\n\nThe 12 final-assay precipitation values averaged 0.151853 and ranged from 0.140957 to 0.160024. The declared final-assay noise scale was 0.006. There was no clear monotonic progression with loading: batches 3, 5, 6, and 7 gave approximately 0.1447, 0.1519, 0.1410, and 0.1531 despite their large concentration differences.\n\nA constant response near 0.152 is therefore an adequate first descriptive model over the measured range. A weak slope or a feature narrower than the sampling grid remains possible. No precipitation threshold, solubility product, solid stoichiometry, or solid yield is identifiable from these data.\n\nOne notable discrepancy must not be hidden: batch 3's pH-meter precipitation proxy was 0.076178, versus 0.144706 in its final assay. The difference is about 3.3 times the combined declared instrument noise scale under an independent-noise calculation. No recipe intervention occurred between those measurements, although termination and measurement sequence did. This discrepancy could reflect unusual noise, instrument-specific response, or an uncharacterized sequence effect. I retain it rather than replacing either result or treating it as a confirmed physical transition.\n\nThe pH meter's reported precipitation channel is not independent imaging or gravimetric evidence of a solid. Likewise, a positive baseline around 0.15 cannot be interpreted as a 15% precipitated mass fraction.\n\n## 8. Diagnostics are not mechanistic proof or epistemic confidence\n\nFinal-assay equilibrium_residual values were between 0 and approximately 0.00625. The pH-meter residual reached approximately 0.02225 in batch 10. These diagnostics provide no conspicuous final-assay warning, but their small values do not prove thermodynamic equilibrium, charge balance, or adequate wait duration. The residual was not supplied as a physically interpretable chemical balance equation.\n\nThe channel equilibrium_confidence is an environment diagnostic. It is not my uncertainty and not a scientific success score. For example, it was approximately 0.549 in batch 5 but 0.366 in batch 7, while their pH and dissociation outputs were fairly similar. That variation was not used to rank mechanistic truth or select an optimum recipe.\n\nThe previously committed recommendation of batch 2 remains unchanged. It was chosen as an evidentiary anchor connecting the archival dilute endpoint with concentrated controls and staged dilution, not because its diagnostic score established superior chemistry.\n\n## 9. Competing explanations and identifiability limits\n\nSeveral explanations remain compatible with the effective acid-speciation curve and compressed loading response:\n\n**Limited accessible acid pool.** Only a bounded portion of nominal reagent may contribute to the acid subsystem. Dissolution limits, partitioning, binding, or an anonymous reservoir could produce such behavior. The campaign did not measure those pools.\n\n**Background buffering or counterions.** Hydrogen activity need not equal \u03b1 times total acid concentration when additional ionic species participate in charge balance. A background buffer could preserve an effective Henderson\u2013Hasselbalch relationship while suppressing pH changes with total loading.\n\n**Nonideal activities.** The pH response may reflect activity coefficients rather than ideal concentrations. No ionic-strength series or independent activity measurement was obtained, so an intrinsic thermodynamic pKa cannot be separated from a condition-dependent apparent pKa.\n\n**Bounded synthetic response mapping.** Nominal loading may be transformed nonlinearly before the public channels are generated. This is especially plausible as an observationally equivalent explanation in a declared synthetic benchmark. It should not be disguised as a demonstrated molecular mechanism.\n\n**Precipitation-limited dissolved concentration.** Precipitation could cap an acid-active pool. However, the nearly flat public precipitation proxy gives no positive identification of this explanation over buffering, binding, or response compression. It is a possibility, not the established cause of saturation.\n\n**Finite relaxation or preparation-history dependence.** The staged-dilution difference leaves this open. Because waiting time was not systematically varied and measurement channels differed across the intervention, kinetics cannot be separated from instrument variation or ordinary preparation effects.\n\nThe present evidence cannot identify molecular species, acid stoichiometry beyond an effective monoprotic description, activity coefficients, dissolved-versus-solid mass balance, temperature dependence, equilibration time constants, or a unique saturation function. Shared generation or calibration of public channels also limits how strongly their internal consistency can be treated as independent confirmation.\n\n## 10. Supported range, extrapolation, and testable consequences\n\nThe empirical scope is water-only preparation with nominal reagent additions from 0.0001 to 0.040 mol, water volumes from 0.018 to 0.054 L, and nominal loadings approximately 0.00185\u20130.74074 mol/L. This is a sparse set of points, not exhaustive coverage of that rectangle. Matched-concentration scaling was tested only around 0.05556 mol/L. History dependence was examined with only one dilution path and one additional-loading path. Conditions were near the initial room-temperature telemetry and used the stated short waits and stirring.\n\nWithin that scope, the strongest supported claims are:\n\n\u2022 pH and dissociation fraction are consistent with a common effective pKa near 4.65.\n\u2022 Dilution generally raises pH and dissociation fraction in the independently prepared endpoints.\n\u2022 Loading dependence is strongly compressed at higher nominal concentrations.\n\u2022 Equal nominal concentration approximately collapses the measured chemical responses across the tested volume scaling.\n\u2022 The final-assay precipitation proxy is approximately constant near 0.15, with no resolved onset.\n\u2022 The staged-loading endpoint agrees with its direct-preparation comparator; staged dilution leaves a modest unresolved discrepancy.\n\nExtrapolation below the lowest loading, above the highest loading, to other solvents, catalysts, temperatures, or long-time behavior is unsupported. In particular, the observed high-loading plateau should not be extended to infinite loading, and the flat precipitation proxy should not be extended to all possible conditions.\n\nIf the effective model is useful, future observations within the tested regime should continue to approximately preserve pH \u2212 log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)] near 4.65, and matched-concentration preparations should agree within measurement and preparation variation. Systematic departures from the first prediction would challenge a single effective acidity relationship; reproducible departures from the second would require an explicit volume, amount, history, or time variable. These are testable consequences, not additional experiments or observations performed for this report.\n\nThe final mechanistic position is therefore deliberately limited: a well-supported effective acid-speciation curve, a strongly nonlinear loading-to-activity relationship, an unresolved physical origin for that nonlinearity, and no identified precipitation law beyond an approximately flat public proxy over the sampled domain."
}
```

{
  "report": "# Retrospective assessment\n\nI interpret K1 as the sealed mechanistic report and Q as the sealed blind predictions. No prediction truth has been disclosed. This assessment uses only the completed campaign and distinguishes contemporaneous choices from interpretations developed afterward. It does not amend either sealed submission.\n\n## 1. Initial claims: supported, contradicted, and untested\n\nThe initial material did contain substantive scientific claims. Its archival estimate supplied an effective-pKa interval of 4.6094\u20134.7094, labelled as an 80% interval, and predicted that diluting 0.001 mol from 0.018 to 0.054 L at 298.15 K would increase normalized pH by 0.003494987 and dissociation fraction by 0.008187416. It explicitly described these as local effective relationships, not a universal aqueous-chemistry law.\n\n**Effective acidity received positive support.** In K1, the transformation pKa_app = 14 pH_normalized \u2212 log10[\u03b1/(1\u2212\u03b1)] gave a mean of 4.6498 across the 12 final assays and a range of approximately 4.608\u20134.694. Agreement across different loading conditions is stronger evidence than merely observing no contradiction at one point. Nevertheless, it supports an effective relationship between public channels, not an independently measured thermodynamic constant. Shared channel generation, activities, and calibration remain possible sources of that agreement. The archival interval's nominal 80% coverage was not itself tested by this campaign.\n\n**The archival dilution increment was not rejected, but was not precisely validated.** Relative to the mean of concentrated batches 1 and 10, dilute batch 2 increased normalized pH by approximately 0.004187 and \u03b1 by 0.013774. These are larger than the archival increments, but the differences are smaller than the noise-based endpoint uncertainty scales calculated in K1. The experiment was also near 297.2 K rather than exactly at the archival 298.15 K. The appropriate conclusion is compatibility with the local prediction, not confirmation of its precise numerical increments.\n\n**An unrestricted ideal weak-acid concentration law encountered counterevidence.** That stronger law was not actually promised by the initial material. It was a natural candidate interpretation that the campaign tested indirectly. Batches 4\u20137 showed only small chemical-response changes across a tenfold concentration increase. K1 also demonstrated that \u03b1C_nom was inconsistent with the hydrogen activity inferred from pH if one imposed ideal, unbuffered charge balance. My contemporaneous progress statement after batch 10 already recognized the compressed loading response. The explicit algebraic diagnosis and the distinction between an effective speciation curve and a nominal-concentration law were developed in K1 afterward.\n\n**Strict preparation-history equivalence received a warning, not decisive rejection.** Batch 11's staged-dilution final \u03b1 was 0.074529, versus 0.092292 in directly prepared dilute batch 2. K1 correctly retained this approximately 2.1 combined-noise-scale discrepancy. Its within-batch \u03b1 change also ran opposite to the simple dilution expectation, although that comparison crossed instruments. These are observations against an exact, noiseless endpoint-equivalence account; they must not be described as an absence of counterevidence. They were insufficient to identify hysteresis, but sufficient to qualify the simpler account.\n\nNo subsequent experiment resolved that warning because batch 11 was near the campaign end and the final batch had already been specified. In the sealed predictions I nevertheless retained equal central estimates for equal-concentration preparations, using uncertainty rather than a fitted history correction. That was a predictive choice under unresolved evidence, not proof that history was irrelevant.\n\nTemperature dependence, chemical identity, precipitation stoichiometry, and universal concentration scaling remained untested. The initial record supplied no substantive precipitation law to confirm or refute.\n\n## 2. Experiments that formed or changed the interpretation\n\n**Batches 1, 2, and 10 were driven directly by the initial material.** They implemented the archival concentrated and dilute endpoints, with batch 10 repeating the concentrated preparation. They supplied the most direct prior comparison. The choice to repeat the concentrated rather than dilute endpoint left unequal replication: the crucial direct dilute endpoint remained a single preparation.\n\n**Batch 3 supplied the strongest observed dilute contrast.** Its final \u03b1 of 0.140542 and normalized pH of 0.276672 were clearly above those at moderate and high loading. Without it, much of the campaign would have occupied a nearly flat response region. It helped establish that dilution matters and that the plateau was not simply complete insensitivity of the public channels.\n\n**Batches 4\u20137 changed the loading interpretation.** Across nominal concentrations of approximately 0.0741\u20130.7407 mol/L, \u03b1 stayed around 0.070\u20130.079 and normalized pH around 0.251\u20130.254. This was the main evidence against unrestricted ideal loading behavior. The corresponding precipitation proxies lacked a monotonic progression. The contemporaneous statement that the higher-loading response was much smaller than an uncomplicated weak-acid law would suggest was grounded in these observations.\n\n**Batches 8 and 9, compared with 1 and 10, supported local concentration collapse.** They changed amount and volume together at fixed nominal concentration. Their final normalized-pH span was only about 0.00154. These controls were important because a one-volume concentration series alone cannot distinguish an amount effect from a concentration effect.\n\n**Batches 11 and 12 tested preparation paths and produced different levels of reassurance.** Staged loading in batch 12 agreed well with directly prepared batch 5: \u03b1 differed by approximately \u22120.00155. Staged dilution in batch 11 was less consistent with direct batch 2. Those results justified retaining a path-independent model as a useful approximation while declining to establish it as a law.\n\nSeveral design choices rested on assumptions rather than established evidence:\n\n- The broad concentration range was selected to expose a response curve; its locations were not based on a known transition threshold.\n- The fixed 120-second wait was an operational standardization choice, not an experimentally established equilibration time.\n- Water-only, catalyst-free preparations reduced confounding but assumed that this restricted slice was sufficient for the principal question.\n- Using one pH measurement and one final assay per batch traded repeat precision for coverage and cross-instrument observations.\n- The two staged paths assumed that useful history information could be obtained despite changing instruments between the pre- and post-intervention measurements.\n\nThere was also a procedural limitation. The initial experiment note specified the broad design, but the exact remaining recipes were fixed only after batch 1. Thus the complete numerical matrix was not fixed before the first result. It was fixed before executing batches 2\u201312, and I did not subsequently alter those recipes in response to their outcomes. I should not retrospectively portray the whole numerical design as having been established before any observations.\n\nFinally, the specific pKa transformation, conditional C_eff calculations, and blind-prediction interpolation formula were post-campaign analysis. They were not detailed mechanistic commitments made before the experiments.\n\n## 3. Most important competing explanations\n\nThe central unresolved question is why nominal loading is strongly compressed before appearing in the chemical outputs.\n\n**A bounded acid-accessible pool** could arise from dissolution, binding, partitioning, or another reservoir. **Background buffering or counterions** could instead decouple hydrogen activity from \u03b1C_nom. **Nonideal activity effects** could produce an apparent rather than intrinsic acidity constant. **A synthetic nonlinear mapping** could generate the same public relationships without any uniquely identifiable physical reservoir. These explanations remain observationally compatible with the measured channels.\n\nA precipitation-limited dissolved pool is one member of that set, but the data do not favor it specifically. K1's statement that there was \u201cno identified precipitation law beyond an approximately flat public proxy\u201d is important: a flat proxy cannot establish either absent precipitation or precipitation-controlled saturation.\n\nThe experiments distinguish several narrower propositions:\n\n- They argue against an unrestricted ideal, unbuffered acid model with dissolved total concentration equal to n/V.\n- They argue against a large independent volume or amount effect at the one matched concentration tested.\n- They show that the response is not flat everywhere, because batch 3 differs substantially from the plateau region.\n- They support approximate path equivalence for the tested loading path, while leaving a dilution-path discrepancy.\n\nThey cannot distinguish physical buffering from a bounded synthetic response mapping, identify dissolved versus solid material, or separate finite relaxation from preparation effects. The near-constant apparent pKa also cannot distinguish a genuine monoprotic acid subsystem from jointly generated channels constrained to resemble one.\n\nAnother unresolved competition is measurement variation versus state change. Batch 3's precipitation proxy changed from 0.076178 on the pH meter to 0.144706 on the final assay without a recipe intervention. Different instruments, termination, and sequence are confounded. Assigning the discrepancy solely to noise or solely to chemistry would exceed the evidence.\n\n## 4. One additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nIf one additional complete experiment and its resources were authorized, I would choose a direct, low-loading aqueous preparation: 0.075 L water and 0.000010 mol reagent. The sequence would be add solvent, add reagent, measure with the pH meter without an intentional premeasurement wait, wait 120 seconds at 300 rpm, terminate, and measure by final assay. I am not executing this proposal.\n\nThe nominal concentration would be approximately 0.0001333 mol/L, about fourteen times below batch 3. This deliberately targets the major low-loading extrapolation gap rather than adding another plateau point. It resembles Q03 in amount and volume, but the measurement and wait sequence differs and would not be an exact realization of that blind query.\n\nI would inspect all available public pH-meter outputs and the final acid-dissociation, normalized-pH, precipitation, and equilibrium diagnostics. The pH raw packet would be valuable for checking replicates and calibration metadata, rather than relying only on the processed number.\n\nPossible outcomes would have different implications:\n\n- A coherent increase in \u03b1 toward roughly 0.3, with normalized pH around 0.31 and apparent pKa still near 4.65, would support the qualitative low-loading continuation used in Q. One result would not validate its full functional form.\n- A much larger dissociation increase while preserving the pKa relationship would indicate that the effective-loading mapping releases its high-loading compression differently from the chosen predictor.\n- Little change from batch 3 would favor a low-loading floor, background acidity, or some other response bound over that predictor.\n- A large departure from the common pKa transformation would challenge the single effective-speciation relationship at trace loading, although instrument disagreement would have to be checked first.\n- A reproducible-looking shift in the precipitation proxy within that batch would challenge the constant-baseline approximation, but would not by itself identify a solid phase or threshold.\n- A marked pre-wait versus final difference would raise concern about time or measurement sequence. Because the instruments differ, it would not uniquely establish kinetics.\n\nThis choice maximizes leverage on an unmeasured regime but is not the cleanest timing experiment. A dedicated same-instrument time series would better isolate relaxation, and an independently repeated staged-dilution comparison would better address batch 11. With only one additional complete experiment, those are genuine opportunity costs.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe campaign was designed around characterization rather than score maximization. I used matched-concentration controls, a replicate, archival endpoints, and staged interventions, rather than selecting subsequent recipes to increase the native scalar score. The highest-loading preparations also explored a region where diagnostic confidence declined rather than improving the score.\n\nFor example, final equilibrium_confidence was approximately 0.549 in batch 5 and 0.366 in batch 7. That did not cause me to abandon batch 7 or replace it with another batch 5. The decline was a diagnostic observation, not evidence that batch 7 was scientifically unsuccessful. Likewise, the sealed recommendation chose batch 2 for its role as an archival dilute endpoint, not because it maximized the score.\n\nThere were costs to identifiability nonetheless. The broad coverage strategy left few replicates, and spending several batches in the high-loading plateau reduced opportunities to investigate the dilute response, timing, and the staged-dilution discrepancy. This was not sacrifice for score; it was a coverage-versus-identification tradeoff. In retrospect, the plateau became well demonstrated while its mechanism remained poorly resolved.\n\nThe design also did not explore solvents or catalysts. That restraint improved interpretability of the water-only slice, at the expense of discovering whether categorical material changes could separate mechanisms or affect the operational score. It should be described as controlled scope, not comprehensive mechanistic coverage.\n\nI cannot quantify a score penalty relative to an unperformed optimization campaign. It is enough to say that the actual decision rule prioritized contrasts and evidence anchors, and that neither the highest observed score nor the maximum of the scoring function was the research objective.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses of the blind predictions\n\n### Evidence not fully exploited\n\nThe campaign purchased substantial public instrument evidence, but I mostly analyzed processed estimates. I inspected one pH-meter artifact, from batch 2, rather than systematically examining all raw packets. That packet reported pH 3.628 and replicate pH values of 3.624 and 3.598, along with calibration and uncertainty metadata. Across all packets, analogous information might have helped assess replicate variability, channel processing, and calibration conventions. I cannot claim those uninspected contents would resolve the mechanism.\n\nThe final-assay raw artifacts were also not systematically analyzed. This leaves a gap between acquiring evidence and using it. The existence of declared channels or mass-balance fields is not equivalent to having measured and interpreted a physically meaningful acid balance.\n\nOther underused observations include:\n\n- The batch 3 precipitation cross-instrument discrepancy, retained in K1 but not resolved by a quantitative instrument-comparison model.\n- Batch 9's normalized pH of 0.262659 on the pH meter versus 0.254098 on final assay, a notable channel discrepancy that received less attention than the final-assay comparisons.\n- Variation in diagnostic channels across similar chemical states, which was not modeled. Avoiding their misuse as epistemic confidence was correct, but does not make them scientifically uninformative.\n- Sampling and actual-volume telemetry, which were acknowledged but not incorporated into an exact material-balance calculation because removed composition was not measured.\n\n### The least reliable blind predictions\n\nQ08 is the largest low-loading extrapolation: its concentration is about 139 times below the lowest campaign point. Q03 and Q09 also leave the observed low-loading regime, and Q10 extends concentration to nine times the campaign maximum. These are the least reliable central estimates even though their intervals were widened.\n\nThe predictor used \u03b1 = 0.068 + 0.73a0(C), where a0 is an ideal weak-acid fraction. This was a convenient bounded interpolation/extrapolation, not a fitted, independently identified mechanism. K1 had explicitly declined to identify a unique effective-loading function. Choosing a numerical continuation for Q was necessary to issue point predictions, but its parameters and asymptotes carried considerably more assumption than the in-domain evidence.\n\nIn particular, that formula approaches \u03b1 = 0.798 as concentration tends to zero. No campaign experiment established such a limiting fraction. The asymptote is a consequence of the chosen predictor, not a discovery. Likewise, preserving pKa \u2248 4.65 through trace-loading regimes assumes that background chemistry does not break the observed channel relationship.\n\n### Intervals that may be too narrow\n\nAll blind queries omit the explicit campaign wait and intermediate measurement. I included a protocol-shift allowance, but had no timing experiment with which to calibrate it. Consequently, even the relatively narrow intervals for Q01, Q02, Q11, and Q12 could be too narrow if immediate termination changes the final state.\n\nQ04 is close to batch 3 in concentration but uses only 0.006 L and 0.000010 mol. The concentration-equivalence evidence came from much larger amounts and volumes at one higher concentration. Its intervals may underrepresent that extrapolation.\n\nQ05\u2013Q07 retain equal central endpoints, despite the batch 11 warning. Their intervals include some history uncertainty, but a reproducible path effect larger than that allowance would expose undercoverage. Increasing only Q07's width modestly was a judgment call, not an empirically calibrated result.\n\nFor Q03, Q08, and Q09, precipitation intervals retain a substantial positive baseline even though the trace regime was untested. A proxy approaching zero could fall below some lower bounds. For Q10, the upper precipitation bound of 0.28 could be too low if a new transition occurs. No data-supported threshold law justifies excluding such behavior strongly.\n\nThere is also a formal tension with K1's scope language. K1 called these extreme regimes unsupported for extrapolation, whereas Q necessarily assigned them point values and finite 80% intervals. The submissions are not logically inconsistent if those numbers are understood as provisional beliefs rather than validated model outputs. Nevertheless, the intervals were judgmental and uncalibrated; they should not be presented as demonstrated 80% coverage. Without prediction truth, I cannot say which intervals actually missed or which central estimates were accurate.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how to assess it\n\nThe sealed recommendation selected completed batch 2: 0.001 mol reagent in 0.054 L water, with the campaign's wait, pH measurement, termination, and final assay. It was explicitly an evidentiary anchor for the archival dilution claim, not a production recommendation or a claimed optimum.\n\nIts main limitation is that it was independently prepared only once. Batch 11 approached a similar endpoint through staged dilution, but that is neither an exact replicate nor fully reassuring evidence of route independence. Batch 2 also depends on the campaign's particular instrument sequence, sampling, and timing. The blind immediate-termination recipes should not be treated as exact reproductions of it.\n\nA future repeatability assessment would require independently repeated exact preparations with a fixed measurement sequence and all outcomes retained. Replicating the concentrated comparators as well would estimate the dilution contrast more reliably than repeating batch 2 alone. Instrument repeatability and between-preparation repeatability should be separated where the legal measurement budget permits.\n\nLocal robustness would require predefined small perturbations of amount, volume, waiting time, and order of addition, with tolerances specified before results are seen. An exact-protocol arm and an immediate-termination arm would specifically address the protocol shift in Q. Such tests would determine whether the recommended anchor is stable to practical variations rather than merely repeatable under one sequence.\n\nCross-material generalization would require new experiments under each legally selectable material condition. Anonymous catalysts and categorical benchmark solvent effects do not justify chemical transfer by name alone. Cross-world generalization would require independent public campaigns in other authorized world instances, with the same evaluation rules and explicit separation of within-world fitting from between-world testing. Neither transfer claim follows from this single water-only campaign.\n\nFinally, \u201csample-internal highest\u201d and \u201cproven optimal\u201d are different claims, and neither describes the rationale for batch 2. The observed native score of batch 12 was approximately 0.3077; that does not make it globally optimal, and batch 2 was not selected because it beat every alternative. Establishing an optimum would require a stated objective, an adequately explored legal domain, uncertainty-aware comparisons, and independent confirmation. Establishing an evidentiary anchor instead requires reproducibility and relevance to the scientific contrast. The recommendation served the latter purpose, with the limitations above."
}
