{
  "report": "# Mechanism report: bounded aqueous acid dissociation and precipitation response\n\n## 1. Principal interpretation\n\nThe observations support a weak-acid-like relationship between pH and the reported acid-dissociation fraction, combined with a strong limitation on how nominal reagent concentration influences the measured chemical state. They do not support treating all added reagent as an unrestricted, freely dissolved monoprotic acid.\n\nMy preferred working explanation has three parts:\n\n1. Reagent loading and liquid volume determine a nominal concentration, but the chemically effective concentration is a bounded or saturating function of that nominal concentration.\n2. The measured dissociation fraction and pH are approximately consistent with a common effective pKa near 5.0.\n3. The precipitation proxy increases between the lowest-loading condition and the higher-loading conditions, then remains near a plateau. It may share the same bounded chemical driver, but the data do not identify a precipitation reaction, solubility product, or precipitated mass.\n\nThe first two points are supported at different levels. The high-loading response plateau is directly observed. The effective pKa is a quantitative relationship inferred from two measured channels. A capped dissolved-acid pool is a plausible mechanistic interpretation, not an observed species inventory. A mathematical cap, smooth saturation, buffering, and limitations in the public response mapping remain competing explanations.\n\nNo task-specific archival relationship or material-property dossier was supplied. The material reference identified an anonymous limiting reagent and selectable solvents, but did not provide an acid identity, pKa, solubility, or nominal mechanism to validate.\n\n## 2. Experimental basis and measurement meaning\n\nThe completed campaign comprised 12 batches, 12 intermediate pH-meter measurements, and 12 final assays. Every batch was explicitly terminated before its final assay. There were 87 laboratory operations and no discarded batches. No additional experiments were performed for this report.\n\nAll batches used water, no added catalyst, and no deliberate heating. Each intermediate measurement followed a 600-second wait at 400 rpm. A further 600-second wait preceded termination. For batches 10\u201312, the planned loading or dilution intervention occurred between the intermediate measurement and the second wait. Thus the intermediate and final measurements are not technical replicates: they differ in time, instrument, sampling history, and, in three batches, composition.\n\nThe public contract defines:\n\n- pH_normalized = pH / 14;\n- acid_dissociation_fraction as a bounded fraction, denoted \u03b1 below;\n- precipitation_signal as a bounded normalized proxy, denoted P below;\n- equilibrium_residual and equilibrium_confidence as environment diagnostics.\n\nIn particular, equilibrium_confidence is neither my confidence in this explanation nor a research-performance score. I do not use it to establish chemical equilibrium or to choose a mechanism.\n\nThe pH-meter operation consumed 0.000030 L and the final assay consumed 0.000300 L. Final pH values below are calculated as 14 times the final-assay pH_normalized estimate. They are not separately observed final electrode readings. Intermediate pH values are the public pH-meter packet values.\n\n### Final-assay results\n\nNominal concentrations in this table use the recipe quantities before sampling corrections. They describe experimental coverage, not verified dissolved concentrations.\n\n| Batch | Reagent and water history | Nominal endpoint concentration, mol/L | Final pH | Final \u03b1 | Final P |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 1 | 0.000040 mol in 0.040 L | 0.001 | 4.2005 | 0.13096 | 0.11653 |\n| 2 | 0.000400 mol in 0.040 L | 0.010 | 3.9400 | 0.07822 | 0.15305 |\n| 3 | 0.002000 mol in 0.040 L | 0.050 | 3.9351 | 0.07685 | 0.15686 |\n| 4 | 0.008000 mol in 0.040 L | 0.200 | 3.9258 | 0.07140 | 0.14935 |\n| 5 | 0.020000 mol in 0.040 L | 0.500 | 3.8724 | 0.05842 | 0.16321 |\n| 6 | 0.040000 mol in 0.040 L | 1.000 | 3.8951 | 0.07033 | 0.15505 |\n| 7 | 0.004000 mol in 0.020 L | 0.200 | 3.8736 | 0.06218 | 0.15572 |\n| 8 | 0.016000 mol in 0.080 L | 0.200 | 3.8527 | 0.07216 | 0.15776 |\n| 9 | Independent repeat of batch 4 | 0.200 | 3.9297 | 0.06849 | 0.15986 |\n| 10 | 0.008000 mol in 0.020 L, then add 0.020 L water | approximately 0.200 | 3.8770 | 0.07615 | 0.15838 |\n| 11 | 0.002000 mol in 0.040 L, then add 0.006000 mol reagent | approximately 0.200 | 3.9259 | 0.07187 | 0.16279 |\n| 12 | 0.040000 mol in 0.020 L, then add 0.060 L water | approximately 0.500 | 3.8586 | 0.08213 | 0.15277 |\n\nFor batches 10 and 12, the intermediate sample left actual final pre-assay volumes of approximately 0.039970 and 0.079970 L. Batch 11 had approximately 0.039970 L after its intermediate sample. Exact retained reagent amounts cannot be reconstructed simply by subtracting solvent volume without assuming how representative the withdrawn sample was. Under homogeneous withdrawal, the concentration corrections are small, around a tenth of a percent or less for these interventions; they cannot account for the large departure from unrestricted concentration scaling.\n\n### Intermediate measurements\n\n| Batch | Nominal concentration at intermediate measurement, mol/L | Intermediate pH | Intermediate \u03b1 | Intermediate P |\n|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 1 | 0.001 | 4.087 | 0.10645 | 0.10771 |\n| 2 | 0.010 | 4.013 | 0.10906 | 0.12676 |\n| 3 | 0.050 | 3.918 | 0.05514 | 0.17523 |\n| 4 | 0.200 | 3.838 | 0.04364 | 0.14997 |\n| 5 | 0.500 | 3.989 | 0.07330 | 0.15354 |\n| 6 | 1.000 | 4.034 | 0.08460 | 0.15670 |\n| 7 | 0.200 | 3.909 | 0.07306 | 0.17065 |\n| 8 | 0.200 | 3.901 | 0.06187 | 0.15460 |\n| 9 | 0.200 | 3.880 | 0.05582 | 0.12931 |\n| 10 | 0.400 | 3.784 | 0.11153 | 0.16144 |\n| 11 | 0.050 | 3.950 | 0.08510 | 0.15427 |\n| 12 | 2.000 | 3.950 | 0.03992 | 0.13501 |\n\nThese readings are retained even where they disagree noticeably with the final assay. In particular, batch 10's intermediate \u03b1 of 0.11153 and pH of 3.784 do not fit the final-assay relationship especially closely. They should not be silently replaced with values predicted by the model.\n\n## 3. What the loading series establishes\n\nBatches 1\u20136 are the main loading series because water volume and the operation schedule were held fixed while reagent amount changed.\n\nBetween batches 1 and 2, nominal concentration increased tenfold, from 0.001 to 0.010 mol/L. Final pH decreased by 0.2605, \u03b1 decreased by 0.05273, and P increased by 0.03653. These changes exceed the stated final-assay instrumental noise scales and indicate a genuine low-loading versus higher-loading contrast, although the lowest-loading condition has no independent repeat.\n\nAbove that contrast, the response becomes much flatter. Between batches 2 and 6, nominal concentration increased another hundredfold, but final pH changed only from 3.9400 to 3.8951, \u03b1 from 0.07822 to 0.07033, and P from 0.15305 to 0.15505. The intermediate points do not form a strong monotonic series: batch 5 has lower \u03b1 than batch 6, for example.\n\nFor a simple unbuffered weak acid with fixed Ka and total dissolved concentration C, the usual weak-dissociation approximation gives\n\nh \u2248 sqrt(Ka C),\n\npH \u2248 0.5(pKa \u2212 log10 C),\n\n\u03b1 \u2248 sqrt(Ka/C),\n\nwhere h is hydrogen-ion concentration under an ideal concentration approximation. A hundredfold increase in C would then lower pH by approximately one unit and reduce \u03b1 roughly tenfold. That behavior is absent over the higher-loading part of this campaign.\n\nThe important rejection is therefore specific: nominal n/V cannot simply be substituted for unrestricted dissolved weak-acid concentration with a fixed Ka. This does not reject acid dissociation itself.\n\nPooling final endpoints from batches 2\u201312 gives descriptive means of pH = 3.8987, \u03b1 = 0.07166, and P = 0.15680. Their observed ranges are pH 3.8527\u20133.9400, \u03b1 0.05842\u20130.08213, and P 0.14935\u20130.16321. These are pooled high-loading observations, not 11 exact-recipe replicates, and the ranges are not formal confidence intervals. They summarize the approximately plateaued region while leaving room for weak remaining concentration dependence or batch effects.\n\n## 4. An identifiable pH\u2013dissociation relationship\n\nFor an ideal monoprotic acid pair HA/A\u2212,\n\nKa = h \u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1),\n\nor equivalently,\n\npH = pKa + log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)].\n\nThis relationship between h and \u03b1 does not by itself require that nominal reagent loading equal the dissolved acid concentration. It therefore remains useful even after the unrestricted loading model fails.\n\nApplying it to the paired final-assay pH and \u03b1 values yields apparent pKa values, for batches 1\u201312 respectively:\n\n5.022, 5.011, 5.015, 5.040, 5.080, 5.016, 5.052, 4.962, 5.063, 4.961, 5.037, and 4.907.\n\nTheir unweighted mean is 5.014, corresponding to an effective Ka of approximately 9.7 \u00d7 10^\u22126 under the ideal concentration convention. The full observed apparent-pKa range is approximately 4.91\u20135.08.\n\nThe agreement between the distinct low-loading endpoint and the high-loading region is particularly useful. Batch 1 gives an apparent pKa of 5.022; batch 4 gives 5.040; batch 9 gives 5.063. Thus the pH\u2013\u03b1 coupling is more stable than an interpretation based on \u03b1 versus nominal loading alone would suggest.\n\nI regard pKa \u2248 5.0 as a supported effective relationship for these public final-assay channels, not as the independently established thermodynamic pKa of a named molecule. Important qualifications are:\n\n- \u03b1's mapping to an actual species inventory is not public.\n- pH is fundamentally an activity measurement; replacing activity by concentration is an assumption.\n- Both channels come from the same bounded synthetic environment, so their agreement is not equivalent to validation by independent analytical chemistry.\n- The relationship was evaluated after observing the data, rather than tested with a separately reserved validation set.\n- The intermediate measurements are noisier and sometimes depart substantially from the final-assay fit.\n\n## 5. A bounded effective-acid model\n\nA useful interpretation introduces an effective acid concentration C_eff distinct from the nominal concentration C_nom = n_added/V.\n\nIf the effective pool is an unbuffered monoprotic acid and its dissociation supplies essentially all hydrogen ions, then\n\nh \u2248 \u03b1 C_eff,\n\nC_eff \u2248 h/\u03b1.\n\nThis additional charge-balance assumption is stronger than the pH\u2013\u03b1 relationship in the preceding section. It can fail in the presence of buffering, background ions, or other proton sources.\n\nConditionally applying it gives:\n\n- Batch 1: h = 10^\u22124.2005 and \u03b1 = 0.13096 imply C_eff \u2248 0.000481 mol/L.\n- The pooled high-loading mean pH and \u03b1 imply a representative C_eff \u2248 0.001762 mol/L.\n\nThe second number is a calculation from pooled response means, not an independently measured concentration or the mean of individually reconstructed concentrations. Its purpose is to indicate scale.\n\nThese calculations motivate the following compact candidate model:\n\nC_nom = retained nominal reagent amount / active liquid volume\n\nC_eff = g(C_nom)\n\nh = [sqrt(Ka\u00b2 + 4 Ka C_eff) \u2212 Ka]/2\n\n\u03b1 = h/C_eff\n\npH_normalized = \u2212log10(h)/14\n\nP = F(C_eff, \u03b1, other unobserved state)\n\nA simple possible choice is g(C_nom) = min(\u03b2 C_nom, C_cap). Rough values \u03b2 \u2248 0.48 and C_cap \u2248 0.0018 mol/L reproduce the inferred low-loading and high-loading scales. The implied crossover would be around 0.004 mol/L.\n\nThat crossover is an extrapolation inside an unmeasured interval, not an observed threshold. There is only one final-assay condition below 0.010 mol/L. Consequently, \u03b2, the cap, and the sharpness or location of a transition are not independently well determined. A smooth saturating function could explain the same observations. The decimal precision of the illustrative parameter values must not be mistaken for identification precision.\n\nThe model captures an important qualitative coupling: dilution can increase pH and increase fractional dissociation at the same time. A smaller fraction is not required for a smaller absolute hydrogen-ion concentration. However, dilution within the saturated nominal-concentration region may produce almost no observable response because C_eff remains bounded near its plateau.\n\n## 6. What the precipitation proxy does and does not establish\n\nThe strongest supported statement about P is empirical. Batch 1 has P = 0.11653, while batches 2\u201312 cluster near 0.157. Loading beyond the low-loading transition produces little further increase in the final proxy.\n\nAcross the main low-to-high contrast, lower pH and lower \u03b1 accompany higher P. This is consistent with a common dependence on effective loading. It does not establish that changing pH causes precipitation, that precipitation causes the pH plateau, or that the undissociated fraction directly determines P.\n\nA precipitation-limited dissolved pool is an attractive explanation: additional nominal material could enter a non-dissolved reservoir while dissolved concentration remains approximately fixed. But a genuine solubility argument would require identification of the dissolved species, solid phase, and relevant activity product. None of those was measured here. Moreover, a plateau in a normalized public proxy need not mean that the amount of solid has plateaued.\n\nOther compatible explanations include a bounded precipitation-response transform, aggregation or sequestration, or a proxy that is simply a smooth function of the same latent state controlling acid dissociation. With effectively two well-separated response levels, fitting a detailed law such as P = a + bC_eff, a sigmoid, or a function of 1 \u2212 \u03b1 would add assumptions rather than identify a mechanism.\n\nFor this report I retain P \u2248 0.157 as a descriptive high-loading response and P \u2248 0.117 at the single tested low-loading endpoint. I do not infer a solubility product, precipitation onset, solid yield, or zero-loading baseline.\n\n## 7. Scale, repeatability, and path comparisons\n\n### Matched nominal concentration at different scales\n\nBatches 7, 4, and 8 all started at nominal C = 0.200 mol/L, but used 0.020, 0.040, and 0.080 L of water, respectively. Their final (pH, \u03b1, P) values were:\n\n- Batch 7: (3.8736, 0.06218, 0.15572).\n- Batch 4: (3.9258, 0.07140, 0.14935).\n- Batch 8: (3.8527, 0.07216, 0.15776).\n\nThere is no clear monotonic absolute-volume effect across these three conditions. Their differences are modest relative to the instrumental scales and other observed variability.\n\nThis supports approximate scale robustness at this tested concentration, but does not prove concentration is the only control variable. All three conditions lie in the plateau region. A strongly bounded response could conceal dependence on total amount, volume, or both. A matched-concentration comparison near the unsaturated response region would have been more discriminating, but was not part of the completed campaign.\n\n### Independent recipe repeat\n\nBatch 9 repeats batch 4. Final differences, batch 9 minus batch 4, are:\n\n- pH: +0.00387;\n- \u03b1: \u22120.00291;\n- P: +0.01051.\n\nThe pH and \u03b1 agreement is close. The P difference is appreciable but compatible with the scale of two noisy final measurements. One repeat pair is insufficient to estimate a stable between-batch variance or exclude occasional larger differences.\n\n### Dilution and staged addition\n\nBatch 10 reached approximately the batch-4 concentration by doubling the water volume after an intermediate measurement. Its final values, pH 3.8770, \u03b1 0.07615, and P 0.15838, fall within the broader high-loading response range.\n\nBatch 11 reached approximately the batch-4 loading by adding reagent after its intermediate measurement. Its final values, pH 3.9259, \u03b1 0.07187, and P 0.16279, likewise resemble the direct-preparation endpoints.\n\nBatch 12 underwent a larger dilution, from nominal 2.0 to approximately 0.5 mol/L. Its final pH 3.8586 and P 0.15277 are near batch 5's pH 3.8724 and P 0.16321. Its \u03b1 of 0.08213 is higher than batch 5's 0.05842 by 0.02371. That difference is larger than expected from the quoted final \u03b1 instrumental noise alone and should not be dismissed. It could reflect path dependence, batch variation, unmodelled process uncertainty, or an unusually large measurement deviation. Without replicated direct-versus-diluted comparisons, it does not establish which explanation is correct.\n\nTaken together, these tests show no compelling large path effect on pH or P after the tested waits, but they do not prove full path independence. The \u03b1 discrepancy between batches 5 and 12 is the strongest caution. Also, neither dilution test crossed into the distinctly low-loading region, so they did not demonstrate reversal of the low-to-high response transition.\n\n## 8. Equilibration, noise, and diagnostic limitations\n\nThe quoted final-assay noise scales are 0.002 for pH_normalized, 0.006 for \u03b1, and 0.006 for P. The pH-normalized scale corresponds to 0.028 pH units. The intermediate pH-meter channel scales are larger: 0.004 in pH_normalized, equivalent to 0.056 pH units, 0.015 in \u03b1, and 0.020 in P. The instrument contract also includes a process-proxy contribution to uncertainty, so these values are not complete uncertainty bounds for every comparison.\n\nNear \u03b1 \u2248 0.07, propagating only the final instrumental scales through pKa = pH \u2212 log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)] gives a rough single-observation pKa uncertainty scale of about 0.05, assuming independent errors. Thus much of the observed apparent-pKa scatter is plausible without requiring a changing acid constant. That calculation does not include unknown correlation, process variation, or model error.\n\nFinal equilibrium_residual values were small, from zero to approximately 0.00740. This is compatible with a low-residual diagnostic state, but it is not independent proof of chemical equilibrium. Diagnostic outputs may be bounded and model-dependent. Similarly, equilibrium_confidence varied substantially: for example, approximately 0.534 in batch 4 and 0.362 in batch 6 despite relatively similar final chemical readings. I treat that variation as diagnostic behavior, not as evidence that one chemical observation deserves an automatically larger scientific weight.\n\nThe available schedule cannot identify a relaxation time. Intermediate-to-final differences confound time with instrument and sampling effects. Establishing equilibration kinetics would require repeated comparable measurements at controlled times, which were not obtained. Statements about equilibrium here therefore refer to an effective endpoint relationship consistent with the observed waits, not a demonstrated kinetic limit.\n\n## 9. Competing explanations and non-identifiability\n\nSeveral explanations remain compatible with the completed evidence:\n\n1. **Solubility-limited acid pool.** Dissolved acid reaches a ceiling while additional material resides in a separate reservoir. This explains a stable pH\u2013\u03b1 relation and weak higher-loading dependence, but no dissolved-pool or solid-phase measurement verifies it.\n2. **Bounded benchmark mapping.** The environment may map nominal loading through a clipped or saturating function before generating chemical response channels. This is observationally difficult to distinguish from a physical concentration cap using the present measurements.\n3. **Buffered or background-controlled acidity.** An unobserved proton reservoir could maintain pH while the reported \u03b1 responds through an effective acid-base relation. In that case C_eff = h/\u03b1 would not be a valid material-balance reconstruction.\n4. **Limited conversion, sequestration, or aggregation.** Only a bounded amount of the anonymous reagent may contribute to the measured acid pool. The rest need not precipitate in the ordinary chemical sense.\n5. **Measurement-response saturation or shared channel construction.** The chemical channels could be bounded transforms of latent variables rather than direct measurements of bulk species. The public values are far from the universal 0 and 1 bounds, so simple clipping at those bounds is not the explanation; a more specific internal response plateau remains possible.\n6. **Weak residual loading or history effects.** The high-loading region may not be perfectly flat. Modest trends and the batch-5/batch-12 \u03b1 discrepancy could coexist with a dominant saturation effect.\n\nThe present experiment set identifies an effective response structure more strongly than it identifies physical species or elementary reactions. It cannot determine the reagent's molecular identity, proton stoichiometry, activity coefficients, solid composition, true solubility constant, or whether the precipitation proxy measures actual solids.\n\n## 10. Supported range and conditional implications\n\nThe directly tested domain was aqueous, catalyst-free operation with initial reagent amounts from 0.000040 to 0.040000 mol, water volumes from 0.020 to 0.080 L, and the stated 600-second waiting schedule. Nominal final concentrations ranged from 0.001 to 1.0 mol/L. The intermediate observations additionally sampled nominal 0.4 and 2.0 mol/L. These were not a full factorial grid: the very low concentration was tested only at one volume, and the scale controls were confined to the high-loading region.\n\nNo temperature-response law, solvent effect, catalyst effect, quench effect, or pressure dependence was tested. The initial public temperature was approximately 297.2 K and no heating was requested, but that does not constitute a temperature study. The bounded synthetic setting should not be treated as a validated model of arbitrary real aqueous samples.\n\nThe working model has conditional implications, not additional observations:\n\n- Under comparable endpoint conditions in the tested high-loading region, pH, \u03b1, and P should remain near their observed plateau rather than follow unrestricted n/V scaling.\n- If dilution reaches the unsaturated effective-acid region, the simple weak-acid interpretation predicts rising pH and rising fractional dissociation. Whether P decreases along a reversible path remains unverified.\n- A plot of pH against log10[\u03b1/(1 \u2212 \u03b1)] should remain approximately consistent with an intercept near 5.0 if the effective acid-base coupling continues to apply.\n- Divergence between true dissolved inventory and nominal loading would distinguish a bounded acid pool from an unrestricted acid model, but such inventory information is absent here.\n\nThese implications should not be extended toward zero loading or outside the tested operational regime without accounting for background acidity, water autoionization, possible changes in mapping, and other unmeasured effects.\n\n## 11. Overall conclusion\n\nThe most defensible mechanism-level result is a separation between acid-base coupling and loading-to-state mapping. The final public pH and dissociation channels are consistent with an effective monoprotic-acid relationship having pKa near 5.0. Nominal loading, however, has a strongly attenuated influence above the lowest tested concentration decade. A bounded effective acid pool explains that combination parsimoniously, but the physical source of the bound is not identified.\n\nThe precipitation proxy shares the low-versus-high loading contrast and then approximately plateaus. It cannot yet be interpreted as a quantified solid phase or assigned a precipitation equilibrium law. Scale and path controls support approximate endpoint robustness within the plateau, while the batch-5/batch-12 dissociation difference prevents a blanket claim of path independence.\n\nBatch 4 remains the previously selected evidentiary anchor because it connects an independent repeat, matched-concentration scale controls, and staged-preparation comparisons. That selection is unchanged and is not an assertion that its diagnostic score or equilibrium_confidence validates the mechanism. This report is an interpretation of the completed public observations, not a revision of sealed outcomes or a claim of unique mechanistic identification."
}

This combination\u2014an initial response followed by flattening\u2014motivated the bounded effective-pool explanation more strongly than either the low-loading observation or the high-loading plateau alone.\n\nThe later pH\u2013\u03b1 calculation added a separate insight: failure of nominal loading to behave as dissolved acid concentration did not imply failure of an effective acid-dissociation relationship. Thus these controls supported practical similarity of endpoints more than they established concentration as the unique causal variable.\n\nBatch 9, the exact-recipe repeat of batch 4, was genuinely useful. The final pH difference was only 0.00387 and the \u03b1 difference was \u22120.00291, although P differed by 0.01051. This justified treating batch 4 as a reproducible anchor at least once, while warning against assuming identical proxy readings.\n\nBatches 10 and 11 showed that approximately the same endpoint could be reached by dilution or staged loading. Their evidentiary value was limited by the fact that the interventions remained within the plateau region. Batch 12 supplied the important \u03b1 discrepancy described above; it weakened any strong claim of path independence.\n\n### What determined the choices\n\nThe initial brief justified varying loading and dilution and prioritizing pH and final assays. The measurement contract justified choosing the pH meter rather than UV\u2013visible spectroscopy: the task-specific observable list gave the latter no direct equilibrium-response channels.\n\nThe detailed concentration grid, the use of 600-second waits, the 400-rpm stirring setting, and the decision to omit catalysts and heating were investigator choices. They were not derived from a supplied kinetic model or validated equilibration criterion. In particular, 600 seconds was an operationally convenient common schedule, not a demonstrated equilibration time.\n\nThe full 12-batch coverage was fixed before the first chemical response measurement. Consequently, it would be misleading to present batches 7\u201312 as an adaptively optimized response to the plateau. They were planned controls that remained in place after the plateau emerged. Preserving that fixed design prevented outcome-driven replacement, but also left substantial redundancy once the high-loading response proved flat.\n\n## 3. The leading competing explanations\n\nThe main competition is not \u201cacid dissociation versus no acid dissociation.\u201d It is between different explanations for why nominal loading has so little effect over most of the campaign.\n\n### A physically bounded dissolved pool\n\nAdditional reagent could enter a solid, aggregate, or other reservoir while an effective dissolved pool remains approximately fixed. This naturally accommodates a stable pH\u2013\u03b1 relation and a plateau in the measured endpoints.\n\nHowever, the campaign never measured the dissolved inventory or identified a solid. K1's conditional reconstruction C_eff \u2248 h/\u03b1 assumes that acid dissociation supplies essentially all hydrogen ions. That assumption is not independently established.\n\n### A bounded response mapping\n\nA clipped or smoothly saturating mapping from nominal loading to public response channels could generate much the same observations without a physical solubility ceiling. K1 explicitly retained this possibility. The observations distinguish such bounded behavior from unrestricted nominal weak-acid scaling, but do not distinguish a software-level response bound from a physical dissolved-pool limit.\n\n### Background-controlled or buffered acidity\n\nAn unobserved background proton reservoir could stabilize pH and produce an apparent acid-base relationship. Under that explanation, C_eff = h/\u03b1 need not reconstruct any actual total acid concentration. The lack of sufficiently dilute conditions is especially important: the campaign did not approach a regime in which background acidity and reagent-driven acidity would predict sharply different behavior.\n\n### Limited conversion or sequestration\n\nOnly part of the reagent may contribute to the measured acid pool. This can resemble a solubility cap without requiring precipitation. The anonymous reagent identity and absence of a species balance prevent a strong distinction.\n\n### History or transient effects\n\nThe batch-5/batch-12 \u03b1 difference leaves room for history dependence. Moreover, intermediate and final readings confound time, instrument, and sampling. For batch 10, the intermediate pair\u2014pH 3.784 and \u03b1 0.11153\u2014is not particularly consistent with the final-assay effective-pKa fit. The available design cannot determine whether that departure is measurement variation, transient chemistry, or a channel-specific effect.\n\nIn summary, the campaign distinguishes an unrestricted nominal-acid model from a broad class of bounded or background-controlled models. It provides much less discrimination within that broad class. K1's \u201ceffective response structure\u201d is more defensible than a unique elementary mechanism.\n\n## 4. The single additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nThis is a hypothetical design only; I would not execute it under the closed-campaign restrictions.\n\nI would use water, no catalyst, and no heating, with the following sequence:\n\n1. Add 0.006 L water and 0.000010 mol reagent, giving nominal concentration approximately 0.001667 mol/L.\n2. Wait 600 seconds at 400 rpm and obtain one pH-meter measurement.\n3. Add 0.069 L water, giving approximately 0.074970 L after the intermediate sampling loss and nominal concentration near 0.000133 mol/L.\n4. Wait another 600 seconds at 400 rpm, terminate explicitly, and obtain a final assay.\n\nThe individual amounts and volumes are within the public action ranges observed in the campaign. This design assumes authorization of one additional batch and its measurement allocation; it is not executable within the already exhausted campaign allowance.\n\n### Why this experiment\n\nIt crosses the poorly sampled low-loading region instead of diluting within the plateau. The first condition is near the response transition inferred from batch 1; the final condition is substantially below the lowest tested concentration. It also keeps the nominal reagent inventory almost fixed while changing concentration by roughly a factor of 12.5. The small intermediate withdrawal can be explicitly accounted for under a stated sampling assumption.\n\nUnder the conditional effective-acid extrapolation used in Q, the endpoints corresponding approximately to these conditions have pH_normalized near 0.291 and 0.335 and \u03b1 near 0.104 and 0.321. Those are model implications, not observed results or revised predictions.\n\n### How possible outcomes would affect interpretation\n\n- A substantial pH increase and \u03b1 increase after dilution would support an unsaturated, concentration-sensitive acid pool below the plateau. It would weaken an explanation based only on a fixed response to total reagent amount.\n- Persistence near the high-loading pH and \u03b1 values would undermine the assumed low-concentration scaling and strengthen background control, a concentration floor, or another bounded mapping.\n- A change in pH without the expected \u03b1 relationship would weaken the approximately constant effective-pKa description in this dilute regime or point to channel-specific behavior.\n- A decrease in P would support reversibility of the proxy response across dilution. Persistently high P while pH and \u03b1 changed would favor a separate proxy baseline, history dependence, or a solid-related response that does not immediately reverse.\n\nOne batch would still not identify all these mechanisms. The intermediate/final instrument difference would remain a limitation, and a dilution endpoint alone would not establish equivalence to a freshly prepared dilute batch. I choose it because it addresses the largest information gap, not because it would provide a decisive answer to every question.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operating score\n\nThe campaign was explicitly a characterization task, not yield or process optimization. I did not use yield-like outputs to choose recipes, and I did not pursue conditions simply because equilibrium_confidence was high.\n\nThe main design expenditures were mechanistic: a broad loading series, matched-concentration scale controls, an independent repeat, and staged-path tests. Those consume batches that could otherwise have been devoted to repeatedly searching around a high native score. I accepted that opportunity cost.\n\nThere was nevertheless a scientific efficiency problem. Once the first six batches revealed the plateau, most remaining comparisons still stayed within it. That was a consequence of the fixed initial design, not score chasing. It limited the ability to identify the low-loading transition or separate concentration dependence from absolute amount dependence.\n\nThe native score is particularly unsuitable as a surrogate for explanatory success because it heavily weights equilibrium_confidence, which the contract defines as an environment diagnostic. Batch 4's diagnostic was approximately 0.534, compared with approximately 0.362 in batch 6, although their final chemical responses were fairly similar. Chasing that difference would not necessarily clarify acid dissociation or precipitation.\n\nThe recommendation of batch 4 was explicitly based on its connections to controls, not on score. This does not mean the design was maximally informative: low-loading replication and a dilution crossing the transition would likely have produced more mechanistic information than another plateau comparison. The sacrifice was not primarily identifiability for optimization; it was identifiability lost through an imperfect, non-adaptive allocation of a fixed batch budget.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses in the blind predictions\n\n### Intermediate measurements were retained but not fully exploited\n\nAll 12 intermediate pH-meter readings were reported, but the numerical model relied primarily on final assays. That preference is defensible because the stated final-channel noise scales are smaller, but it creates a risk of explaining the cleaner subset while underweighting discrepant observations.\n\nBatch 10 is an example. Its intermediate pH and \u03b1 do not sit comfortably on the final effective-pKa relation. Batch 12 also changed from intermediate \u03b1 0.03992 to final \u03b1 0.08213, but dilution, elapsed time, and instrument changes occurred together. These observations cannot simply be classified as kinetic evidence, yet they are relevant warnings against treating the endpoint model as universally instantaneous.\n\nThe public pH packets also included replicate pH values and electrode information. Those could have supported a more systematic assessment of within-packet variation and consistency of the reported pH values. I did not complete such an analysis. It would still not substitute for independent batch replication or resolve the shared-calibration issue.\n\n### The repeat and path discrepancies deserved more quantitative emphasis\n\nBatch 4 versus batch 9 gives a small empirical check on repeatability. Batch 5 versus batch 12 gives a larger discrepancy in \u03b1. K1 discussed both, but I did not fit a defensible hierarchical error model separating instrumental noise, recipe differences, and batch effects. With only one exact repeat pair, such a model would itself have been weakly identified.\n\nThe final assay exposed additional spectral summaries, but the public mechanism-to-species mapping was hidden. Those summaries could not safely be converted into named acid or precipitate inventories. Not exploiting them for species identification was a limit of identifiability, not merely an omitted calculation.\n\n### The weakest blind predictions are the dilute extrapolations\n\nQ08 is the most fragile. Its nominal concentration is 75 times below the lowest tested endpoint. Its \u03b1 center of 0.688 and pH_normalized center of 0.383 depend strongly on extending the conditional unbuffered effective-pool model into an unobserved regime. Q03 and Q09 have the same structural vulnerability, albeit less extreme.\n\nK1 explicitly stated that no zero-loading baseline was identified and that background acidity or water-related effects might matter. The Q intervals were broad, but their breadth does not prove they adequately represent those alternatives. For example, the Q08 lower bound of 0.20 for \u03b1 does not include a persistent plateau-like fraction near 0.07. If a background-controlled model is assigned substantial probability, that interval could still be too narrow despite appearing wide numerically.\n\nThe low-loading precipitation estimates are even less mechanistically identified. Q introduced a provisional smooth function to produce point estimates below the observed range. That was transparent, but it remained an additional forecasting convention, not a result established in K1. Q08's upper bound of 0.12, for example, excludes a continuation of the observed plateau near 0.157. Such a continuation was not ruled out by dilute measurements because no such measurements existed.\n\n### High-concentration and protocol extrapolations also matter\n\nQ10 extends to approximately 6.67 mol/L nominal concentration at 0.006 L. Its pH_normalized interval of 0.259\u20130.299 remains centered tightly around the plateau relative to the range of qualitatively different mechanisms that could emerge beyond the tested domain. Its bounds could therefore be too narrow if the effective cap fails or small-volume behavior differs substantially.\n\nAll queries omit the campaign's waits and intermediate sampling. The predictions acknowledged this, but the interval increases were judgmental rather than calibrated from immediate-termination controls. Even interior Q12 and apparently well-supported Q01\u2013Q02 may therefore be less secure than their concentration coverage suggests.\n\nFinally, Q05\u2013Q07 have identical centers and intervals. This is consistent with selecting an endpoint model, but the batch-5/batch-12 \u03b1 discrepancy and absence of immediate-termination path tests mean that operation-order equivalence was not demonstrated.\n\nThere is no basis, without the withheld outcomes, to claim that any particular prediction has failed. The retrospective criticism is about how much model uncertainty the forecasts represented. K1 was explicit about conditional assumptions; Q necessarily committed to numerical outputs and may have given those assumptions more predictive weight than the evidence warrants.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how to assess generality\n\nThe sealed recommendation selected batch 4: 0.008 mol reagent in 0.040 L water with the campaign's waiting, intermediate measurement, termination, and final-assay sequence. Its rationale was that it links the repeat, scale controls, and staged-path comparisons.\n\n### What that selection establishes\n\nIt identifies a useful reference recipe within the completed study. Batch 9 provides one independent repeat, and batches 7\u20138 provide limited scale comparisons. Batches 10\u201311 connect alternative preparation paths to similar endpoints.\n\nIt does not establish a unique mechanism, a globally optimal recipe, or the best recipe for resolving the remaining uncertainty. Because batch 4 lies on the plateau, it is less informative about the transition than a carefully chosen dilute condition might be.\n\n### Reproducibility\n\nA genuine reproducibility assessment would repeat the complete batch-4 protocol across independently initialized batches, retain every result, and estimate variation in all three chemical channels rather than selecting favorable runs. It should also separate repeatability of the full protocol from agreement between instruments or measurement schedules. One repeat pair is a useful start, not a variance characterization.\n\n### Local robustness\n\nA separate, predeclared study would perturb amount and volume independently around batch 4, vary waiting time, and compare the protocol with and without intermediate sampling. The distinction between constant-concentration and fixed-amount perturbations would remain essential. Because the plateau can hide sensitivity, robustness checks should include an approach toward its lower boundary rather than only very small perturbations at its center.\n\n### Cross-material and cross-world transfer\n\nNo alternative reagent identity was available in this study, no catalysts were tested, and all batches used water. The solvent labels do not justify importing real-material chemistry into the anonymous benchmark mechanism. Applying the recommendation to another solvent, formulation, or world would require new evidence under a separate authorized study. Agreement across world initializations or material settings would need to be measured; it cannot be inferred from a fit within one campaign.\n\n### Best observed is not proved optimal\n\nI did not select batch 4 by demonstrating that it had the highest sample score. Nor would the highest observed score among 12 noisy batches prove optimality: it would only identify the largest sampled diagnostic result under those observations. The recommendation was an evidentiary anchor, and the research objective was characterization. It should remain described that way.\n\nThe strongest general lesson is that the campaign discovered a robust qualitative separation\u2014acid-base-like coupling alongside a strongly bounded loading response\u2014but did not uniquely identify the bound's cause. The recommendation is useful for preserving that empirical anchor, not for converting an effective explanation into a universally validated mechanism."
}

{
  "report": "# Mechanistic report: bounded aqueous acid dissociation and precipitation response\n\n## 1. Main interpretation\n\nThe observations support an effective monoprotic acid relationship with an apparent pKa close to 5.02, coupled to a strongly saturating relationship between nominal reagent concentration and the chemically expressed acidity. Dilution generally raises pH and increases the dissociated fraction. Increasing loading does the reverse, most clearly at low loading, but the response becomes nearly flat at higher loading. The precipitation proxy rises between the two lowest tested loadings and then remains approximately constant over a very broad higher-loading range.\n\nThe important qualification is that nominal reagent concentration cannot simply be substituted for dissolved acid concentration in an ideal, unbuffered weak-acid mass balance. That model gives much stronger concentration dependence than observed. A capped or saturating effective acid pool is one useful explanation. The experiments do not identify an actual precipitating species, a solubility product, or a microscopic reaction network.\n\nThis report analyzes the completed public observations only. It does not change the sealed handoff or recommendation, and no additional laboratory work was performed.\n\n## 2. Experimental evidence and measurement basis\n\nExactly 12 batches were completed, each with explicit termination and a final assay. Each also received one intermediate pH-meter measurement. All used water, the anonymous reagent, and no catalyst. The standard preparation included a 60-second heating operation targeted at 298.15 K with stirring at 300 rpm. Staged interventions were followed by an additional 60-second wait at the same stirring speed. The target temperature was not attained identically: representative pre-assay temperatures were approximately 297.867 K for 0.050 L preparations, 298.104 K for a 0.020 L high-loading preparation, and 298.148 K for a 0.010 L preparation.\n\nThe reported observables are pH_normalized = pH/14, acid dissociation fraction alpha, and a bounded precipitation proxy P. The table converts final-assay pH_normalized to pH; these are not additional electrode measurements. Values are rounded for readability.\n\n| Batch | Preparation and endpoint | Nominal endpoint concentration, mol/L | Final pH | Final alpha | Final P |\n|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|\n| 1 | 0.001 mol in 0.018 L, measured, then 0.036 L water added | approximately 0.0185 | 3.9396 | 0.08022 | 0.15928 |\n| 2 | 0.001 mol in 0.054 L, direct preparation | 0.01852 | 3.9351 | 0.08080 | 0.15002 |\n| 3 | 0.001 mol in 0.018 L, direct preparation | 0.05556 | 3.8780 | 0.07612 | 0.16001 |\n| 4 | 0.000010 mol in 0.050 L | 0.00020 | 4.5066 | 0.22516 | 0.04521 |\n| 5 | 0.000100 mol in 0.050 L | 0.00200 | 4.1184 | 0.10375 | 0.13870 |\n| 6 | 0.001 mol in 0.050 L | 0.02000 | 3.9741 | 0.06888 | 0.15251 |\n| 7 | 0.005 mol in 0.050 L | 0.10000 | 3.9014 | 0.06480 | 0.14893 |\n| 8 | 0.020 mol in 0.050 L | 0.40000 | 3.8767 | 0.06460 | 0.15798 |\n| 9 | 0.040 mol in 0.020 L | 2.00000 | 3.8346 | 0.07428 | 0.14686 |\n| 10 | 0.020 mol in 0.010 L | 2.00000 | 3.8941 | 0.07848 | 0.15659 |\n| 11 | 0.040 mol in 0.020 L, measured, then 0.040 L water added | approximately 0.6667 | 3.8886 | 0.07164 | 0.15537 |\n| 12 | 0.001 mol in 0.050 L, measured, then 0.019 mol reagent added | approximately 0.400 | 3.9625 | 0.07583 | 0.15985 |\n\nConcentrations in this table are nominal added-reagent/added-water ratios, not measured dissolved concentrations. The pH meter consumed 0.000030 L and the final assay consumed 0.000300 L. Batch 1 therefore had 0.053970 L after dilution and before its final assay; batch 11 had 0.059970 L. For batch 12, the intermediate sample withdrawal also means the final concentration is not exactly the simple 0.020/0.050 ratio. Exact retained reagent amounts after withdrawal were not independently measured.\n\nAll 12 intended batches obtained final observations; none was discarded. An orchestration interruption occurred during batch 4. Public status showed that preparation and heating had committed but its intermediate measurement had not yet committed; the run resumed from that state. This is not evidence of a failed chemical measurement or grounds to replace a result.\n\n## 3. Acid dissociation: a robust cross-observable relationship\n\nA useful candidate relationship is\n\nalpha = 1 / [1 + 10^(pKa_eff - pH)].\n\nEquivalently,\n\npKa_eff = pH - log10[alpha/(1-alpha)].\n\nThis is a hypothesis about the relationship between public observables, not an assertion that all nominal reagent exists as one freely dissolved acid.\n\nApplying the second equation to the 12 final-assay pairs gives apparent pKa values, in batch order:\n\n4.999, 4.991, 4.962, 5.043, 5.055, 5.105, 5.061, 5.037, 4.930, 4.964, 5.001, 5.048.\n\nTheir unweighted mean is 5.0164. The observed spread, approximately 4.93\u20135.11, includes measurement variation and potentially small process effects; it is not a confidence interval. At alpha near 0.07, even a modest absolute error in alpha materially affects the logarithmic pKa estimate.\n\nThe archival record gave an 80% interval of 4.9694\u20135.0694. The campaign is consistent with retaining that local effective acidity estimate. The mean agrees closely with the archival center, and individual departures are not surprising given the stated channel noise. This supports an effective pKa around 5.02; it does not establish an exact universal constant or independent confirmation of a real molecular acid identity.\n\nThe interpretation was shaped particularly by batches 4 and 5. Their pH and alpha differ substantially from the higher-loading plateau, yet the same transformed pKa remains approximately applicable. Thus a common dissociation relationship explains more than the narrow archival anchor alone.\n\n## 4. Why nominal concentration is not the whole mechanism\n\nFor an ideal unbuffered monoprotic acid, neglecting water autoionization and activity corrections,\n\nKa = H^2/(C - H),\nalpha = H/C,\npH = -log10(H),\n\nwhere C is dissolved analytical acid concentration and H is hydrogen-ion concentration on the chosen standard-state basis.\n\nThe experiment does not support setting C equal to total added reagent divided by liquid volume over the full tested range. For example, batch 9 has nominal concentration 2 mol/L but pH 3.8346 and alpha 0.07428. If all nominal reagent were the acid pool and H = alpha C, those two measured quantities would be grossly inconsistent. The public reagent loading is therefore not a directly usable dissolved-acid mass balance under that simple model.\n\nA diagnostic construction makes the discrepancy clearer. Define\n\nC_eff = 10^(-pH)/alpha.\n\nThis is the acid concentration that would reconcile the two observed channels under the ideal unbuffered model. It is a derived quantity, not an independently measured dissolved concentration. For batches 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, and 9 respectively, it is approximately:\n\n0.000138, 0.000734, 0.001541, 0.001937, 0.002056, and 0.001970 mol/L.\n\nMeanwhile, nominal concentration spans 0.0002 to 2 mol/L. This construction suggests a saturating chemically expressed pool of order 0.002 mol/L. It explains why pH and alpha become almost insensitive to large additional nominal loading.\n\nA compact candidate model is therefore:\n\n1. Compute nominal concentration c = n_retained/V_liquid.\n2. Map c to an effective acid pool C_eff = F(c), with F increasing and saturating.\n3. Use Ka_eff approximately 10^(-5.02).\n4. Compute H = [sqrt(Ka_eff^2 + 4 Ka_eff C_eff) - Ka_eff]/2.\n5. Compute pH = -log10(H) and alpha = H/C_eff.\n\nA function such as F(c) = C_max c/(c + c_half) is a possible smooth representation, with C_max of order 0.002 mol/L. I have not established that particular form or fitted a uniquely identifiable half-saturation constant. Other saturating functions, activity-based descriptions, or a buffered equilibrium could reproduce the same observations.\n\nThe most secure finding is the saturation pattern itself. In the constant-volume series, increasing nominal concentration tenfold from batch 4 to batch 5 lowers pH by 0.3882 and alpha by 0.1214. Increasing it another tenfold from batch 5 to batch 6 lowers pH by only 0.1442. Between batches 7 and 8, a fourfold increase changes pH by only -0.0247 and alpha by -0.00020. The latter differences are small relative to measurement uncertainty.\n\n## 5. Testing the archival dilution relationship\n\nThe archival anchor specified 0.001 mol diluted from 0.018 to 0.054 L near 298.15 K, with predicted changes:\n\nDelta alpha = +0.004043983,\nDelta pH_normalized = +0.001825842,\n\nor Delta pH = +0.02556.\n\nBatch 1 directly approximated this path. Before dilution, its pH-meter values were pH_normalized = 0.278434763, alpha = 0.066347829, and P = 0.181363537. After dilution, its final assay gave pH_normalized = 0.281399282, alpha = 0.080218438, and P = 0.159281533. The observed changes were therefore:\n\nDelta pH_normalized = +0.00296452, corresponding to Delta pH = +0.04150;\nDelta alpha = +0.01387061;\nDelta P = -0.02208200.\n\nThese differences have the predicted dilution direction, but compare two instruments at two times. They are not precise estimates of a universal dilution coefficient.\n\nThe independently prepared final endpoints provide a second comparison. Batch 2 minus batch 3 gives:\n\nDelta pH_normalized = +0.00407538, or Delta pH = +0.05706;\nDelta alpha = +0.00467969;\nDelta P = -0.00999810.\n\nThe alpha difference is close to the archival estimate. The pH difference is larger, but not decisively inconsistent given the noise and lack of extensive replication. For orientation, the declared final-assay noise scales are 0.002 in normalized pH and 0.006 in alpha, giving independent pairwise difference scales of about 0.00283 and 0.00849 respectively, before additional process uncertainty.\n\nI therefore retain the archival relationship as a plausible local estimate. I do not reject it, but neither do I claim that the campaign precisely verified its numerical coefficients. The evidence supports weak positive dilution responses near this anchor more strongly than any exact slope.\n\n## 6. Precipitation proxy and its coupling\n\nThe clearest precipitation-proxy change is between batches 4 and 5: P rises from 0.04521 to 0.13870 as nominal concentration rises from 0.0002 to 0.002 mol/L. Over the higher-loading endpoints, P is generally about 0.15\u20130.16. Even batches 9 and 10 at nominal 2 mol/L give only 0.14686 and 0.15659.\n\nThis supports an increasing, then saturating loading response, with most of the observed rise occurring below the broad high-loading plateau. At the same time, pH and alpha decrease toward their plateaus. These coupled changes can be represented by a common latent effective concentration: loading raises C_eff, which lowers pH and alpha and increases a bounded proxy P = G(C_eff). An alternative representation could make P a function of pH or speciation. This campaign cannot distinguish these representations because loading changes these quantities together.\n\nThe proxy is not calibrated precipitate mass, solid volume, or a dissolved concentration. A value near 0.15 does not mean that 15% of the reagent precipitated. Nor does a nonzero value establish a solid phase without a background or detection model. No zero-reagent chemical blank, solid characterization, or dissolved-phase assay was obtained.\n\nA precipitation-limited dissolved pool could explain both the acidity plateau and the proxy response. However, the proxy itself also plateaus, whereas actual precipitate mass could continue increasing. Thus a physical precipitation interpretation requires a saturating readout or another normalization. It cannot be inferred directly from the numerical proxy alone.\n\n## 7. Scale and preparation history\n\nBatches 9 and 10 were an equal-nominal-concentration scale comparison: 0.040 mol/0.020 L versus 0.020 mol/0.010 L. Their final differences are 0.05949 pH units, 0.00420 in alpha, and 0.00973 in P. These are modest relative to the measurement scales. They do not reveal a strong independent total-amount effect, but because both are on the apparent plateau, this comparison has limited power to establish concentration-only control.\n\nBatch 1, prepared by dilution, closely matches directly prepared batch 2 at the final nominal concentration: their pH values differ by 0.00452 and alpha by 0.00058. Their P values differ by 0.00927. This is evidence against a large path effect at that endpoint, not proof of complete path independence.\n\nBatch 12 reaches approximately the same final loading and volume as batch 8, but by staged reagent addition. Its final pH is higher by 0.08579, alpha by 0.01123, and P by 0.00187. The first two differences merit caution about assuming exact history independence. They could reflect preparation history, additional waiting, temperature differences, measurement noise, or a combination; one pair cannot identify the cause.\n\nBatch 11 tests dilution on the high-loading plateau. Its intermediate pH_normalized was 0.281006852, alpha 0.074347760, and P 0.151339889. Following dilution from approximately 0.020 to 0.060 L, its final values were 0.277754520, 0.071641201, and 0.155374127. The small changes do not show the positive dilution response seen near the archival anchor. Because both endpoints remain highly loaded and the comparison crosses instruments, this is consistent with a nearly flat response rather than persuasive evidence that dilution intrinsically lowers pH.\n\n## 8. Uncertainty and competing explanations\n\nThe final-assay declared noise scales are 0.002 in pH_normalized, 0.006 in alpha, and 0.006 in P. The corresponding pH-meter scales are larger for these processed channels. The inspected batch-1 electrode packet also supplied its own raw-pH calibration and uncertainty information. Raw electrode uncertainty should not simply be substituted for uncertainty in every processed channel or cross-instrument comparison.\n\nSeveral limitations are consequential:\n\n- The pKa calculation combines two noisy channels and becomes particularly sensitive to alpha near the low-alpha plateau.\n- Only a few endpoint preparations were approximately repeated. There is no full variance decomposition.\n- Heating targets produced small volume-dependent temperature differences. Temperature effects were not independently characterized.\n- Sampling slightly perturbed volume and material inventory.\n- Low-loading coverage consists of only two widely spaced points before the broad plateau. The detailed shape and location of the transition are weakly constrained.\n- Measurements of pH and alpha may be separate synthetic readouts of a shared latent calculation. Their agreement with a dissociation equation is evidence of an effective relationship, not necessarily independent molecular corroboration.\n\nReasonable competing explanations are:\n\n1. A solubility- or partition-limited acid pool. Increasing loading eventually adds material outside the active dissolved pool, leaving pH and alpha almost constant.\n2. Buffering, complexation, or activity effects. A hidden acid/base reservoir or nonideal activities could regulate hydrogen activity without a literal concentration cap.\n3. A saturating benchmark mapping. Nominal loading may be transformed into bounded effective acidity and precipitation observables. This would reproduce the public relationships without corresponding to a unique physical phase equilibrium.\n4. Finite-time or preparation-dependent behavior. A short approach to equilibrium might contribute to the staged-path discrepancies. The campaign did not include a systematic time series to establish relaxation rates.\n\nThe final equilibrium_residual values range from zero to about 0.0112. They are useful environment diagnostics, but small values do not establish that all possible histories have converged physically. Likewise, equilibrium_confidence is an environment diagnostic, not my uncertainty, a confidence interval, or a research success score.\n\n## 9. Scope and testable implications\n\nDirect empirical coverage is limited to water, anonymous reagent loadings from 0.000010 to 0.040 mol per batch, nominal endpoint concentrations approximately 0.0002\u20132 mol/L, water additions totaling 0.010\u20130.060 L per batch, and short near-room-temperature protocols. No catalyst, other solvent, deliberate pH adjustment, independent salt perturbation, or broad temperature scan was tested.\n\nWithin that scope, the most defensible predictive statements are qualitative: strong response to loading at the dilute end; weak additional response on the higher-loading plateau; approximately constant effective pKa near 5.02; and a bounded precipitation proxy that rises early and then changes little. Quantitative interpolation should carry instrument noise plus model-form uncertainty, especially near the sparsely sampled transition.\n\nOutside the observed range, no specific limit is established. In particular, the model is not validated toward pure water, extreme concentrations, other solvents, or substantially different temperatures. The formal low-concentration limit of a weak-acid formula would also require water autoionization and any background ions, which this campaign did not identify.\n\nPotential discriminating tests, not performed here, would include direct dissolved-material or solid-phase measurements, equal-concentration scale comparisons below the plateau, independently controlled pH at fixed loading, and matched-instrument time series after dilution. Those would distinguish a real limited dissolved pool from buffering, proxy saturation, and history effects.\n\n## 10. Final assessment\n\nThe best-supported mechanism is an effective acid-speciation law with pKa around 5.02 embedded in a bounded, saturating loading-response system. The archival acidity estimate remains credible. Its small local dilution response is compatible with the experiments but is not precisely determined. The strongest revision to a naive chemistry picture is that nominal total reagent concentration is not equivalent to the dissolved acid concentration governing the observed pH and dissociation fraction.\n\nBatch 1 remains the previously committed evidentiary anchor because it directly probes the archival dilution path and has independently prepared endpoint comparisons. That selection does not imply that it uniquely identifies the mechanism or optimizes a native score. The campaign identifies useful effective relationships, while leaving the molecular identity, phase inventory, detailed precipitation law, and microscopic origin of the saturation unresolved."
}
```
