### K2

```json
{
  "report": "# Retrospective assessment of the completed campaign\n\nI interpret \u201cK1\u201d as the sealed mechanistic report and \u201cQ\u201d as the sealed blind predictions. No prediction truth has been supplied. This assessment uses only the previously observed campaign results and the statements already made in K1 and Q. It neither changes those submissions nor claims that interpretations developed during reporting were already established during experimentation.\n\n## 1. What the initial information supported, contradicted, or left untested\n\nThe initial material information contained substantive claims. In particular, the archival estimate supplied an effective pKa interval of 4.9694\u20135.0694 with stated 80% coverage and a local dilution prediction for 0.001 mol diluted from 0.018 to 0.054 L near 298.15 K: an increase of 0.004043983 in dissociation fraction and 0.001825842 in normalized pH. These were local effective claims, not a complete mechanism.\n\n**The effective pKa claim received positive support.** K1 calculated apparent pKa from the final-assay pairs using pH \u2212 log10[alpha/(1\u2212alpha)]. The twelve values had an unweighted mean of 5.0164 and ranged approximately from 4.93 to 5.11. Importantly, the agreement extended beyond the archival loading: batches 4 and 5, whose dissociation fractions were 0.22516 and 0.10375, yielded apparent pKa values of approximately 5.043 and 5.055. That is more informative than merely failing to reject a value near 5.02 at one endpoint.\n\nThere are limits to this support. The pKa calculations use two public channels that may share a latent synthetic calculation; they are not necessarily independent molecular measurements. Nor does agreement of the mean validate the archival interval's stated coverage across repeated campaigns or worlds.\n\n**The local dilution claim was compatible with the observations but not precisely verified.** Batch 1's intermediate-to-final differences were +0.01387 in dissociation and +0.0029645 in normalized pH. The independent final-assay comparison, batch 2 minus batch 3, gave +0.004680 and +0.0040754. The dissociation difference in the latter comparison was close to the archival prediction; the normalized-pH difference was larger. Given the declared measurement scales, neither comparison decisively refuted the archival coefficients. K1's wording\u2014\u201ca plausible local estimate\u201d rather than a precisely established slope\u2014was appropriate.\n\nThis is a case of limited corroboration and no decisive counterexample, not proof of the exact numerical relationship. There was no clear archival falsification that I knowingly ignored.\n\n**A broader ideal-acid interpretation was contradicted, but it was not an explicit archival claim.** K1 rejected substituting nominal reagent concentration directly into an ideal, unbuffered acid mass balance. Batch 9 illustrates why: nominal concentration was 2 mol/L, while final pH was 3.8346 and alpha was 0.07428. Those observations cannot both satisfy H = alpha \u00d7 nominal concentration. It would be misleading to describe that as refuting the supplied pKa estimate. It refutes an additional interpretation of what the reagent inventory represents.\n\n**Exact concentration-only and history-independent behavior remained unproven.** Batch 12 versus batch 8 supplied a cautionary difference: staged addition produced final pH higher by 0.08579 and alpha higher by 0.01123. K1 acknowledged this. Nevertheless, Q used equal central predictions for equivalent final compositions. That is a pragmatic unresolved assumption, not an experimentally established equivalence. The evidence is suggestive of a possible history effect, rather than a decisive contradiction that justifies assigning its direction to every new preparation.\n\nFinally, the initial material labels did not establish real catalyst identities, a particular acid, a precipitating species, or transferable solvent physics. Those remained untested, and K1 correctly did not claim otherwise.\n\n## 2. Which experiments formed the interpretation, and what drove their selection\n\n**Batches 1\u20133 were primarily prior-driven.** Batch 1 directly approximated the archival dilution path, while batches 2 and 3 independently prepared its endpoints. This was a strong choice for testing the supplied local relationship and checking whether staged dilution led to a grossly different endpoint. It was less strong for estimating a precise dilution effect because batch 1 compared different instruments and the endpoint controls were not extensively replicated.\n\nBatch 1 and batch 2 eventually supported similar final states: pH differed by only 0.00452 and alpha by 0.00058. This justified treating large path effects as unsupported at that particular endpoint. It did not establish global reversibility.\n\n**Batches 4\u20138 were the most important for recognizing the shape of the loading response.** At fixed 0.050 L volume, the first tenfold increase, from batch 4 to batch 5, lowered pH by 0.3882 and alpha by 0.1214, while increasing the precipitation proxy from 0.04521 to 0.13870. Later changes were much smaller: batches 7 and 8 differed by only \u22120.0247 pH units and \u22120.00020 in alpha despite a fourfold loading increase.\n\nThe contemporaneous campaign update after eight batches said that responses changed strongly at low loading and were flatter at higher loading. That qualitative recognition genuinely occurred during execution. The transformed pKa calculations and numerical effective-pool reconstruction were performed later for K1. I should not present them as if they had guided the earlier experimental choices.\n\n**Batches 9 and 10 were design-driven tests of scaling.** Their equal nominal concentration, 2 mol/L, at different amounts and volumes was intended to distinguish concentration control from independent amount or volume effects. The comparison showed no large difference, but both preparations sat on the apparent plateau. Consequently, this design had low sensitivity to several competing models: two models with different scaling rules can both appear constant after saturation.\n\n**Batches 11 and 12 were tests of preparation history, but not cleanly isolated ones.** Batch 11 diluted a highly loaded preparation; batch 12 accumulated reagent in stages. These choices were motivated by the need to test endpoint-based reasoning rather than by a measured kinetic model. The extra wait and different thermal histories introduced confounding. Batch 12's departure from batch 8 should therefore be retained as a warning, not promoted to a resolved history law.\n\nSeveral choices rested on unverified assumptions: that near-room-temperature heating and a short wait were adequate to reveal the relevant state; that a wide loading range would cross a useful transition; that the pH-meter channels would be more informative than UV\u2013visible measurements; and that nominal concentration was a useful organizing variable. Some were sensible, especially avoiding an instrument with no listed task-relevant observables, but they remained choices rather than findings.\n\nA procedural shortcoming also deserves acknowledgment: the concise opening note described classes of tests but did not publicly enumerate every one of the twelve recipes or a detailed statistical decision rule. The executed series was interpretable, but it should not be portrayed retrospectively as a fully specified confirmatory design.\n\n## 3. The principal competing explanations and what is distinguishable\n\nK1's central claim was an \u201ceffective acid-speciation law with pKa around 5.02 embedded in a bounded, saturating loading-response system.\u201d That describes the public relationships more securely than it identifies their physical cause.\n\nThe leading alternatives remain:\n\n- A solubility- or partition-limited active acid pool, with excess nominal material outside the acidity-controlling pool.\n- Buffering, complexation, or activity effects that regulate hydrogen activity without a literal concentration cap.\n- A synthetic saturating mapping from loading to public observables, with no uniquely inferable phase-equilibrium mechanism.\n- Finite-time or history-dependent behavior superimposed on one of those explanations.\n\nThe observations distinguish these from a simple model in which every nominal mole contributes to one ideal, unbuffered dissolved acid pool. They also establish that the response is not constant over the entire tested domain: batches 4 and 5 differ strongly.\n\nThey do not distinguish a true dissolved-pool cap from an algebraic mapping that yields the same pH and alpha. K1's C_eff = 10^(\u2212pH)/alpha is a useful diagnostic construction, but it is not an independent measurement of dissolved acid. Its plateau is partly a restatement of the plateaus in its input channels.\n\nLikewise, the association between increasing loading, lower pH, lower alpha, and a higher precipitation proxy does not identify a causal direction. Loading could affect all three through a shared latent variable. Alternatively, precipitation could limit acidity, or acidity could influence the proxy. No independent pH perturbation or phase measurement broke that coupling.\n\nThe equal-concentration comparison weakly constrains large scale effects at high loading, while the batch-1/batch-2 comparison constrains large path effects near their common endpoint. Neither establishes universality. Batch 12 also prevents a strong claim that all preparations with the same nominal endpoint are experimentally interchangeable.\n\n## 4. The single additional experiment I would choose\n\nThis is a hypothetical design only. The actual campaign resources are exhausted, so execution would require authorization for one additional complete batch.\n\nI would prepare **0.000010 mol reagent in 0.005 L water**, use the same nominal heating operation as the campaign\u2014298.15 K target for 60 seconds at 300 rpm\u2014take one pH-meter measurement, explicitly terminate, and obtain a final assay. I would add no catalyst and perform no intervening dosing or dilution. The small-volume target should be checked against the public action bounds if such a new allocation were ever authorized; it lies within the solvent-addition range displayed during this campaign.\n\nThis gives nominal concentration 0.002 mol/L, matching batch 5, while using one-tenth of batch 5's amount and volume. It also matches batch 4's total amount while concentrating that amount tenfold. It therefore makes more efficient use of the existing evidence than another comparison deep on the plateau.\n\nThe primary readouts would be final pH_normalized, alpha, and precipitation proxy. The intermediate reading would supply a matched public measurement and a check for gross channel disagreement, not an independent kinetic series.\n\nPossible outcomes would change the interpretation as follows:\n\n- **Similarity to batch 5**\u2014approximately 0.294 normalized pH, 0.104 alpha, and 0.139 precipitation proxy\u2014would support concentration-based scaling in the transition region, where it is more discriminating than the batch-9/batch-10 comparison.\n- **Similarity to batch 4**\u2014approximately 0.322, 0.225, and 0.045\u2014would count strongly against concentration-only scaling and suggest a separate amount dependence, a minimum effective-volume rule, or another hidden extensive-state effect.\n- **Intermediate or channel-specific behavior** would imply that a single concentration mapping does not organize every observable equally. For example, pH and alpha matching batch 5 while the precipitation proxy matched batch 4 would challenge a common one-dimensional latent-pool explanation.\n- **A large inconsistency between instruments or with both anchors** would require considering protocol sensitivity and measurement behavior before selecting a new mechanism.\n\nOne batch could discriminate only large effects. It would not establish repeatability, and small-volume heat transfer and sampling fractions would remain confounders. The experiment is chosen for expected information, not because it could by itself prove a molecular mechanism.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe research objective explicitly concerned characterization, not yield or scalar-score optimization. The campaign reflected that objective: it used all twelve completed batches for dilution, loading, scale, and history comparisons rather than repeating whichever condition produced the largest native scalar.\n\nThe sealed recommendation also reflected this distinction. Batch 1 was selected as an evidentiary anchor for the archival dilution test, not as a proven best operational recipe. Its final environment diagnostic equilibrium_confidence was about 0.391, whereas batch 4's was about 0.533. The recommendation was therefore not simply a choice of the most favorable value of that diagnostic. In any event, equilibrium_confidence is an environment output, not my confidence or the research objective.\n\nThere is no evidence that I knowingly sacrificed identifiability to improve the native score. A more accurate criticism is that some identification-oriented experiments were poorly placed. Batches 9\u201311 spent scarce batches exploring a region where the chemistry-facing outputs were nearly flat. That may be useful for bounding gross high-loading behavior, but it yields little leverage on detailed saturation shape or scale dependence.\n\nConversely, choosing batch 1 as the recommendation prioritized traceable evidence over any claim to operational performance. That is appropriate for the stated task, but it should not be repackaged as optimization success.\n\nThe main tradeoff was thus between broad coverage and precise identification, rather than between mechanism and score. Twelve intermediate readings across twelve batches provided broad paired observations, but fewer conditions with deliberate replication or matched timing might have better separated noise, kinetics, and preparation effects.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and the weakest blind predictions\n\n**Raw measurement evidence was underused.** I inspected the batch-1 pH-meter packet, but did not systematically inspect all meter packets or the large final-assay packets. The inspected packet reported a scalar pH of 3.898 and replicate pH values of 3.870 and 3.882. That difference should have prompted a more explicit treatment of the relationship between raw replicates, the packet scalar, and processed estimates. It is not necessarily a failure, but it cautions against treating every supplied number as the same estimator.\n\nSimilarly, the final-assay packets contained additional calibration and signal information that might have helped assess channel dependence, clipping, and uncertainty. Their size made them less convenient to inspect, but convenience is not evidence that they were irrelevant. I cannot now claim they would have resolved the mechanism because I did not analyze them.\n\n**The intermediate readings were not used in a comprehensive joint analysis.** For example, batch 7's intermediate alpha was 0.09491 and its final alpha was 0.06480, a difference of about 0.0301. Batch 5's corresponding difference was about 0.0313. Those differences could reflect noise, instrument-specific behavior, or process changes. A systematic comparison of all intermediate\u2013final pairs would have been more informative than using selected pairs mainly for narrative checks.\n\n**Operational telemetry was acknowledged but not modeled.** The preparation temperature varied with volume. K1 correctly warned against reading post-closeout reset telemetry as the assay temperature, but did not quantify temperature's contribution. Sampling also mattered proportionally more at smaller volumes.\n\nAmong Q, the least reliable predictions are:\n\n- **Q08**, which extrapolates to one-fifteenth of the lowest tested nominal concentration and one-tenth of the lowest tested reagent amount. Its alpha estimate of 0.613 is strongly model-dependent.\n- **Q09 and Q04**, which rely on concentration-based scaling at amounts or volumes not covered by a comparable experimental pair.\n- **Q10**, which extrapolates to 6.667 mol/L, beyond the tested maximum of 2 mol/L.\n- **Q05\u2013Q07**, whose identical central predictions assume that different dosing histories collapse to the same final state despite the unresolved batch-8/batch-12 discrepancy and the absence of a waiting step.\n\nThere is an additional issue shared by all twelve queries: they omit the campaign's heating and intermediate measurement. Q mentioned this and widened intervals, but there was no experimental estimate of the resulting bias. A common protocol effect could shift many predictions together; marginal intervals do not eliminate that risk.\n\nThe intervals most plausibly too narrow are the near-domain intervals if that shared protocol effect is appreciable, and the precipitation intervals in trace-loading extrapolations. In particular, Q08's upper80 of 0.029 for the precipitation proxy assumes a low background and a decline toward zero. K1 explicitly said that no blank or detection model established that limit. The alpha and pH intervals for Q08 were broad, but the proxy interval may not fully represent the same structural uncertainty.\n\nThere is also a methodological tension between K1 and Q. K1 stated that a specific saturating function and half-saturation parameter were not established. Q then used C_eff = 0.00185c/(c + 0.0025) to produce numbers. Q disclosed that this was a prediction-time approximation, so it was not a hidden revision. Nevertheless, the nominal 80% coverage was judgmental rather than empirically calibrated, and the intervals may underrepresent uncertainty across alternative functions. Without prediction truth, I cannot conclude that any specific interval missed or that a model was vindicated.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how to evaluate it\n\nThe committed recommendation was **batch 1**, selected because it connected the archival dilution anchor to independently prepared endpoints. Its utility is evidentiary. It is not a proven optimum, a uniquely identified mechanism, or a validated process recipe.\n\nIts principal limitations are the mixed-instrument before/after comparison, sample withdrawal before dilution, different process time from direct preparations, lack of repeated full dilution trajectories, and restriction to one anonymous reagent in water near room temperature. Agreement with batch 2 constrains the final endpoint more than it validates the entire path.\n\nA future reproducibility programme would first repeat the full batch-1 procedure with the same measurement sequence, preserving all outcomes and specifying variability criteria in advance. Separate matched preparations at the two endpoints would help distinguish assay repeatability from path variability. Such repetitions should not replace the original record when they are more favorable.\n\nLocal robustness would require nearby perturbations in amount, initial and final volume, target temperature, waiting time, and measurement sequence. The present evidence supports a small neighborhood only tentatively; it does not show that the dilution coefficients are invariant to those changes.\n\nCross-material transfer would require new evidence for other solvent categories, catalysts, or reagents. The public material names do not license transferring the inferred pKa or saturation scale to real chemistry. Cross-world transfer would likewise require independent worlds or instances: a fit to this world's public response cannot establish the same latent parameters elsewhere.\n\nFinally, \u201chighest observed in the sample\u201d and \u201coptimal\u201d are different claims. Even selecting the largest scalar among twelve noisy outcomes would establish only a sample ranking over those tested protocols, subject to selection noise. This campaign did not perform a global optimization, resolve all feasible alternatives, or demonstrate repeatability of a winner. Batch 1 was not selected under such a claim. Its defensible status is the previously stated one: a useful, completed reference experiment for the local dilution question, with explicit limitations on mechanism and transfer."
}

Its final endpoint closely matches independently prepared batch 2.\n\nThe appropriate conclusion is that the archival pKa requires revision, whereas its small local dilution increments remain insufficiently resolved to reject.\n\n## 6. Why nominal concentration is not enough for an ideal weak-acid model\n\nFor a simple ideal monoprotic acid without other important sources of charge, one would approximately write\n\n[H+] = alpha C_total,\n\nKa = [H+] alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha),\n\nand therefore\n\nKa = C_total alpha^2/(1 \u2212 alpha).\n\nIdentifying C_total with the nominal n/V does not reconcile the observed pH and fraction across this campaign.\n\nAs a diagnostic, define\n\nC_equiv = 10^(\u2212pH)/alpha.\n\nThis is the concentration that would be required by the simplified charge-balance equation, treating hydrogen activity as concentration. It is a derived effective quantity, not a measured dissolved concentration.\n\nSelected values are:\n\n| Batch | Nominal concentration, mol/L | Derived C_equiv, mol/L |\n|---|---:|---:|\n| 5 | 0.00001852 | 0.00001628 |\n| 9 | approximately 0.0001852 | 0.00012884 |\n| 12 | 0.0001852 | 0.00013674 |\n| 4 | 0.001852 | 0.00066576 |\n| 10 | approximately 0.001852 | 0.00071678 |\n| 2 | 0.01852 | 0.00156841 |\n| 7 | 2.222 | 0.00162375 |\n\nAt the smallest loading, the inferred and nominal concentrations are reasonably close. With increasing nominal loading, they diverge dramatically, and C_equiv appears to approach a scale of roughly 0.0015\u20130.0020 mol/L.\n\nThis calculation explains why an ideal weak-acid model based directly on nominal loading is inadequate. It does not demonstrate that dissolved acid actually saturates at that concentration: activity corrections, charge compensation, public-channel definitions, and synthetic bounding can all affect this inference.\n\n## 7. A minimal working process model\n\nA compact representation consistent with the evidence is:\n\n1. Calculate nominal loading C_nom = n/V, accounting for additions and sampling where possible.\n2. Map nominal loading to an effective acid-state concentration C_eff = f(C_nom, other state variables).\n3. Apply an effective acid-base relation with Ka_eff approximately 10^(\u22125.02).\n4. Map the same state, or a coupled supersaturation state, to the precipitation proxy P.\n5. Add instrument-specific noise and bounded-output effects.\n\nIf C_eff is defined as an ideal-equivalent acid concentration, one possible parameterization is\n\nh = [sqrt(Ka_eff^2 + 4 Ka_eff C_eff) \u2212 Ka_eff]/2,\n\nalpha = h/C_eff,\n\npH = \u2212log10(h).\n\nThis is a useful representation, not an identified microscopic law. The available evidence suggests that f initially increases with nominal loading and then becomes strongly sublinear or saturating. Its exact functional form is not established. I have not fitted a unique Langmuir law, solubility cap, or activity model, because the observations do not discriminate those choices adequately.\n\nThe main advantage of this two-stage representation is that it preserves the well-supported pH\u2013fraction relation without forcing the unsupported identity C_eff = C_nom.\n\n## 8. What can be said about precipitation?\n\nThe measured quantity is a bounded normalized proxy, not a precipitated mole fraction or solid mass.\n\nThe observations indicate three broad regimes:\n\n- At nominal concentration 0.00001852 mol/L, batch 5 had a near-zero final proxy of 0.00312.\n- Around 0.0001852 mol/L, batches 9 and 12 had final proxies of 0.02551 and 0.04372.\n- Around 0.001852 mol/L and above, the proxy was approximately 0.14\u20130.16 in the final assays.\n\nBatch 9's intermediate proxy of 0.10204 at approximately 0.0005556 mol/L places another observation between the low and high regimes, although it came from the noisier intermediate instrument.\n\nThese data support a rising, saturating loading response for P. They do not identify a precise precipitation threshold. The low-concentration transition is sparsely sampled, the instruments differ in noise, and the outputs are bounded at zero. A small positive value near zero is not enough to establish a nonzero solid population.\n\nAt fixed nominal amount, dilution lowers both effective acidity and precipitation signal. Therefore pH and precipitation are correlated, but this campaign cannot establish whether precipitation is driven directly by pH, directly by loading, by ionic strength, or by a shared latent state. There was no independent pH intervention at fixed loading.\n\nNo solubility product can be estimated defensibly without species identities, ionic stoichiometry, dissolved-species concentrations, and an interpretable solid measurement. In particular, P = 0.16 must not be read as 16% of the reagent precipitated.\n\n## 9. Equilibrium, history, and time\n\nSeveral comparisons favor a largely endpoint-controlled response:\n\n- Batches 4 and 10 reached similar final states by direct preparation and staged loading.\n- Batches 2 and 11 reached almost identical final states by direct preparation and staged dilution.\n- Batches 9 and 12 had similar final pH and dissociation at the same nominal endpoint despite different preparation histories and hold durations.\n\nHowever, this is not proof of full thermodynamic equilibration. There were no dense time courses, no repeated same-instrument measurements within one unchanged vessel, and no approach from separately prepared acid-rich and base-rich states.\n\nBatch 12 does not isolate time alone: its intermediate-to-final comparison also changes instrument and includes termination. Its precipitation discrepancy relative to batch 9 leaves room for modest history or hold effects, process variability, or underrepresented uncertainty.\n\nThe final equilibrium_residual values were small, ranging from zero to approximately 0.00403. These are supportive environment diagnostics, not an independently established thermodynamic residual based on measured species balances. Similarly, equilibrium_confidence varied substantially across loading conditions; it is an environment output, not my uncertainty estimate.\n\n## 10. Uncertainty and evidential strength\n\nThe declared final-assay noise scales are 0.002 for pH_normalized and 0.006 each for dissociation fraction and precipitation signal. The intermediate pH-meter scales are 0.004, 0.015, and 0.02, respectively. Actual uncertainty may also contain process-proxy contributions.\n\nFor orientation, assuming independent noise and using only those declared scales:\n\n- A difference between two final normalized-pH readings has a noise scale of approximately 0.00283, or 0.040 pH unit.\n- A difference between two final dissociation or precipitation readings has a scale of approximately 0.00849.\n- An intermediate-to-final difference has scales of approximately 0.00447 in normalized pH, 0.0162 in dissociation fraction, and 0.0209 in precipitation proxy.\n\nThese calculations explain why the small archival dilution response is weakly resolved, while the staged low-loading contrasts are much more informative. They are not formal hypothesis tests and do not include unknown covariance or all process effects.\n\nThe transformed pKa is particularly sensitive to fraction error when alpha is small. Linear propagation gives the approximate fraction sensitivity\n\n|d pKa_eff/d alpha| = 1/[ln(10) alpha(1 \u2212 alpha)].\n\nNear alpha = 0.07, final-assay fraction noise alone contributes roughly 0.04 pKa unit; normalized-pH noise contributes about 0.028. Near alpha = 0.54, the fraction contribution is closer to 0.01. Thus an effective pKa near 5.02 is well supported as a descriptive relationship, but precision to several decimal places is not justified. A working uncertainty allowance of order 0.05\u20130.10 pKa unit is more credible than treating the displayed numerical precision as inferential precision; it is not a formally calibrated coverage interval.\n\nThe precipitation difference between batches 9 and 12 is about 0.0182, somewhat more than twice the simple final-to-final noise scale. I retain this discrepancy rather than averaging it away or presenting exact endpoint reproducibility.\n\n## 11. Reasonable competing explanations\n\n### A. Solubility-limited availability\n\nAs loading increases, a smaller share remains in an acid-base-active dissolved pool. This creates an apparent effective-concentration plateau, while precipitation signal rises. This is chemically coherent, but no direct dissolved/solid mass balance was obtained. It remains a candidate, not a demonstrated mechanism.\n\n### B. Activity and matrix effects\n\nNominal concentration may continue increasing while hydrogen activity, effective acid activity, or the meaning of the reported fraction changes nonlinearly. Such effects can produce the observed failure of [H+] = alpha C_nom without requiring a literal cap on dissolved material. No ionic-strength intervention or activity-coefficient measurement distinguishes this explanation from A.\n\n### C. A bounded synthetic mapping\n\nThe benchmark may explicitly transform loading into a bounded effective state and derive multiple channels from it. This can reproduce a stable pH\u2013fraction relation, apparent concentration saturation, and a correlated precipitation proxy without representing an ordinary physical precipitation equilibrium. Given the instrument boundary statements, this is an important explanation, not merely an experimental nuisance.\n\n### D. Instrument or output-channel limitations\n\nA processed channel could have a compressed response or other calibration limitation. The high-loading values are not close to the generic zero-to-one output bounds, so simple clipping at those outer bounds is not sufficient by itself. Nevertheless, an internal calibration or state transformation could cause compression. Agreement between intermediate and final channels does not eliminate a shared synthetic mapping.\n\n### E. Slow equilibration, termination effects, or preparation history\n\nThese might account for some discrepancies, particularly in precipitation. The endpoint comparisons argue against large history effects in the tested preparations but cannot exclude smaller effects. Instrument changes and termination are confounded with several within-batch interventions.\n\n### F. An unobserved buffer or additional acid-base component\n\nA buffer or secondary pool could attenuate pH changes. The relatively coherent single effective pKa favors a compact one-state description, but it does not uniquely rule out multiple microscopic species. Their identities and amounts are not available from these observations.\n\n## 12. Supported scope and untested extrapolations\n\nThe observations cover water preparations with nominal reagent amounts from 0.000001 to 0.04 mol, nominal liquid volumes from 0.018 to 0.054 L, and nominal concentrations from approximately 0.00001852 to 2.222 mol/L. The standard target temperature was 298.15 K, and stirring was 400 rpm. There was only one extended hold.\n\nWithin that scope, the strongest supported statements are:\n\n- Low-loading dilution raises pH and dissociation and reduces the precipitation proxy.\n- Increasing loading has the opposite effects before an apparent plateau is reached.\n- The reported pH and dissociation fraction follow an effective pKa relationship near 5.02.\n- Nominal loading cannot be inserted directly into an ideal unbuffered weak-acid mass balance across the entire range.\n- Endpoint concentration is a useful explanatory variable, although a complete separation of concentration, total amount, volume, and history has not been achieved.\n\nThe campaign does not establish solvent dependence, catalyst effects, temperature dependence, pressure dependence, stirring dependence, microscopic acid identity, solid identity, or a general law for real aqueous samples. It also does not establish behavior at zero reagent, volumes outside the tested range, concentrations below the lowest loading, or loadings above the highest tested point.\n\nFurther dilution below the lowest tested concentration might continue to increase alpha and pH, but eventually background chemistry and water autoionization could become important. Continued plateau behavior above the highest tested loading is likewise an extrapolation. Neither should be stated as an observation.\n\n## 13. Discriminating implications and final assessment\n\nThe explanation generates several testable implications, stated here without performing new experiments:\n\n- If the pH\u2013fraction relation is fundamental to the public state, new in-scope observations should remain close to pH = 5.02 + log10(alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)), even when nominal loading differs.\n- If a dissolved-availability limit causes the plateau, an independent dissolved-species measurement should become sublinear in nominal loading, while an appropriate total-material measurement need not plateau.\n- If precipitation depends independently on pH, changing pH at fixed total loading should alter the proxy beyond what a loading-only model predicts.\n- If history is important, opposite preparation paths to the same endpoint should produce reproducible differences under the same instrument and timing protocol.\n- If high-loading behavior is only a compressed public-channel mapping, additional channels with independent calibrations could diverge from a physical solubility interpretation.\n\nBatch 9 remains the selected evidentiary anchor because its staged dilution traversed a responsive region and produced coherent changes in all three principal chemical observables. It was not selected to maximize an environment diagnostic or yield.\n\nOverall, I regard the effective pH\u2013dissociation relationship and the transition from a responsive low-loading regime to a weakly responsive high-loading regime as the principal results. The physical source of that bounding, and the mechanistic meaning of the precipitation proxy, remain underdetermined. The appropriate model is therefore an effective, explicitly bounded description with competing microscopic explanations\u2014not a uniquely identified chemical mechanism."
}
```

{
  "report": "## 1. Initial claims: supported, contradicted, and untested\n\nI use K1 to refer to my sealed mechanistic report. This retrospective does not revise K1 or the sealed predictions, and no prediction truth has been supplied.\n\nThe initial material information did contain substantive quantitative claims. It supplied an effective-pKa interval of 4.6094\u20134.7094, described as an 80% interval from a local archival fit. It also predicted that diluting 0.001 mol from 0.018 to 0.054 L at 298.15 K would increase dissociation fraction by 0.008187416 and normalized pH by 0.003494987. These claims need separate assessments.\n\n**The effective-pKa claim encountered contrary evidence.** K1 calculated pKa_eff = 14q \u2212 log10[alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)] from the final public channels. The resulting values were approximately 4.98\u20135.08, centered near 5.02. Batch 5 was especially useful: its fraction of 0.5378 was far from the low-fraction regime where this transformation magnifies measurement error, yet it implied pKa_eff approximately 4.992. Thus the disagreement with the archival interval was not merely an artifact of transforming small fractions.\n\nThat rejection is conditional on a common effective Henderson\u2013Hasselbalch interpretation. It does not prove that an unobserved microscopic acidity, differently defined quantity, or different experimental scope could not match the archival value. K1 explicitly made this distinction.\n\n**The small archival dilution increments were not rejected.** The final-assay contrast between batches 1 and 2 gave changes of +0.003847 in normalized pH and +0.005464 in dissociation fraction. Those are compatible with the archival increments at the available precision. Compatibility is not strong confirmation: the final-to-final dissociation noise scale discussed in K1 was approximately 0.00849, larger than the observed change and comparable to the claimed effect.\n\nBatch 11 supplied an unfavorable-looking observation that should not be hidden. Its intermediate fraction was 0.08599 and its final fraction after dilution was 0.07427, a decrease rather than the predicted increase. However, this contrast changed instruments, and the intermediate dissociation noise scale was 0.015. K1 retained the reversal and explained why it did not convincingly resolve the sign of such a small effect. This is a case of observed contrary direction without sufficiently strong evidence to reject the claim, not a clean confirming result.\n\n**Other initial information was mostly scope and measurement definition, not a tested mechanism.** The normalization pH = 14q, the bounded nature of the precipitation proxy, and the synthetic-instrument boundary were contractual definitions used in interpretation. The campaign did not independently validate those definitions. It also did not test other solvents, catalyst categories, or real-material transferability.\n\nOn chronology, I did not begin by declaring the archival pKa wrong. After the first four batches, my commentary said that the joint pH\u2013fraction readings warranted a separate pKa check. After batches 5\u201310 and the numerical transformation, I explicitly stated that the effective pKa was near 5.0 and outside the archival interval. There was therefore an actual revision during the campaign, not merely an uncorrected discrepancy discovered after sealing. Conversely, the more elaborate effective-concentration analysis in K1 was a later synthesis and should not be represented as the model that had guided the first experiments.\n\n## 2. Experiments that formed or changed my judgment\n\n**Batches 1 and 2 were driven directly by the archival anchor.** Their amounts and volumes matched the supplied dilution comparison. This was a defensible way to make the prior falsifiable, but the anticipated effect was small relative to the available fraction noise. The design was therefore stronger as an anchor check than as a precise estimate of the dilution derivative.\n\n**Batch 3 tested a concentration-based explanation.** It used three times the amount and volume of batch 1 while retaining the same nominal concentration. Final normalized pH values were 0.27888 and 0.27722, fractions were 0.06317 and 0.06861, and precipitation signals were nearly identical. This supported concentration as an organizing variable. However, both conditions were in a weakly responsive regime. In retrospect, a matched-concentration comparison in the transition region would have been more discriminating. The expectation that n/V would dominate was initially a chemically motivated hypothesis, not a fact established by the material information.\n\n**Batches 4 and 5 exposed the responsive low-loading region.** Batch 4 gave fraction 0.11193 and normalized pH 0.29484; batch 5 gave 0.53780 and 0.36127. Batch 5 also had precipitation signal 0.00312. This expanded the picture beyond a nearly constant high-loading endpoint and helped make the effective-pKa interpretation credible across substantially different fractions.\n\n**Batches 6\u20138 established that high-loading behavior was compressed.** Across nominal concentrations of 0.5556\u20132.222 mol/L, their final fractions remained approximately 0.073\u20130.078 and their precipitation proxies approximately 0.159\u20130.161. This changed the mechanistic question from simply estimating an ideal weak-acid constant to explaining why nominal loading had so little additional effect. K1's statement that nominal concentration cannot be inserted directly into an ideal weak-acid mass balance follows from this evidence combined with the pH\u2013fraction relationship.\n\n**Batches 9 and 10 provided the strongest directional interventions.** Batch 9's staged dilution changed fraction from 0.15540 to 0.24526 and precipitation from 0.10204 to 0.02551. Batch 10's staged loading changed fraction from 0.24157 to 0.10255 and precipitation from 0.05021 to 0.14231. These contrasts made the coupled dilution/loading interpretation much more convincing than the small archival-anchor contrast alone. They did not independently manipulate pH and loading, and they changed instrument between the before and after observations.\n\n**Batches 11 and 12 checked endpoints and exposed limitations.** Batch 11's final endpoint closely matched batch 2 despite a different route. Batch 12's final pH and fraction approximately matched batch 9's endpoint after a longer hold, but its precipitation proxy was 0.04372 rather than 0.02551. That discrepancy is an important reason K1 did not claim exact endpoint reproducibility or absence of history effects.\n\nSeveral choices were exploratory rather than consequences of an established law. The broad loading series was motivated by the research goal and the possibility of precipitation saturation. The low-loading staged contrasts became attractive after the earlier observations showed a plateau. The longer hold in batch 12 was a limited probe of whether preparation history or relaxation might matter, not a rigorous kinetic experiment.\n\nA design-process weakness also deserves acknowledgment. The opening experiment note described broad categories rather than fixing a complete numerical matrix and quantitative decision rules. The later batches were selected adaptively within those categories. That makes this an exploratory campaign; it should not be retrospectively presented as a fully specified confirmatory design. Likewise, K1's later noise comparisons were useful analysis, but they were not all predefined pass/failure thresholds.\n\n## 3. Most important competing explanations\n\nK1's central distinction remains the right one: the public pH\u2013fraction relationship is better identified than the mechanism mapping nominal loading to that relationship.\n\n**A solubility-limited active pool** would explain why increasing nominal loading produces less-than-proportional effective acidity while precipitation rises. **Activity or matrix effects** could produce a similar divergence without a literal cap on dissolved acid. **A bounded synthetic state transformation** could generate all three public channels from a common internal variable without representing an ordinary physical solubility equilibrium. These explanations are still observationally close.\n\nThe campaign does distinguish them collectively from a particularly simple model: an ideal, unbuffered monoprotic acid whose analytical concentration is always exactly n/V. K1's derived C_equiv = 10^(\u2212pH)/alpha was approximately 0.00162 mol/L in batch 7 despite a nominal concentration of 2.222 mol/L. Under the assumptions of that diagnostic, the simple nominal-concentration model fails dramatically. But C_equiv is not a dissolved-concentration measurement, so it does not choose between solubility, activity, and synthetic bounding.\n\n**Endpoint control versus strong path dependence** is partly distinguishable. Batches 2 and 11 and batches 4 and 10 argue against large endpoint differences between the particular routes tested after conditioning. They do not settle immediate-termination behavior, which matters for the blind queries. Nor do they exclude smaller precipitation-history effects suggested by batches 9 and 12.\n\n**Direct pH control of precipitation versus a shared loading driver** is not identified. Loading and dilution changed pH, fraction, and precipitation together. There was no independent pH intervention at fixed loading. It would be incorrect to interpret the observed correlation as proof that pH causally controls the proxy.\n\n**Instrument compression versus state compression** also remains unresolved. The plateau is not simple clipping at the public zero-to-one bounds, since the observed values are interior. However, both instruments may share a synthetic latent-state mapping. Agreement between their processed channels is therefore not necessarily independent physical validation.\n\nFinally, small equilibrium_residual values and the environment's equilibrium_confidence output cannot resolve these competing mechanisms. K1 properly treated them as environment diagnostics, not as an independent species-balance proof or my own probability of correctness.\n\n## 4. One additional experiment I would choose, hypothetically\n\nIf one additional complete batch were authorized, I would prioritize the **unresolved effect of conditioning before assay**, rather than another plateau point. Every blind query omitted the campaign's temperature-control operations, yet nearly all mechanistic comparisons were based on conditioned preparations.\n\nI would independently prepare 0.00001 mol reagent in 0.054 L water, explicitly terminate immediately after the additions, and perform the required final assay. I would omit the intermediate measurement and any heat or wait operation. This is a legal complete sequence and uses the nominal endpoint of batches 9 and 12, in a responsive region where a protocol effect should be easier to detect than on the plateau.\n\nThe principal readings would be normalized pH, dissociation fraction, and precipitation proxy. I would retain residual and confidence diagnostics as diagnostics only. The point of this experiment would be to test the whole immediate-closeout protocol against the existing conditioned endpoints, not to pretend that it isolates time alone.\n\nPossible interpretations would be:\n\n- If pH and fraction agreed with the existing endpoints near q = 0.321 and alpha = 0.24, that would strengthen the practical endpoint-control assumption used in the blind predictions. One match would not establish universality.\n- If pH and fraction shifted together while retaining effective pKa near 5.02, the acid-base relation would remain useful, but the mapping from nominal preparation to final state would require a protocol or relaxation term.\n- If the shift broke the pH\u2013fraction relation beyond plausible measurement variation, K1's apparent common effective pKa might depend on conditioning or channel behavior more strongly than recognized.\n- If only precipitation shifted materially, the evidence would favor a distinct precipitation-relaxation, history, or proxy-calibration issue rather than a general failure of the acid-base description.\n\nThe comparison would still confound the absence of heating, a slightly different temperature, the absence of intermediate sampling, and the shorter sequence. A single batch cannot separate those components or calibrate reproducibility. Nevertheless, it would directly address a weakness common to all 12 blind predictions. I have not executed this experiment.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe stated goal was characterization rather than yield or score optimization, and that materially affected the campaign.\n\nI spent experiments on matched concentrations, opposite staged interventions, plateau mapping, and repeats. Several of these were redundant from a scalar-score perspective but useful for rejecting simplistic explanations. I also committed batch 9 for its informative within-batch dilution contrast rather than for a demonstrated maximum score. That choice was explicitly described at commitment as supporting mechanism discrimination and replay.\n\nThe scoring contract assigned substantial positive weight to equilibrium_confidence. Batch 5 had a much higher value of that diagnostic than batch 9, as well as substantially greater dissociation. Under the published weighted scalar, it would have been a stronger score-oriented candidate than the selected evidentiary anchor. That is not a reason to reinterpret confidence as scientific certainty. It illustrates why the research objective and the operational scalar were not interchangeable.\n\nThere is no evidence that I deliberately sacrificed identifiability to chase a favorable score. In particular, I did not replace unfavorable outcomes, discard batches, or choose the final recommendation by the largest observed diagnostic. But the converse claim\u2014that the design maximized identifiability\u2014would also be unjustified.\n\nImportant inefficiencies were:\n\n- Three high-loading batches established a plateau but could not identify its cause.\n- The matched-concentration pair was placed in a region with low sensitivity.\n- Every batch used one intermediate measurement, even where it largely duplicated a final endpoint and introduced a cross-instrument comparison.\n- There was no immediate-termination control, despite the importance of separating equilibrium-state relationships from operation-history effects.\n- The archival anchor was tested at an effect size poorly resolved by the fraction channel.\n\nThose are limitations of experimental allocation, not evidence of score-seeking. The selected batch also incurred extra operations and measurements to obtain a mechanistic contrast; a minimal production recipe would not necessarily need them. Thus some operational efficiency was intentionally traded for information, but the information gained was still incomplete.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weak blind predictions\n\n**The rich signal packets were not fully used.** The campaign returned final-assay artifacts and intermediate non-spectral information. I relied chiefly on processed estimates. For example, batch 1's intermediate packet included a reported pH of 3.950 and replicate values of 3.958 and 3.968. I did not conduct a systematic raw-versus-processed calibration analysis across all batches. The final packets also exposed spectral peaks, but anonymous species mapping and the restricted observation contract made it unsafe to interpret those peaks directly as acid or solid concentrations. Their existence does not justify inventing an unperformed species analysis.\n\n**Paired intermediate/final discrepancies deserved a more formal assessment.** Some high-loading intermediate fractions were below their final values; some pH comparisons shifted in the opposite direction. These could reflect noise, systematic channel differences, conditioning, or termination effects. K1 acknowledged the confounding but did not estimate a paired instrument/protocol offset or test whether a common offset was plausible.\n\n**Operational telemetry was available but not fully consolidated into an analysis dataset.** I used nominal preparation conditions and acknowledged sample removal. I did not reconstruct every pre-assay active volume, temperature, and operation-history detail into an exact state-based fit. This limits the precision of concentration-based claims, especially for small-volume extrapolations.\n\n**Model alternatives were described rather than quantitatively compared.** K1 intentionally avoided fitting a unique saturation law. That restraint was appropriate, but the blind predictions nevertheless required numerical interpolation and extrapolation. I did not construct an explicit ensemble of solubility-cap, smooth-saturation, activity, and protocol-dependent models and propagate their disagreement. Consequently, the interval widths were judgment-based rather than calibrated by model comparison or held-out coverage.\n\nThe least reliable blind predictions are:\n\n- **Q10:** Its nominal concentration is 6.667 mol/L, three times the campaign maximum, at an untested 0.006 L volume. Its point estimates extend the plateau. K1 explicitly said that continuation above the tested maximum was an extrapolation. The sealed precipitation interval of 0.132\u20130.190 and normalized-pH interval of 0.270\u20130.289 may be too narrow if the bounding mechanism changes rather than merely fluctuates.\n- **Q08:** It extrapolates below the lowest tested concentration and above the tested volume range, with only batch 5 as a nearby trace-loading anchor. Its fraction prediction of 0.588 with interval 0.542\u20130.634 may underrepresent alternative low-loading asymptotes, background effects, and conditioning dependence. K1 expressly warned that more dilute behavior was not established.\n- **Q04 and Q09:** Their concentrations have useful nearby analogues, but their 0.006 L volume is one-third of the smallest tested preparation. Concentration equivalence was supported mainly on the plateau, not rigorously at these lower-load conditions. The intervals may understate an independent volume or protocol effect.\n- **Q05\u2013Q07:** Identical marginal point predictions are a defensible consequence of the working endpoint model, but the campaign did not test these exact dosing orders followed by immediate termination. The common fraction interval of 0.056\u20130.088 and normalized-pH interval of 0.273\u20130.285 may not adequately cover a systematic order or short-time effect.\n\nQ01 and Q02 are relatively well anchored in concentration, but their equality also assumes that amount and volume have no important independent effect. Q11 and Q12 are better concentration interpolations than Q10, yet all queries share the untested omission of conditioning.\n\nThis is not a logical contradiction with K1: the prediction rationale did identify extrapolation and protocol uncertainty. There is, however, a possible mismatch in degree. K1's uncertainty about the origin of the plateau and immediate-state behavior was broad, whereas the numerical intervals assigned fairly concentrated probability to the working model. Without prediction truth, I cannot say which intervals failed. I can say their nominal 80% label was not empirically validated and may have been overconfident about structural uncertainty. I do not revise any sealed numbers here.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how it could be tested\n\nThe sealed recommendation was completed batch 9. Its rationale was that low-loading staged dilution produced an informative, jointly resolved response away from the plateau, with partial endpoint checks from batches 10 and 12. It was an evidentiary anchor, not a demonstrated optimal operation.\n\nIts limitations are concrete:\n\n- The before/after contrast used different instruments.\n- Dilution was accompanied by another temperature-control operation and eventual termination.\n- Intermediate sampling slightly changed the material state before dilution.\n- The procedure was performed only once as that exact sequence.\n- Batch 12 was an endpoint analogue, not an exact replay, and its precipitation signal differed from batch 9 by approximately 0.0182.\n- No independently measured dissolved/solid mass balance established what the precipitation change physically represented.\n\n**Repeatability** would require independent exact replays of batch 9, preserving addition order, volumes, amount, temperature-control operations, stirring, measurement sequence, sampling, and termination. All outcomes should be retained. The relevant endpoints would include both individual readings and the paired dilution changes. A single replay would be informative but insufficient to estimate reliable coverage or a failure rate.\n\n**Local robustness** would require a prospectively fixed neighborhood around the recipe: modest amount and volume perturbations, changes in dilution ratio, hold duration, and temperature or stirring where legally available. The question should be whether the directional response and effective pKa relationship persist, not whether one can selectively find a more favorable final reading. Same-instrument or carefully balanced measurement designs would be needed to separate chemical response from measurement-protocol effects.\n\n**Cross-material transfer** is unestablished. The campaign used only water and the anonymous reagent, without catalysts. Other selectable solvent or catalyst categories would constitute new conditions, and their benchmark effects cannot be inferred from familiar real chemical names. Any transfer claim would need a new, explicitly scoped comparison rather than relabeling the aqueous result as general chemistry.\n\n**Cross-world transfer** is even less established. A different world could change the effective pKa, loading transformation, precipitation mapping, or instrument relationship. Testing transfer would require independently evaluated worlds and frozen predictions or qualification criteria, keeping calibration data separate from evaluation data. Similar-looking public channels alone would not demonstrate shared microscopic mechanisms.\n\nFinally, 'sample-in highest' and 'proven optimal' are fundamentally different claims. Batch 9 was not presented as the sample-in scalar maximum, and an informative contrast is not itself an optimization objective. Even the highest observed scalar among the 12 batches would establish only an observed winner under those measurements. It would not prove global optimality over legal recipes, robustness to repetition, or superiority in another world. No such optimality proof was obtained or claimed."
}

Uncertainty and limitations\n\nThe declared final-assay noise scales were 0.002 in normalized pH, equivalent to 0.028 pH units; 0.006 in alpha; and 0.006 in P. Corresponding pH-meter channel scales were 0.004 in normalized pH, equivalent to 0.056 pH units; 0.015 in alpha; and 0.020 in P. These are useful reference scales, not complete confidence intervals incorporating all process effects.\n\nThe fall in alpha between batches 1 and 3, approximately 0.0347, is substantially larger than the final-assay channel noise scale. The corresponding pH decline, approximately 0.120, is also appreciable. In contrast, most plateau differences are comparable to expected measurement variation. The low-to-plateau increase in P is weaker evidence and deserves caution.\n\nIntermediate and final readings sometimes differed noticeably even without an intended chemical intervention. In batch 6, normalized pH changed from 0.28516 in the intermediate measurement to 0.29318 in the final assay, a difference equivalent to approximately 0.112 pH units. In batch 9, alpha changed from 0.04346 to 0.07207. These observations were retained. They caution against treating small individual fluctuations as mechanistic effects or assuming cross-instrument identity.\n\nImportant remaining limitations are:\n\n- only one low-loading batch at each concentration;\n- only one direct repeated preparation pair;\n- no independent measurement of dissolved acid concentration or acid identity;\n- no ionic-strength, counterion, or activity-coefficient characterization;\n- no direct solid detection, solid mass, or solubility measurement;\n- no blank or zero-reagent chemical measurement;\n- no systematic temperature, solvent, catalyst, or time intervention;\n- no independent variation of pH at fixed loading;\n- unknown dependence among synthetic output channels;\n- matched-concentration and history controls concentrated in the plateau, where important dependencies can be hidden.\n\nThe run's terminal response also caused a client-side parsing error because it lacked a next-state object. Public status subsequently confirmed that batch 12's final assay succeeded and all 12 batches were complete. No assay was rerun or replaced as a result.\n\n## 8. Competing explanations\n\n### A. Saturating availability of an acid-active component\n\nOnly a bounded fraction or pool of the anonymous reagent participates in the acid equilibrium. Increasing total loading initially changes that pool, then has little additional effect. This naturally explains the plateau and allows a common effective pKa. The limitation is that the active pool was not directly measured.\n\n### B. Buffering or background electrolyte control\n\nA background buffer or other charge-bearing formulation maintains pH near 4.1. Alpha then follows the approximately constant pH. The nominal reagent may not itself account for most acid equivalents. This is compatible with the failure of the simple [H+] = alpha C_nom balance, but background species and buffer capacity are unidentified.\n\n### C. Solubility-limited dissolved concentration\n\nAbove a loading threshold, dissolved acid-active material remains approximately constant while additional reagent resides in an unobserved phase. This could explain a dissolved-state plateau. However, the nearly flat P signal provides no direct mass-balance support for accumulating solids. This explanation requires a nonlinear or non-mass interpretation of P, or another unobserved mechanism.\n\n### D. Bounded observation or internal state mapping\n\nThe synthetic environment may compress or cap the mapping from nominal loading to chemical outputs. This would reproduce plateau behavior without a literal buffer or solid phase. The measured channels are far from their visible bounds of zero and one, so simple clipping at those reported bounds is not the explanation. An upstream bounded mapping remains possible.\n\n### E. Preparation-dependent or incompletely relaxed states\n\nThe observations may reflect reproducible short-time states rather than fully relaxed equilibria. Similar history-control endpoints make a large persistent effect less evident in the tested region, but the absence of time-dependent measurements prevents excluding this alternative.\n\nThese explanations are not mutually exclusive. A buffered acid-active pool with a bounded precipitation proxy would combine several of them. The existing evidence does not justify selecting a unique hidden implementation.\n\n## 9. Supported scope and untested implications\n\nThe supported empirical scope is water-based preparations with 0.00010\u20130.040 mol added reagent, nominal final volumes of 0.020\u20130.050 L, and nominal final concentrations of 0.002\u20131.6 mol/L. Batch 10 also supplied one intermediate observation at 0.0125 L. These intervals are the union of tested conditions, not a fully crossed coverage region. They should not be interpreted as validation of every combination within them.\n\nWithin the observed higher-loading region, a useful provisional description is a nearly constant pH\u2013alpha\u2013P triplet, with pKa_eff near 5.2 as a compatible speciation summary. At the lowest loading, higher pH and alpha are supported. The detailed interpolation between those regimes is uncertain.\n\nUntested implications distinguish the candidate explanations:\n\n- Dilution well below 0.002 mol/L could reveal whether there is a background pH floor, continued weak-acid dilution behavior, or a separate low-concentration regime.\n- Independent pH perturbation at fixed loading could test whether alpha follows the proposed acid-speciation equation.\n- Direct dissolved-concentration and solid measurements could distinguish a solubility ceiling from buffering or an upstream bounded mapping.\n- Matched-concentration scale controls outside the plateau could distinguish concentration dependence from separate amount and volume effects.\n- Time-dependent observations could distinguish immediate state updates from relaxation or hysteresis.\n\nThese are proposed discriminating observations, not experiments performed in this campaign and not claims of their outcomes.\n\n## 10. Conclusion\n\nThe campaign supports a limited-response aqueous system in which nominal reagent loading produces a modest low-loading change followed by broad saturation of the observable chemical state. An effective weak-acid speciation relation with pKa around 5.2 is compatible with the pH and dissociation measurements, but nominal reagent concentration cannot be equated with the concentration of an ideal unbuffered acid pool. The precipitation channel is approximately constant over the plateau and cannot be converted into a solid amount or solubility law.\n\nThe scale, preparation-history, and repeat controls support local reproducibility and no large resolved endpoint dependence beyond loading in the plateau region. They do not uniquely identify the mechanism. The defensible result is therefore an empirical plateau plus a provisional acid-speciation interpretation, with buffering, limited dissolved availability, and bounded state or observation mappings remaining reasonable competing explanations."
}
