### K2

```json
{
  "report": "# Retrospective assessment\n\nI refer to the sealed mechanistic report as K1 and to the sealed blind predictions by their query identifiers. No prediction truth has been supplied. This assessment uses only the completed campaign and identifies shortcomings without changing K1, the predictions, or the committed recommendation. Proposed experiments below are hypothetical, not executed.\n\n## 1. What the initial information did\u2014and did not\u2014establish\n\n### There was no substantive numerical prior mechanism to validate\n\nThe initial brief described a bounded aqueous acid-dissociation and precipitation task, but the material-information response explicitly supplied no task-specific nominal property dossier. Its status was \u201copaque_identifiers_only,\u201d and there was no archival local relationship. K1 correctly stated: \u201cthere was no numerical prior law to confirm or revise.\u201d It would therefore be misleading to describe the campaign as having validated or overturned a supplied acid constant, precipitation threshold, or concentration law.\n\nThe initial information did establish measurement semantics: pH_normalized is pH/14, acid dissociation is a fraction, and precipitation_signal is a bounded normalized proxy. Those were contractual definitions, not experimentally verified mechanistic propositions. Likewise, equilibrium_confidence was an environment diagnostic, not my uncertainty or the scientific objective.\n\n### What was supported\n\nThe observed response was compatible with the broad characterization of a bounded weak-acid-related system. Between batches 1 and 3, nominal concentration increased from 0.002 to 0.050 mol/L, final pH decreased from 4.2036 to 4.0835, and dissociation fraction decreased from 0.10357 to 0.06888. That direction is consistent with acid speciation responding to increased proton activity.\n\nHowever, this only supports compatibility with an acid-related response. It does not independently verify the anonymous reagent's identity, establish that it is a monoprotic acid, or validate the interpretation of all added material as dissolved acid.\n\n### What encountered actual counterevidence\n\nAn unrestricted ideal weak-acid interpretation based on all nominal reagent did encounter counterevidence. K1 explicitly rejected the combined assumptions underlying [H+] approximately equal to alpha times C_nom. For batch 5, alpha times C_nom was approximately 0.0157 mol/L, whereas the measured pH implied hydrogen activity of approximately 9.2 \u00d7 10^-5 on the conventional molar reference scale. The high-loading plateau supplied a second reason to reject unrestricted nominal-concentration dependence.\n\nThis was my candidate interpretation to assess, not a numerical claim supplied by the initial materials. The distinction matters.\n\nThere was also an observable change in my contemporaneous interpretation: after eight batches I stated that the results did not support an unrestricted textbook weak-acid model based solely on added reagent divided by volume. Thus, this was not a case where decisive counterevidence was observed but the same unrestricted model was knowingly retained throughout.\n\n### What merely lacked detected counterevidence\n\nPreparation-history independence and concentration-based scale equivalence fall into a weaker category. Batches 5 and 9, and the near-0.20 mol/L endpoints of batches 10 and 11, did not resolve a large effect. But those tests were performed on a plateau, where distinct mechanisms can give the same answer. K1 appropriately warned that saturation could conceal separate amount and volume dependencies.\n\nConsequently, \u201cno resolved history effect\u201d must not become \u201chistory independence established.\u201d The same applies to volume effects. I did not obtain evidence that either relationship holds outside the tested plateau.\n\nSolvent identity effects, catalyst effects, temperature dependence, kinetic relaxation, actual solid formation, and generalization to another world were untested. The absence of contradictions in these areas says nothing favorable about their validity.\n\n## 2. Which experiments changed the interpretation, and what drove the choices\n\n### Experiments that materially shaped the interpretation\n\n**Batches 1\u20133 established the low-loading contrast.** Their declining pH and dissociation fraction motivated a dilution/speciation interpretation rather than a completely constant response. The precipitation signal increased from 0.14014 to 0.15775, but this was weaker evidence because it was a small change without low-loading replication.\n\n**Batches 4\u20138 established the broad plateau.** Extending nominal concentration through 0.10, 0.20, 0.40, 0.80, and 1.60 mol/L did not continue the low-loading trend. For example, batch 5 had pH 4.0380, but batches 6 and 7 had higher values, 4.1045 and 4.1100. That pattern made a monotonic, unrestricted concentration model untenable at the observed precision. These batches genuinely changed the working interpretation.\n\n**Batches 7 and 8 strengthened the high-loading insensitivity result.** They used the same 0.040 mol reagent amount while halving volume from 0.050 to 0.025 L. Their final dissociation fractions, 0.06936 and 0.07062, were nearly identical. This was informative about a plateau, but much less informative about whether amount or concentration is the causal input.\n\n**Batch 9 tested scale, but only locally.** At the same nominal 0.20 mol/L concentration as batch 5, reducing both amount and volume by a factor of 2.5 produced similar endpoints. It supported using a common local response, but did not establish a universal n/V law.\n\n**Batches 10 and 11 tested preparation history.** Their endpoints were broadly compatible with direct preparations near 0.20 mol/L. They reduced concern about a large endpoint history effect within that region. They did not strongly challenge hysteresis, because their starting and ending conditions remained in the plateau.\n\n**Batch 12 supplied the direct repeat.** Its differences from batch 5\u2014approximately 0.0130 pH units, 0.00374 in dissociation fraction, and 0.00128 in precipitation signal\u2014supported local repeatability. One repeat pair was insufficient to characterize the full variance or validate the predictive intervals.\n\n### What was based on prior information or assumptions\n\nUsing water, prioritizing pH-related observables, and performing final assays followed directly from the research brief and instrument contracts. Avoiding catalysts and alternative solvents reduced confounding, but it also restricted the scientific scope.\n\nThe loading sweep was a defensible general identification strategy rather than a consequence of an established mechanism. The specific concentration range and spacing were design judgments. I had no validated reason to believe that 0.002 mol/L would reach the informative dilute regime, and it did not expose the low-loading behavior later required by several blind queries.\n\nThe scale and history controls were announced before the plateau was known. That preserved the stated design, but it meant their final locations were not optimized for discrimination after the first eight batches. I should not retrospectively portray those controls as deliberately chosen to exploit a newly inferred plateau mechanism.\n\nFinally, the pKa near 5.2 and the limited acid-active-pool formulation were developed in the post-campaign K1 analysis. They were not numerical models that guided the initial experimental choices. The contemporaneous conclusion was narrower: the nominal-concentration textbook model was inadequate.\n\n## 3. The most important competing explanations\n\nThe central unresolved issue is **where the compression of the response occurs**.\n\n### Limited acid-active availability\n\nNominal loading may feed a smaller active pool that approaches a ceiling. Acid speciation within that pool could follow the effective relationship in K1:\n\nalpha = 1 / (1 + 10^(pKa_eff \u2212 pH)).\n\nThis can account for the pH\u2013dissociation pairing without equating nominal reagent concentration with dissolved acid concentration. But the active pool was never independently measured.\n\n### Background buffering or charge-bearing components\n\nA background formulation could maintain a relatively stable pH, with the dissociation fraction responding to that pH. This also explains why alpha times C_nom fails to reproduce hydrogen activity. Existing observations do not identify buffer capacity, counterions, or background acid equivalents.\n\n### Solubility limitation\n\nDissolved material could saturate while added material enters an unobserved phase. This explanation remains possible, but K1 correctly noted that the nearly flat precipitation proxy does not establish accumulating solid mass. It would require either a nonlinear proxy or an unmeasured solid-state contribution.\n\n### Bounded internal or observation mapping\n\nThe environment might compress the transformation from loading to chemical state or from chemical state to reported channels. The reported values are not close to zero or one, so simple clipping at the visible channel limits is not the obvious explanation. An upstream cap or nonlinear mapping remains observationally plausible.\n\n### Short-time or preparation-dependent states\n\nBecause there was no deliberate waiting-time experiment, apparently stable endpoints might be reproducible short-time states rather than demonstrated thermodynamic equilibria. Small equilibrium_residual values do not independently settle that issue.\n\n### What the existing data distinguish\n\nThe campaign distinguishes the observed bounded response from the unrestricted ideal model in which all nominal reagent directly participates as an unbuffered acid. It also disfavors large scale or persistent preparation-history effects at the tested plateau conditions.\n\nIt does not distinguish buffering from limited active availability, establish precipitation as the cause of saturation, or determine whether a common synthetic mapping accounts for the apparent pH\u2013dissociation relationship. K1's statement that the campaign \u201cidentifies the effective response much better than its molecular implementation\u201d remains the appropriate summary.\n\n## 4. One additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nIf authorized to add exactly one complete experiment with sufficient resources, I would choose a **within-batch dilution across the untested low-loading boundary**, rather than another plateau replicate.\n\nA concrete legal sequence would be:\n\n1. Add 0.006 L water.\n2. Add 0.000012 mol reagent, giving nominal 0.002 mol/L, matching batch 1's concentration at a smaller scale.\n3. Measure with the pH meter.\n4. Add 0.074 L water, giving approximately 0.080 L total added water and a final nominal concentration near 0.00015 mol/L, with a small correction for the intermediate sample.\n5. Explicitly terminate.\n6. Perform the final assay.\n\nThis uses the same public operations and one intermediate measurement plus one final assay. It is proposed only; it was not executed, and the actual completed campaign's resources are exhausted.\n\n### Why this experiment\n\nThe intermediate observation checks whether batch 1's low-loading state transfers to a much smaller amount and volume. The final observation then crosses substantially below the lowest studied concentration. This addresses both the concentration-versus-scale ambiguity and the most consequential extrapolation gap in the blind predictions.\n\n### How outcomes would change my judgment\n\n- **Intermediate agreement with batch 1, followed by substantially increased final pH and dissociation:** this would favor continuing dilution sensitivity below the studied range and weaken a strong persistent-background-floor explanation. It would support the direction, though not necessarily the magnitude, of Q03 and Q09.\n- **Intermediate agreement, followed by little final change:** this would favor a background-controlled low-loading floor or a bounded mapping that remains insensitive below 0.002 mol/L. It would undermine the central trace-loading extrapolations.\n- **Intermediate disagreement with batch 1 beyond plausible measurement variation:** this would challenge concentration-only transfer at small scale before interpreting the dilution endpoint. Absolute amount, volume, sampling, or preparation effects would become more important.\n- **A large change in precipitation signal without a corresponding acid-speciation change:** this would argue against treating all three channels as expressions of a single latent acid-active pool.\n- **A pH\u2013dissociation pair inconsistent with the provisional effective pKa relationship:** this would weaken the common-speciation interpretation itself, rather than merely revise a loading-to-state mapping.\n\nOne such experiment would still not identify a solid phase or a kinetic law. Its purpose would be to eliminate a major ambiguity, not to solve every remaining question.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe campaign was designed around characterization rather than score maximization. The broad loading sweep deliberately included conditions with relatively low diagnostic values. For example, equilibrium_confidence was approximately 0.374 in batch 8, versus approximately 0.533 in batch 5. Continuing into that high-loading region was scientifically useful because it tested whether the chemical response continued changing.\n\nThere was no observed sequence of score-driven recipe optimization. The recommendation of batch 12 was justified as a repeat and comparison anchor, not as a selected maximum. I also did not manipulate catalyst, temperature, or other variables to increase the native score.\n\nNevertheless, the design did sacrifice some identifiability\u2014not for score, but for simplicity, planned controls, and repeatability. Four of the final batches concentrated on approximately 0.20 mol/L, where the response was already flat. These comparisons helped assess reproducibility and endpoint consistency but could not strongly separate the competing mechanisms. The same resources might have been more discriminating at lower concentrations, with a blank-like condition, or with a time intervention.\n\nThe fact that those repeated endpoints also had higher equilibrium_confidence is not evidence that they were chosen to optimize it: the controls were announced before those values were observed. Still, it created an incidental concentration of evidence around a diagnostically favorable region.\n\nThe research objective also justified avoiding yield-oriented instruments or chemistry changes without a clear identification role. The UV\u2013visible observation contract did not provide the target equilibrium channels, so spending scarce intermediate measurements on it had an uncertain return. That choice was reasonable, but it left no independent compositional measurement to challenge the shared-channel interpretation.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses in the blind predictions\n\n### Evidence that was not fully exploited\n\n**Intermediate measurements were mostly treated as supplementary checks.** Their discrepancies with final assays could have been analyzed more systematically. For example, batch 6's normalized pH difference corresponds to approximately 0.112 pH units, and batch 9's dissociation fraction changed from 0.04346 to 0.07207. These differences were retained in K1, but I did not fit or estimate a cross-instrument discrepancy model. With only one intermediate and one final measurement per batch, separating bias, random noise, and chemical change would still have been difficult.\n\n**The batch-1 raw pH packet contained more information than a single processed channel.** It reported pH 4.249 and replicate readings of 4.261 and 4.224. K1 distinguished that from the final-assay-derived pH, but the campaign did not systematically preserve and analyze equivalent raw replicate information across all intermediate measurements in the compact working record. I should not now claim that a comprehensive replicate-level analysis was performed.\n\n**Sampling bookkeeping was acknowledged but not fully modeled.** For the history controls, nominal final concentration was used with a caveat about removed material. The corrections were small, but an exact public mass-and-volume reconstruction would have made the comparisons cleaner.\n\n**The residual and confidence diagnostics were described rather than mechanistically explained.** The relatively high residual of batch 10, 0.00995, deserved attention, but it was a single value on a noisy bounded channel. It could not support a history-effect claim by itself. The loading dependence of equilibrium_confidence was evident but could not legitimately be substituted for chemical mechanism or epistemic confidence.\n\n### Least reliable predictions\n\n**Q08 is the least reliable chemical extrapolation.** Its nominal concentration, 0.00001333 mol/L, lies 150-fold below the lowest studied condition. Its central dissociation prediction of 0.5, normalized pH of 0.3715, and precipitation prediction of 0.075 relied on continued dilution sensitivity that K1 had explicitly left unidentified.\n\n**Q03 and Q09 are also weakly constrained.** Their central dissociation estimates of 0.25 and 0.235 were not obtained from a fitted, validated loading law. They were judgmental extrapolations consistent with one of the candidate interpretations. Their wide intervals acknowledged this, but the point estimates can appear more mechanistically grounded than they really were.\n\n**Q10 extrapolates the plateau to 6.667 mol/L.** That is over four times the highest studied nominal concentration and uses only 0.006 L water. Continuing the plateau is a reasonable default, not a demonstrated result. A regime change remains possible.\n\n**Q04 is near the observed concentration boundary but far outside the tested absolute scale.** Treating its 0.001667 mol/L concentration as nearly equivalent to batch 1 assumes that amount and volume remain secondary at very small scale. The only matched-concentration scale test was on the plateau.\n\n### Intervals that may be too narrow\n\nThe trace-loading intervals were broad, but breadth alone does not establish 80% calibration. For Q08, even the dissociation interval of 0.09\u20130.90 could miss a near-complete-dissociation regime or another unanticipated mapping. The pH interval likewise depended on an unvalidated set of plausible mechanisms.\n\nQ10's precipitation interval of 0.131\u20130.183 and normalized pH interval of 0.282\u20130.302 may be too narrow if an unobserved high-loading transition occurs. They mostly represent a widened plateau model rather than a fully weighted set of alternative regimes.\n\nQ01 and Q02 received identical central estimates and relatively narrow intervals despite K1's warning that scale equivalence was tested mainly on the plateau. Their prediction is defensible as an interpolation assumption, but the uncertainty about separate amount and volume effects may not have been fully represented.\n\nEven Q12, the strongest interior prediction, has intervals that are only judgmentally calibrated. Its dissociation interval of 0.061\u20130.081 and normalized pH interval of 0.2878\u20130.2958 are supported by instrument noise scales and plateau variation, but a single repeat pair does not validate their nominal coverage.\n\n### Consistency with K1\n\nThe blind-prediction rationale explicitly labeled trace extrapolation as uncertain and distinguished predictive intervals from confidence intervals. That was consistent with K1 in wording. The greater concern is quantitative: assigning specific low-loading centers and finite interval widths gave operational precision to mechanisms that K1 said were not identified. That is a limitation to acknowledge, not a reason to retroactively alter the sealed values.\n\nThere is also a deliverable limitation: the sealed prediction response supplied numerical predictions for pH_normalized, acid_dissociation_fraction, and precipitation_signal only. It did not provide predictions for equilibrium_residual or equilibrium_confidence, although those are also public final-assay observables. If the request intended every equilibrium channel under the contract, that response was incomplete. This is a reporting omission, independent of any unknown prediction truth.\n\n## 7. Limits of the committed recommendation and how it could be qualified\n\nThe committed recommendation was batch 12: 0.010 mol reagent in 0.050 L water, with its intermediate pH measurement and final assay. Its purpose was to serve as an evidentiary anchor for the direct repeat, scale control, and history comparisons.\n\n### It is not a demonstrated optimum\n\nK1 and the recommendation rationale did not claim optimization. Batch 12 was not selected through a statistically validated search over an objective, and the campaign did not establish any globally optimal condition.\n\n\u201cSample-internal highest\u201d would mean only the largest observed value of a specified metric among the tested noisy outcomes. Even that description would require identifying the metric and comparing all outcomes. It would not establish that the selected condition has the highest true expected value, much less that no untested condition is better. For example, batch 5's equilibrium_confidence was slightly higher than batch 12's; this alone neither establishes superiority nor changes the scientific recommendation.\n\n### Repeatability qualification\n\nThe existing evidence is one direct repeat pair, batches 5 and 12. A proper repeatability study would use multiple independent preparations at the committed condition, retain all results, and estimate between-batch variation separately from within-assay variation where the instrument permits. It should also distinguish reproducing the exact committed sequence from reproducing a recipe without the intermediate destructive measurement.\n\n### Local robustness qualification\n\nSmall, preplanned perturbations of reagent amount and water volume would test whether the apparent plateau is robust rather than an artifact of sparse sampling. A crossed design would be preferable to changing only concentration: it would independently vary amount and volume, include matched-concentration pairs, and test a small number of preparation histories. Waiting-time and temperature perturbations would address the currently untested assumption that the endpoint does not depend materially on relaxation or thermal conditions.\n\nAny such study should have fixed coverage and evaluation rules before execution. It should not replace unfavorable runs or selectively retain the most stable measurements.\n\n### Cross-material scope\n\nOnly water and the anonymous limiting reagent were studied. There is no basis for transferring the recommended condition to ethanol, acetonitrile, toluene, or catalyst-containing preparations. The material catalog described categorical benchmark effects and did not authorize inference from real solvent names to real chemical behavior. Each material change would need its own controlled comparison and measurement interpretation.\n\n### Cross-world scope\n\nThe campaign characterized one bounded synthetic world. Generalization to another world would require independent qualification across worlds with explicitly defined coverage and uncertainty accounting. A common measurement contract does not imply common parameter values or a common hidden mechanism. There is no empirical basis here for transfer to real aqueous samples.\n\nThe defensible recommendation is therefore narrow: batch 12 is a reproducible-looking, locally well-connected reference preparation within this campaign. It is neither a proven chemical optimum nor a validated universal operating condition."
}

Effective acid-dissociation relationship\n\nLet q denote the measured normalized pH and alpha the measured dissociation fraction. Define:\n\npH = 14q.\n\nFor an effective monoprotic acid, a useful candidate relation is:\n\nalpha/(1 \u2212 alpha) = 10^(pH \u2212 pKa_eff),\n\nor equivalently:\n\npKa_eff = pH \u2212 log10[alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)].\n\nThis relation is an interpretation of the public channels. It assumes that their dissociation fraction and pH can be related by an effective Henderson\u2013Hasselbalch expression; it does not identify a chemical species or prove ideal activities.\n\nApplying this expression separately to the 12 final assays gives the following apparent pKa values, in batch order:\n\n5.1451, 5.2090, 5.1446, 5.2423, 5.2262, 5.2160, 5.0910, 5.2276, 5.1585, 5.1982, 5.1915, 5.2300.\n\nTheir arithmetic mean is 5.1900, and their observed range is 5.0910\u20135.2423. The former corresponds to 0.028 pH units. This is only an illustrative measurement-error scale: process variation, correlation between channels, and uncertainty in the effective model are not included. The observed spread of apparent pKa values is therefore not strong evidence by itself for batch-specific acid constants.\n\n## 5. Why an effective loading transformation is needed\n\nConsider the stronger, more restrictive model in which the supplied reagent is an ideal monoprotic acid with analytical concentration C = n/V, with hydrogen arising predominantly from that acid. Ignoring water autoionization in the acidic range, this gives:\n\nh = alpha C,\nKa = h\u00b2/(C \u2212 h),\nh = [sqrt(Ka\u00b2 + 4KaC) \u2212 Ka]/2,\npH = \u2212log10(h).\n\nUsing pKa near 5.2, this unrestricted model predicts a pH near 3.2 at the dilute archival anchor C approximately 0.0185 mol/L and near 2.7 at C = 0.600 mol/L. Observed values are instead around 4.1 at both. It gives:\n\n- Batch 4: C_eff approximately 0.000127 mol/L.\n- Batch 5: C_eff approximately 0.000511 mol/L.\n- Batch 2: C_eff approximately 0.001011 mol/L.\n- Batch 8: C_eff approximately 0.001211 mol/L.\n- Batch 12: C_eff approximately 0.001181 mol/L.\n\nAcross the ten higher-loading final states, the inferred values span roughly 0.000994\u20130.001237 mol/L. Thus, this representation converts the observed pH/dissociation plateau into an effective acid concentration of order 10^\u22123 mol/L, even when nominal supplied concentration is much larger.\n\nOne parsimonious process representation is therefore:\n\nC_eff = F(n, V, preparation history; unmeasured formulation factors),\nh = [sqrt(Ka_eff\u00b2 + 4Ka_eff C_eff) \u2212 Ka_eff]/2,\nalpha = h/C_eff,\npH_normalized = \u2212log10(h)/14,\nprecipitation_signal = G(n, V, C_eff, history),\nobserved channels = underlying response + measurement error, with the declared bounds.\n\nIn this representation, F increases through the low-loading regime and approaches approximately 10^\u22123 mol/L over the higher-loading region tested. Archival dilution claim: what was and was not tested\n\nThe supplied local claim concerned 0.001 mol acid at 298.15 K diluted from 0.018 to 0.054 L. The dissociation difference has the opposite sign, but it is not decisive at the declared noise scale. With independent final-assay errors, a difference between two measurements has approximate noise scales of 0.00283 in normalized pH and 0.00849 in dissociation. Both archival increments are smaller than those difference scales.\n\nBatch 3's staged dilution did not provide a clean resolution: its intermediate-to-final pH change had the opposite sign, and the instruments differed. It would be inappropriate to average away that discordance or describe the archival dilution law as experimentally confirmed.\n\nMy conclusion is consequently asymmetric: the effective pKa scale is supported by the joint pH/dissociation observations, but the particular small archival dilution increments remain unresolved. They are neither convincingly validated nor convincingly rejected by this campaign.\n\n## 7. Precipitation: supported coupling versus microscopic interpretation\n\nThe precipitation channel increases sharply between batches 4 and 5 and then remains near 0.15 across most of the campaign. This supports a loading-associated bounded response with a transition somewhere between the two low-loading recipes, under the tested protocol.\n\nThat statement does not imply that actual precipitation begins at a uniquely identified concentration. There was no zero-reagent blank, direct solid measurement, dissolved-species assay, or independent turbidity calibration in the observations used here. Batch 4's positive signal of 0.03113 could include baseline response, a small genuine precipitation-related contribution, or both.\n\nThe plateau value of about 0.15 is far below the channel's declared upper bound of 1. Therefore, the observed plateau is not explained simply by clipping at the public upper bound. It could reflect an internal bounded response, a chemical or formulation limit, or a proxy insensitive to further accumulated material.\n\nA dissolution or precipitation equilibrium that limits the acid-bearing dissolved state is a plausible explanation for the acid-related plateau. However, the observations do not show that the precipitating component is the acid itself. A different anonymous component or a coupled formulation response could produce the same joint channel behavior. It is also possible that precipitation and acidity are separate functions of loading rather than one causing the other.\n\n## 8. Reasonable competing explanations\n\n### A. Dissolved-acid limitation coupled to precipitation\n\nAdditional reagent initially increases dissolved acid. Beyond a threshold, a phase constraint limits the effective dissolved acid concentration, leaving pH and dissociation nearly constant. The precipitation proxy rises and then becomes insensitive to additional material.\n\nThis explains the qualitative regime structure, but it requires caution: a capped proxy is not a mass balance, and no solid phase or solubility constant was measured. The fact that the proxy reaches its plateau before the acidity channels finish changing also argues against assuming a single sharp threshold controls everything.\n\n### B. A bounded transformation of supplied loading\n\nThe benchmark may transform amount or concentration into a bounded effective acid input, then compute weak-acid-like pH and dissociation. A separate bounded function may generate the precipitation proxy.\n\nThis reproduces the observable structure without requiring literal formation of a solid. The campaign cannot distinguish such an effective mapping from a physical concentration limitation when both produce the same public outputs.\n\n### C. Buffering, background ions, or activity effects\n\nUnmeasured formulation components could constrain hydrogen activity while changing the relationship between nominal supplied reagent and the acid species used by the dissociation channel. In that case, the inferred C_eff is a convenient parameterization rather than a real analytical concentration, because h = alpha C need not hold.\n\nThe approximate Henderson\u2013Hasselbalch relationship can survive this interpretation as an effective relation. No background-ion intervention, titration, or activity measurement was performed to distinguish it from the preceding explanations.\n\n### D. Total-amount dependence rather than concentration dependence\n\nAll low-loading discrimination was obtained at the same water volume. The strongest matched-concentration scale test was performed on the plateau, where amount-based and concentration-based saturation models both predict little change. The archival fixed-amount dilution comparison was also in the plateau region and targeted a very small effect.\n\nConsequently, the observations do not establish F = F(n/V). An F that depends primarily on n, or on both n and V, remains viable. This is a material identifiability limit, not a minor technical caveat.\n\n### E. Kinetic or preparation-history effects\n\nThe common conditioning procedure and similar high-loading endpoints reduce concern about large preparation-path effects in that region. They do not determine a relaxation time. Without time series or post-addition waiting comparisons, a finite-time endpoint response could resemble an equilibrium plateau.\n\nThe reported equilibrium_residual was zero in 11 of 12 final assays and 0.0002935 in batch 5. Those values are compatible with small residual diagnostics but do not independently prove thermodynamic equilibrium. Likewise, equilibrium_confidence is an environment diagnostic, not my uncertainty estimate and not the scientific objective.\n\n## 9. Uncertainty and limits of identification\n\nThe strongest conclusions are the low-loading contrast, the higher-loading plateau, and the compatibility of pH/dissociation with an effective pKa near 5.2. The weakest are the exact dilution coefficient, the independent roles of amount and volume, the microscopic source of the plateau, and any detailed precipitation law.\n\nImportant limitations are:\n\n- Only one exact high-loading preparation was repeated; there was no exact replicate of batch 4 or batch 5.\n- Intermediate measurements were generally noisier than final assays. Their declared noise scales were 0.004 for normalized pH, 0.015 for dissociation, and 0.02 for precipitation proxy, compared with 0.002, 0.006, and 0.006 for final assays.\n- Intermediate and final measurements are not interchangeable replicates, particularly in staged batches where an intervention occurred between them.\n- There were only two low-loading points below the broad plateau, so transition curvature and thresholds are poorly resolved.\n- Sample consumption and any associated species removal prevent treating supplied n/V as an exact final-state chemical concentration.\n- Conditioning was brief and no time dependence was measured.\n- No UV\u2013visible measurements or independent species-resolved analyses were collected. Species assignments cannot be inferred from unavailable spectra or hidden mechanism mappings.\n- No catalyst, solvent, temperature, pressure, or pH-control intervention was systematically varied.\n\nMy illustrative per-channel error calculations use the declared noise scales. They are not comprehensive uncertainty intervals for mechanism parameters or predictions. The diagnostic channels do not fill these gaps.\n\n## 10. Supported range and extrapolation boundaries\n\nThe directly tested water volumes supplied were 0.018\u20130.054 L. Total supplied reagent ranged from 0.000010 to 0.032400 mol. Final nominal recipe concentrations ranged from approximately 0.000185 to 0.600 mol/L. One intermediate measurement, in batch 10, sampled nominal concentration 1.2 mol/L before dilution. It should not be described as a final-assay-validated extension of the full response law.\n\nThe interpretation applies to the anonymous reagent in water under the common near-298 K conditioning protocol and the measured acidic response range, approximately pH 4.03\u20134.61 in final assays. It is not a claim about arbitrary weak acids, other solvents, catalysts, temperatures, highly dilute water-dominated behavior, or concentrations beyond the tested region.\n\nWithin the observed high-loading region, an approximately constant response near pH 4.08, dissociation 0.074, and precipitation proxy 0.153 is an evidence-based local summary. Interpolating through the low-loading transition is less secure. Extrapolating toward zero reagent, assigning a universal solubility ceiling, or predicting temperature dependence would require assumptions not tested here.\n\n## 11. Testable consequences of the interpretation\n\nThese are consequences that distinguish explanations, not additional experiments performed in this report:\n\n1. If the effective pKa relationship persists within the tested regime, new jointly observed pH and dissociation pairs should approximately satisfy pH \u2212 log10[alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)] near 5.2, even when loading changes.\n2. If nominal concentration is the relevant input, matched-concentration scale changes in the unsaturated low-loading region should give similar responses. A strong difference there would favor amount dependence, explicit volume dependence, or scale-sensitive preparation effects.\n3. If a reversible phase constraint limits dissolved acid, sufficient dilution out of the plateau should increase dissociation and pH while lowering the precipitation-related response. The transition might nevertheless differ from that of the proxy if the latter is strongly nonlinear.\n4. If preparation history matters, otherwise matched low-loading endpoints reached by dilution and by direct preparation could differ, particularly before sufficient waiting.\n5. If the plateau is solely a bounded proxy transformation rather than a dissolved-state limit, an independent calibrated measure of dissolved species could continue changing while the existing public channels remain flat.\n\n## 12. Final assessment\n\nThe campaign supports a compact effective description rather than a uniquely identified microscopic mechanism: a weak-acid-like pH/dissociation relation with pKa around 5.2, driven by a loading-related state that saturates strongly, accompanied by a precipitation proxy that rises earlier and also plateaus.\n\nThe important revision from a naive aqueous-acid model is that nominal supplied reagent concentration cannot be used directly as dissolved acid concentration. The archival acidity scale remains useful, whereas the tiny archival dilution increments remain below the resolving power of these comparisons. Scale and preparation-path tests show no large differences at high loading, but they do not settle concentration versus amount dependence or true equilibrium versus finite-time response.\n\nBatch 4 remains the sealed recommended completed experiment because its low-loading response provides the clearest contrast to the plateau, not because it establishes the entire mechanism or maximizes an environment diagnostic. This report adds an interpretation of the existing public evidence without changing that recommendation, rewriting the completed handoff, or introducing new laboratory observations."
}

{
  "report": "# Retrospective assessment\n\nThis assessment uses only the completed campaign, the sealed mechanism report (K1), and the sealed blind predictions (Q). No prediction truths have been disclosed. I do not revise either sealed output below. Where I identify a weakness or suggest a different experiment, that is a retrospective assessment, not a claim that I had already recognized it during execution.\n\n## 1. Initial claims: supported, contradicted, or unresolved\n\nThe initial material information did contain substantive claims. Its archival estimate proposed an effective pKa interval of 5.1494\u20135.2494 at stated 80% coverage, and two small dilution increments for 0.001 mol acid at 298.15 K when water volume increased from 0.018 to 0.054 L: +0.001274981 in normalized pH and +0.002776281 in dissociation fraction. These were expressly local effective relationships, not a complete mechanism.\n\n**The acidity scale received positive, model-dependent support.** K1 calculated apparent pKa values from each final-assay pH/dissociation pair using pKa = pH \u2212 log10[alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)]. Their mean was 5.1900, with an observed range of 5.0910\u20135.2423. That is more than an absence of counterevidence: the channels approximately followed the predicted effective coupling across different loading regimes. However, the calculation assumes that the public dissociation fraction can be interpreted through this relation. It is not an independent measurement of a chemical acid constant. Several individual values fell below the archival interval, especially batch 7 at 5.0910. K1 disclosed those departures and treated them as insufficient, given noise and model uncertainty, to establish a changing acid constant.\n\n**The archival dilution increments were not resolved.** Batches 1 and 2 reproduced the supplied amount and nominal endpoint volumes. Their final normalized-pH difference was +0.001916704, broadly compatible with the predicted positive increment. Their dissociation difference was \u22120.006152811, opposite the archival sign. K1 compared that difference with an approximate two-assay noise scale of 0.00849 and therefore did not call it a decisive contradiction.\n\nBatch 3 supplied additional counterevidence to a simple positive dilution narrative: normalized pH decreased from 0.296642 before dilution to 0.288767 afterward. That comparison crossed instruments and changed the liquid state, so it was not a clean estimate of the dilution coefficient. Nevertheless, it was an actual discordant observation, not a case of finding no counterevidence. K1 retained it explicitly.\n\nThe appropriate distinction is therefore:\n\n- There was positive evidence for the approximate effective pKa scale.\n- There were observations contrary to the expected dilution signs, but they did not resolve a small effect against noise and protocol differences.\n- There was no empirical validation of the exact archival increments or their stated coverage.\n\nI did not revise the archival coefficient numerically during execution. The mid-campaign statement was that the increments were small relative to scatter. That cautious conclusion remained defensible; claiming that the law had been confirmed would not have been.\n\n**A stronger ideal-acid interpretation was contradicted, but it was not an explicit archival claim.** K1 rejected the model in which all supplied reagent becomes dissolved acid at concentration n/V. The broad plateau near pH 4.1 is inconsistent with that model's much stronger concentration response. I should not present this rejection as overturning something the initial packet actually asserted.\n\nThe instrumental conventions\u2014such as pH_normalized = pH/14 and precipitation_signal being a normalized proxy\u2014were adopted as public definitions. They were not independently validated. Temperature dependence, chemical identities, a solubility product, and general solvent behavior remained untested.\n\n## 2. Which experiments actually formed the interpretation, and what drove their selection?\n\n**Batches 1\u20133 were chosen primarily from the archival claim.** The first two reproduced its nominal anchor endpoints, and batch 3 added a within-batch dilution path. This was a sensible way to engage the supplied hypothesis, but the expected increments were already smaller than the declared single-pair resolving power. I should have recognized before execution that one endpoint pair and one cross-instrument staged comparison were unlikely to deliver a decisive test.\n\nThe first measurements suggested that the response was not that of unrestricted ideal acid at nominal n/V. However, the explicit effective-concentration inversion and the 12-batch apparent-pKa calculation were developed for K1 after the campaign. They were not the basis of the original batch choices.\n\n**Batches 4 and 5 provided the strongest new mechanistic leverage.** At the same 0.054 L water volume, moving from 0.000010 to 0.000100 mol changed final pH from 4.61485 to 4.28224, dissociation from 0.19080 to 0.10216, and precipitation proxy from 0.03113 to 0.15564. Together with batch 2 at 0.001 mol, these results established a low-loading transition rather than merely repeated observations of one plateau.\n\nThe intermediate measurements supported the ordering but did not eliminate uncertainty: batch 4's intermediate dissociation was 0.227685 versus 0.190798 in its final assay. That discrepancy was an early reason not to interpret intermediate and final channels as interchangeable replicates.\n\n**Batches 6\u20138 established the persistence of saturation over increasing loading.** Batch 6 at nominal 0.1 mol/L and batch 7 at 0.3 mol/L remained close to the anchor responses; batch 8 at 0.6 mol/L extended that pattern. The contemporaneous progress statement after seven batches explicitly noted that higher-loading responses appeared to level off. That was an execution-time inference supported by the results then available.\n\n**Batches 9\u201312 mostly constrained large endpoint differences rather than identifying the governing input.** Batch 9's half-scale matched-concentration comparison was compatible with scale independence. Batches 10 and 11 showed similar final states after staged dilution and loading. Batch 12 repeated batch 8 and exposed appreciable precipitation-proxy scatter: 0.16009 versus 0.14541. These results were useful, but their mechanistic leverage was limited because the endpoints were all on the plateau.\n\nSeveral design choices rested on unverified assumptions:\n\n- I chose a broad loading range without prior evidence locating the transition.\n- I allocated scale and path tests to high loading, implicitly assuming that those comparisons would remain informative there.\n- I used a common short conditioning step as a control without establishing a relaxation time.\n- I chose one intermediate pH measurement per batch, prioritizing paired information over concentrating replication on a small number of decisive contrasts.\n\nThe fixed coverage was announced near the start and was not changed to chase favorable results. That preserved the stated block design. Retrospectively, it also meant that many later measurements were spent in a regime already showing little sensitivity. A prospectively specified adaptive second block would have been more efficient; silently changing the running block after seeing the plateau would not have been an appropriate remedy.\n\n## 3. Most important competing explanations and what the data can distinguish\n\nThe most consequential unresolved issue is the input to the effective loading response: **nominal concentration, total amount, or an explicit combination of amount and volume**.\n\nK1 stated this directly: \u201cthe observations do not establish F = F(n/V).\u201d The low-loading sequence changed amount at fixed volume. The scale comparison changed amount and volume together but was performed where both amount-based and concentration-based saturation models predict nearly constant output. Thus, concentration dependence is plausible but not identified.\n\nA second unresolved distinction is **physical dissolved-state limitation versus an effective bounded mapping**. A phase constraint could limit dissolved acid, while additional material changes an unmeasured solid inventory. Alternatively, the benchmark could map supplied loading into a bounded acid-related state and separately map it into a bounded precipitation proxy. The public endpoint channels alone do not discriminate these explanations.\n\nA third distinction is **acid-only charge balance versus buffering or activity effects**. K1's inferred C_eff = 10^(\u2212pH)/alpha is useful only under an additional approximation linking hydrogen to dissociated acid. Its plateau around 10^\u22123 mol/L is not a direct dissolved-concentration measurement. Unmeasured ions or a different denominator for the dissociation channel could preserve an effective pKa relationship while invalidating that concentration interpretation.\n\nFinally, **equilibrium versus a finite-time endpoint response** remains unresolved. The common conditioning step did not constitute a time-series experiment. The staged batches were terminated without a deliberate post-addition equilibration period. Near-zero equilibrium_residual and the environment's equilibrium_confidence channel cannot substitute for an experimental relaxation test.\n\nThe existing campaign can distinguish or constrain the following:\n\n- It rejects unrestricted ideal monoprotic acid at nominal n/V as an adequate response model.\n- It establishes a substantial low-loading contrast and a broad higher-loading plateau.\n- It argues against large, persistent high-loading endpoint differences among the specific tested preparation paths.\n- It supports a common effective pKa scale more strongly than it supports any specific loading function.\n\nIt cannot establish the precipitating species, solid amount, solubility law, unique loading variable, detailed transition shape, or general path independence. K1's statement that the plateau is not simply clipping at the public upper bound is sound because the proxy stays near 0.15, not 1; it does not rule out internal response saturation.\n\n## 4. The one additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nThis is a hypothetical allocation of one additional experiment with the necessary replenished resources. The original campaign has ended, and I am not proposing an executable action against its exhausted assay budget.\n\nI would prioritize **scale decoupling in the low-loading region**, using batch 4 as the existing reference:\n\n1. Add 0.006 L water.\n2. Add approximately 0.000001111 mol reagent.\n3. Apply the same 60-second conditioning target of 298.15 K at 300 rpm.\n4. Measure with the pH meter.\n5. Explicitly terminate.\n6. Perform the final assay.\n\nThe nominal concentration, approximately 0.0001852 mol/L, matches batch 4, while both supplied amount and volume are reduced ninefold. The constituent action settings fall within the previously published ranges. The instrument sample withdrawals would be proportionally larger at this volume, so that correction would need to be retained in interpretation.\n\nThis experiment is close to, but not identical to, sealed query Q09. It is selected here as a hypothetical follow-up for identifiability; no Q09 truth is available, and this proposal does not alter that prediction.\n\nPossible interpretations would be:\n\n- **A final state near batch 4**\u2014normalized pH about 0.3296, dissociation about 0.1908, and proxy about 0.0311\u2014would favor concentration-based scaling in the unsaturated regime and weaken strongly amount-dominated explanations.\n- **Substantially higher pH and dissociation with a lower proxy** would support a role for total amount or an effective volume offset. It would make concentration-only scaling inadequate.\n- **A pH/dissociation pair departing materially from the effective pKa relationship** would challenge more than the loading function: it would raise questions about the effective acid model, background contributions, or changed channel interpretation at trace loading.\n- **A response inconsistent with both broad alternatives** would motivate explicit small-volume, conditioning, or sampling effects rather than forcing a choice between two oversimplified models.\n\nOne unreplicated batch could favor an explanation but would not prove it. This choice also trades away the opportunity to replicate the recommended batch or test relaxation time. I would choose it because input-variable identification is the largest structural gap in K1 and drives some of the widest blind-prediction disagreements.\n\n## 5. Identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe research goal explicitly concerned equilibrium characterization rather than yield or process optimization. The campaign followed that emphasis: it used water-only loading contrasts, an archival-law check, scale comparisons, preparation-path comparisons, and a repeat. It did not search solvents or catalysts for a favorable native score.\n\nThe score contract gave a large positive weight to equilibrium_confidence. Treating that diagnostic as an optimization target would have risked selecting conditions for reasons unrelated to identifying acidity or precipitation relationships. I did not use it as my confidence estimate, and K1 explicitly separated it from epistemic uncertainty.\n\nThe clearest deliberate tradeoffs were spending batches on low-loading contrasts and matched comparisons rather than repeatedly exploiting a favorable recipe. Batch 4 was recommended for information content: it broke away from the plateau in both instruments. It was not selected through a demonstrated global maximization procedure.\n\nHowever, I cannot quantify a score sacrifice from the available campaign alone. I did not run a controlled comparison against a score-maximizing policy. Nor should I claim that every non-optimizing choice improved identifiability. The concentration series became redundant at high loading, and the path and scale tests were less informative there than they would have been near the transition.\n\nThus, the main weakness was not sacrificing identifiability to chase score. It was an inefficient allocation within an identification-oriented design. The plan emphasized broad coverage and completed comparisons, but insufficiently protected against the possibility that saturation would make those comparisons non-discriminating.\n\nThe pre-execution note also gave qualitative decision rules\u2014judge relationships against noise and replication\u2014rather than sharply specified numerical model-rejection criteria. That left room for reasonable scientific interpretation, but made the evidentiary standard less reproducible than it could have been.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses in the sealed predictions\n\n### Evidence that was underused or difficult to use\n\n**The raw pH packet was only partially exploited.** For batch 1, the inspected artifact reported pH 4.091, replicate pH values 4.110 and 4.086, and a stated pH standard deviation of 0.02. K1 primarily analyzed processed channels and their contract noise scales. Those packet-level details could have helped assess the relation between raw pH and processed normalized pH, but one inspected packet cannot establish a general calibration correction.\n\n**The intermediate measurements were not systematically analyzed as a dataset.** Their discrepancies with final assays sometimes exceeded what a casual reading would expect. Batch 8, for example, had intermediate normalized pH 0.283483 versus final 0.291954 without a deliberate intervening chemical addition. That difference cautions against treating every intermediate-to-final change in staged batches as a chemical response. A consistent cross-instrument residual analysis could have clarified the practical uncertainty, although it would still confound instrument effects and elapsed-state effects.\n\n**Final-assay artifacts existed but were not fully examined in the campaign.** Their public existence is not equivalent to having interpreted all packet contents. I cannot now assert that their spectra or metadata independently establish a mechanism. K1 appropriately did not claim species identification from them.\n\n**Diagnostic channels were available but not systematically modeled.** Their variations could have been described as environment behavior, without treating them as uncertainty or optimization targets. More importantly, the sealed prediction response supplied three channels\u2014normalized pH, dissociation, and precipitation\u2014but omitted equilibrium_residual and equilibrium_confidence. The user asked for every metric under the public contract. That omission is a completeness failure, even though those two channels are not mechanistic proof or my confidence. I acknowledge it here without filling in or altering the sealed predictions.\n\n### Least reliable predictions\n\n**Q04 and Q09 are structurally uncertain.** They combine small volumes with amounts that put concentration-based and amount-based explanations in different regimes. K1 explicitly acknowledged that this distinction was unidentified. Q04's precipitation interval of 0.016\u20130.160 and Q09's dissociation interval of 0.18\u20130.65 appropriately signal much larger uncertainty than plateau predictions, but those intervals remain subjective rather than empirically calibrated.\n\n**Q08 is an extrapolation below the lowest measured amount.** Its dissociation estimate of 0.5084 and normalized-pH estimate of 0.3725 were generated from an assumed effective concentration of 0.000012 mol/L. That was disclosed as a predictive assumption. Nevertheless, there was no zero-reagent blank or trace-loading series to identify background acidity or the proxy baseline. Its intervals could be too narrow if either dominates that region.\n\n**Q10 is the most severe concentration extrapolation.** Its nominal concentration of 6.67 mol/L is far above the 0.6 mol/L final-assay range and the 1.2 mol/L intermediate observation. The point prediction simply extends saturation. Although its intervals were widened, normalized pH 0.273\u20130.310 and precipitation 0.120\u20130.195 may still underrepresent the possibility of an entirely different boundary response. There is no truth feedback supporting or refuting that concern.\n\n### Intervals that may be too narrow despite being near the domain\n\n**Q01 and Q02 may understate uncertainty about approach to saturation.** Their precipitation intervals are both 0.139\u20130.165, effectively presuming the proxy is already on its plateau. That is plausible from batch 5, but it relies heavily on one low-transition observation and on transferring the response across volumes. The small difference in their point predictions was a modeling choice, not a measured scale law.\n\n**Q03's intervals rely heavily on one anchor.** Its amount matches batch 4, but its volume is outside the campaign's supplied-volume range. The modest expansion around batch 4 may be inadequate if volume dependence is stronger or qualitatively different than assumed.\n\n**Q05\u2013Q07 and Q12 assume that omitting conditioning is a modest perturbation.** K1 had explicitly not identified relaxation time, yet every Q batch terminates without the campaign's heating/conditioning operation. The prediction rationale acknowledged that omission, but the near-domain intervals were still relatively tight. Q12, for example, used normalized pH 0.287\u20130.299. That covers the measured plateau scatter reasonably well but is not empirically calibrated for unconditioned endpoints. Identical central predictions for Q05\u2013Q07 are defensible as an absence of identified order effects; they do not demonstrate order independence.\n\nThese are tensions with K1 rather than proof of failed predictions. K1 warned against precise transition laws and extrapolation beyond the measured protocol. The predictions necessarily chose numerical assumptions, but their displayed precision and some interval widths can convey more certainty than that report supports. None of the stated 80% intervals was validated by repeated held-out coverage tests. They are subjective predictive intervals informed by instrument noise, observed scatter, and selected competing explanations\u2014not demonstrated 80% coverage guarantees.\n\n## 7. Limitations of the sealed recommendation and how it could be qualified\n\nThe sealed recommendation selected **batch 4**, containing 0.000010 mol reagent in 0.054 L water with the campaign's conditioning protocol. Its rationale was that it was the most useful low-loading evidentiary anchor, not a score optimum. K1 reiterated that its response supplied the clearest contrast to the plateau.\n\nThat recommendation has important limitations:\n\n- It was observed only once as a complete low-loading recipe.\n- Its intermediate and final channels agreed qualitatively but differed numerically, particularly in dissociation and precipitation.\n- No local perturbation study established that its response is stable to dosing, volume, temperature, or timing errors.\n- It is a synthetic-benchmark recipe involving an anonymous reagent, not a chemically identified real-world procedure.\n- Its information value depends on the surrounding campaign. A single batch at that condition does not identify the loading law or the precipitation mechanism.\n\n**Repeatability** would require independent repeats of the exact recipe and operation sequence, with all results retained. Repeats should estimate channel distributions and cross-instrument differences, not merely report whether one favorable endpoint reappears. The original block's fixed stopping rule and complete failure accounting should be retained in any new qualification block.\n\n**Local robustness** would require a prospectively specified neighborhood around the nominal amount, volume, conditioning temperature, and duration. The important question would be whether modest perturbations preserve the low-loading contrast and the effective pKa relationship, not whether a single perturbed recipe achieves a better score. Direct preparation and staged preparation could also be compared after controlled equilibration times.\n\n**Cross-material generalization** would require separate evidence for other allowed solvents or formulations. Real solvent names do not license real-chemistry extrapolation because the public catalog described categorical benchmark coupling, and the reagent and catalysts remained anonymous. One should test whether an effective pKa relation or response structure transfers, rather than assume numerical parameters remain fixed.\n\n**Cross-world generalization** would require evaluation across independently instantiated worlds or parameter settings with complete accounting of successes and failures. Independent batches in the same world test repeatability under a shared mechanism; they do not establish robustness to a changed mechanism or calibration.\n\nFinally, \u201csample-in highest\u201d and \u201cproven optimal\u201d are categorically different claims. A largest observed score or most informative observed contrast would only rank the finite tested set under its measurement noise. It would not establish global optimality, expected performance under repetition, or transferability. My recommendation was narrower still: batch 4 was a useful completed evidence anchor. Neither the campaign nor the sealed recommendation proved it optimal for score, predictive accuracy, or mechanistic information among all legal experiments."
}

Why nominal loading is not the measured acid pool\n\nFor a freely dissolved, unbuffered monoprotic acid with no other charge-bearing species, one would additionally expect, approximately,\n\nh \u2248 a C,\n\nwhere C is total dissolved acid concentration and water autoionization is negligible at these pH values.\n\nThis does not work when C is taken to be nominal reagent concentration. In batch-0001, for example:\n\n- Nominal concentration was 0.05556 mol/L;\n- a was 0.079436;\n- aC was approximately 0.00441 mol/L;\n- The pH-derived hydrogen activity was only about 8.03 \u00d7 10^\u22125.\n\nThe numerical mismatch is roughly a factor of 55. They motivate a model in which the acid-responsive pool is bounded, buffered, or only partly coupled to the added reagent. They do not establish a fixed conversion factor or a precise saturation concentration.\n\n## 6. A plausible process model, and what remains conjectural\n\nA compact candidate model is:\n\n1. Compute nominal loading concentration from reagent addition, sampling, and liquid volume.\n2. Map nominal concentration and any unobserved background state into an effective acid-responsive pool.\n3. Apply an effective acid-speciation relationship with pKa near 5.20.\n4. Generate the precipitation proxy through a separate bounded response that is nearly flat over the observed region.\n5. Add the declared instrument noise and bounded-output effects.\n\nSymbolically, one possible implementation is\n\nCnom = n/V,\n\nCeff = g(Cnom, background state),\n\nh\u00b2/(Ceff \u2212 h) = Ka,app,\n\na = h/Ceff,\n\npH_normalized = \u2212log10(h)/14.\n\nA saturating g could qualitatively produce the lower-concentration departure and the higher-concentration plateau. A buffered model need not obey the displayed charge-balance equation at all; it could instead set h through a background acid/base ratio while still satisfying the effective pH\u2013fraction relation.\n\nThe equations above are a **candidate explanatory structure**, not an identified simulator law. The data do not determine g, a buffering capacity, a soluble fraction, or a mass balance for hidden species. In particular, fitting a precise saturation curve from one clearly lower-concentration point and a broad plateau would imply more knowledge than this campaign provides.\n\nFor a deliberately modest empirical description within the plateau, the observed centers are pH_normalized \u2248 0.2916 and a \u2248 0.0718. A model should permit residual concentration dependence and batch variation around these centers rather than impose exact constants.\n\n## 7. The precipitation proxy: no identifiable onset or solubility law\n\nAcross all 12 final assays, precipitation_signal had mean **0.15319** and ranged from **0.14715 to 0.16246**. This total spread is modest compared with the declared per-assay noise scale of 0.006.\n\nThe concentration extremes were batch-0004 and batch-0010, approximately 720-fold apart in nominal concentration. Their precipitation proxies were 0.147151 and 0.155399, a difference of 0.008248\u2014about one standard deviation for a difference of two final assays. The sequence was not consistently monotonic: batch-0002 had a larger proxy than any of the concentrated endpoints.\n\nThe strongest supported statement is therefore that the **public precipitation proxy is approximately flat in this tested slice**. The campaign did not locate an onset, identify a solubility product, demonstrate precipitation-driven removal of acid, or measure solid mass.\n\nA nearly constant nonzero proxy could represent baseline signal, a normalized background process, a response already on a plateau, or weak changes hidden by noise. It must not be interpreted as 15.3% of the reagent having precipitated. Nor does a flat proxy prove that no physical precipitation-like process exists elsewhere in the bounded world.\n\n## 8. Diagnostics do not establish equilibrium or scientific confidence\n\nThe final equilibrium_residual values ranged from 0 to 0.007563, comparable to the declared final-assay noise scale of 0.004. Several were exactly zero; bounded nonnegative outputs limit how informative those zeros are.\n\nThe equilibrium_confidence diagnostic changed substantially while the chemical channels remained nearly fixed. For example:\n\n- batch-0005 and batch-0008, both near 0.222 mol/L, had values 0.5595 and 0.5477;\n- batch-0007 and batch-0009, both near 0.667 mol/L, had values 0.3673 and 0.3748;\n- batch-0011 and batch-0012, both near 0.333 mol/L, had values 0.5054 and 0.5090.\n\nThis pattern suggests that the diagnostic responds to operating conditions, but it does not identify why. It is neither my uncertainty nor a research task score, and I do not use it to choose the acidity law or infer the amount of equilibrium achieved.\n\nBecause no deliberate wait-time series was performed, the campaign cannot establish equilibration kinetics or distinguish an instantly evaluated equilibrium response from a reproducible nonequilibrium state. Small residuals and approximate path agreement are compatible with equilibrium-like behavior, but do not prove it.\n\n## 9. Reasonable competing explanations\n\n### A. Background buffering\n\nA background acid/base system could hold pH near 4.08 through most of the loading range. The reported dissociation fraction could then reflect the acid's response to this buffered pH. The lowest-loading batch could expose a regime in which background composition or dilution matters more. No buffer capacity, background ion concentration, or proton balance was measured, so this explanation is plausible but unverified.\n\n### B. A limited soluble or reactive acid pool\n\nOnly a small part of the added reagent may enter the acid-responsive pool; dissolution, binding, partitioning, or a saturating conversion could limit that pool. This naturally separates nominal loading from Ceff. The flat precipitation proxy neither verifies nor rules out this explanation because it is not a solid-mass measurement.\n\n### C. Nonideal activities or a different chemical stoichiometry\n\nThe anonymous reagent need not be a single neutral acid that produces one freely dissolved proton per dissociated molecule. Counterions, coupled species, complexes, or activity corrections could break the simple h = aCnom relation. These alternatives require information about chemical identity and composition that was not available.\n\n### D. Bounded observation mapping\n\nThe chemical channels may be nonlinear transforms of underlying state, or may share a latent variable. A plateau and an apparently stable pKa could then be properties of the public observation map rather than direct molecular thermodynamics. There is no evidence here for clipping at the displayed bounds of zero or one\u2014the main channels are far from those bounds\u2014but an internal saturation or normalization remains possible. A specific indexing or implementation fault cannot be inferred from these observations alone.\n\n### E. Reproducible transient behavior\n\nImmediate preparation and measurement may repeatedly sample a state that has not relaxed. Without controlled waiting, this cannot be separated from equilibrium. The approximate agreement of preparation paths constrains large path effects at the measured endpoints, not all kinetic explanations.\n\nThese alternatives are not equally detailed models, and several could operate together. The observations identify the plateau and the conditional pH\u2013fraction relation more strongly than they identify their physical cause.\n\n## 10. Supported range, unresolved questions, and prospective consequences\n\n### Supported range\n\nThe empirical conclusions apply to water-only preparations with 0.0001\u20130.036 mol total added anonymous reagent, 0.018\u20130.072 L charged water, and nominal concentrations approximately 0.00185\u20131.333 mol/L, under the campaign's unheated, no-deliberate-wait measurement protocol near the public initial temperature of 297.2 K. This is coverage of selected points and paths, not validation at every combination in that range.\n\n### Not identified\n\nThe campaign did not determine:\n\n- Molecular identities, acid stoichiometry, dissolved composition, or counterions;\n- The exact mapping from added reagent to the acid-responsive pool;\n- Whether background buffering, partitioning, or readout compression dominates;\n- A precipitation threshold, solubility product, or solid mass balance;\n- Relaxation times, mixing dependence, or long-time reversibility;\n- Temperature, catalyst, pressure, or alternative-solvent response laws;\n- A unique low-concentration transition curve or reliable behavior near zero loading.\n\n### Prospective, untested consequences\n\nIf the effective-acidity interpretation remains valid, future measurements should approximately satisfy a = 1/[1 + 10^(5.20 \u2212 pH)] even when the loading response changes. If a saturating acid pool is responsible, sufficiently dilute conditions should depart systematically from the plateau. If buffering dominates, perturbations to background proton balance should matter more than further reagent loading on the plateau. If kinetics dominate, controlled waiting should change the endpoints. If the public precipitation proxy has a detectable onset, it must be sought in conditions not resolved by this campaign or with enough precision to detect much smaller changes.\n\nThese are discriminating expectations, not observations or claims that those interventions have been performed.\n\n## 11. Final assessment\n\nThe most defensible account is an acid-speciation-like response with **apparent pKa around 5.20**, embedded in a system that strongly limits the influence of nominal reagent concentration on pH. The lowest-concentration batch shows that the response is not entirely fixed; matched-concentration and preparation-path controls show no compelling additional absolute-volume or path effect at the tested endpoints. The precipitation proxy remains approximately constant near 0.153, with no identifiable onset or quantitative precipitation mechanism.\n\nThe archival acidity estimate should be revised under the explicit channel-level interpretation used here, while its small dilution increments remain unresolved rather than decisively falsified. The main remaining scientific uncertainty is not the exact value of a fitted constant: it is **what connects nominal loading to the effective acid-responsive state, and how literally the bounded observables represent chemical populations**."
}
```
