Batch 12, an undiluted anchor repeat, differed from batch 1 by +0.003552 in normalized pH\u2014slightly more than the direct dilution comparison. K1 therefore correctly described the exact archival increments as \u201cneither validated nor falsified.\u201d The observed variation did not justify treating the initial pair as a decisive contradiction that I then ignored.\n\nThere was also a temperature qualification: the archival anchor specified 298.15 K, while the public reactor telemetry initially showed approximately 297.205 K and the inspected pH artifact used a 298.15 K sample/calibration basis. I did not resolve that distinction experimentally. Thus even a more precise numerical disagreement would have required care before declaring the archival relationship false.\n\n**A broader ideal-acid interpretation was contradicted, but it was not an explicit archival claim.** K1 rejected substituting all nominally charged reagent directly into an ideal, unbuffered acid mass balance. For batch 1, alpha times nominal concentration was approximately 0.00388 mol/L, whereas hydrogen activity inferred from pH was approximately 0.000058. The discrepancy grew enormously at batch 8. Likewise, the nearly flat response across batches 5\u20138 was inconsistent with the large changes expected from an ideal dissolved acid spanning 0.075\u20131.0 mol/L. It would be misleading to portray this as falsification of an archival claim that all reagent was dissolved; the initial material did not make that claim.\n\nThe exact transformation establishing these discrepancies was performed after the campaign. During execution, I recognized the plateau and the unresolved dilution effect, but had not yet completed the quantitative effective-pKa and mass-balance analysis. This is an important distinction between an interpretation developed from the evidence and a hypothesis demonstrably driving the original experimental choices.\n\n## 2. Which experiments actually shaped the interpretation, and what drove their selection\n\n**Batches 1 and 2 were primarily prior-driven.** Their amounts and volumes directly reproduced the archival loading/dilution comparison. They were scientifically justified as a local check, but the expected increments were already small relative to single-assay noise. One pair was therefore unlikely to determine the numerical dilution law. The initial choice prioritized direct comparability with the prior rather than statistical power.\n\n**Batches 3 and 4 supplied the strongest evidence that the chemical observables were responsive rather than simply constant.** At fixed 0.040 L, the tenfold loading increase lowered final pH from 4.6279 to 4.3128, lowered dissociation from 0.15266 to 0.09483, and raised precipitation proxy from 0.05389 to 0.14928. The intermediate pH-meter observations supported the same qualitative contrast. Without these batches, the campaign could have supported little more than a local plateau description.\n\n**Batches 5\u20138 established the broad plateau.** Increasing reagent from 0.003 to 0.040 mol at fixed volume did not produce a convincing monotonic trend in the three chemical endpoints. After eight completed batches, my contemporaneous progress statement explicitly identified a distinct lowest-loading response and an apparent higher-loading plateau. This was an execution-stage inference, not a later reconstruction.\n\nThe choice of a wide fixed-volume loading series was made before those outcomes. It depended on a reasonable but unvalidated expectation that varying loading would expose nonlinear equilibrium responses. The coverage was useful, but sparse: the series jumped from 0.0001 to 0.003 mol. Once the outcomes were known, that gap was clearly a weakness for locating the transition. I did not change the fixed block in response to its results.\n\n**Batch 9 was a deliberate scale-decoupling check**, doubling amount and volume relative to batch 1. It supported approximate endpoint similarity at constant nominal concentration. However, K1 acknowledged its central limitation: both conditions lay on the plateau, where even the wrong controlling variable could predict similar endpoints. The design intention was stronger than the resulting discrimination.\n\n**Batches 10 and 11 tested preparation history**, respectively through staged dilution and staged reagent addition. Their final endpoints were reasonably close to directly prepared counterparts, batches 2 and 6. They supported treating final composition as a first approximation, but did not establish reversibility or equilibration. Their before/after contrasts used different instruments, so path, instrument, timing, and sampling effects were not cleanly separated.\n\n**Batch 12 materially changed the strength of the dilution conclusion.** Its difference from batch 1 demonstrated that an anchor-to-anchor pH shift could be as large as the apparent dilution shift. This made a strong claim from batches 1 and 2 untenable. The repeat was preplanned; its interpretive value became clear only after its result.\n\nThe campaign therefore combined a prior-driven anchor, hypothesis-driven broad coverage, and checks motivated by general concerns about scale and history. It was not an adaptively optimized sequence in which every later condition was selected after fitting the preceding results.\n\n## 3. The most important competing explanations\n\nThe central unresolved issue is what creates the apparent saturation.\n\n**Limited dissolved or chemically available acid** is one explanation. Additional reagent could increasingly enter an unavailable pool or another phase, leaving an approximately capped acidity-related pool. This is compatible with the plateau and with the rise in precipitation proxy between batches 3 and 4.\n\n**Buffering or coupled speciation** is another. Background proton-binding capacity, complexation, or additional acid\u2013base species could decouple pH from nominal loading without a simple dissolved-material cap. The effective pKa relation could remain approximately valid for a reported fraction while the total material balance is more complicated.\n\n**A bounded synthetic mapping** is equally important in this benchmark. An internal transformation of nominal loading could generate a saturating effective state and correlated observables without supporting a literal physical interpretation of C_eff. K1 explicitly described this as a model-class possibility, not a discovery of implementation details.\n\nA second unresolved distinction concerns the input to that saturation: **concentration, total amount, or a combination of amount and volume**. The strongest loading contrast, batches 3 and 4, changed amount and concentration together. The explicit decoupling comparisons were on the plateau. Consequently, they could not determine whether concentration is the principal controlling variable in the responsive low-loading regime. This uncertainty is especially consequential for Q03, Q04, Q08, and Q09.\n\nThe existing campaign does distinguish some explanations:\n\n- It argues strongly against all nominal reagent behaving as a single ideal, unbuffered dissolved acid.\n- It argues against a globally constant response, because batches 3 and 4 differ clearly.\n- It provides no evidence for a large persistent preparation-order effect at the tested plateau endpoints.\n- It supports a useful numerical coupling between pH and dissociation through an effective pKa.\n\nIt does not distinguish a solubility limit from buffering or an internal response transformation; determine a precipitation mass balance; identify a transition curve; or prove that the shared pH\u2013dissociation relation reflects independently observed microscopic chemistry. The precipitation proxy is particularly limiting: a plateau near 0.15 is neither measured solid mass nor saturation at the public channel maximum of 1.\n\n## 4. The single additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nIf one additional batch were authorized with the same operation types and one intermediate measurement plus a final assay, I would use **0.000010 mol reagent in 0.006 L water**, then take a pH-meter measurement, explicitly terminate, and obtain the final assay. I would add no catalyst and impose no heating or other intervention. This is a hypothetical proposal only; the completed campaign budget remains exhausted.\n\nThe condition deliberately matches batch 3's reagent amount while decreasing water volume from 0.040 to 0.006 L. It is also the chemical preparation in Q04, though the added intermediate measurement would mean it was not an exact replay of that sealed prediction recipe. The assay-withdrawal history would have to be accounted for explicitly.\n\nThis choice targets the most important unresolved distinction in the responsive regime rather than collecting another plateau point. Its nominal concentration, approximately 0.001667 mol/L, lies between the tested concentrations of batches 3 and 4, while total amount remains exactly that of batch 3.\n\nPossible outcomes would have different implications:\n\n- **A shift toward batch 4**, such as lower dissociation and pH with precipitation proxy near the plateau, would support a strong concentration or volume-mediated effect. The provisional Q04 central values\u2014dissociation about 0.089, normalized pH about 0.3108, and precipitation proxy about 0.143\u2014illustrate this possibility, but are not pass/fail thresholds or known truth.\n- **A response near batch 3**, with dissociation around 0.153 and precipitation proxy around 0.054, would favor total-amount dependence or another mapping that weakens ordinary concentration scaling. It would challenge the concentration-based central extrapolations without invalidating the observed effective-pKa relation.\n- **An intermediate response** would suggest combined amount and volume dependence or a different transition shape. One batch would still not identify a unique formula.\n- **A change in the pH\u2013dissociation relationship itself** beyond plausible measurement variation would weaken the shared effective-pKa approximation and raise the priority of activity, speciation, or observation-model explanations.\n\nI would not interpret a single result as resolving the physical-versus-synthetic saturation question. The primary gain would be discrimination between input dependencies that the original campaign left confounded.\n\n## 5. Mechanistic identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe stated research objective was characterization, not yield or scalar-score optimization. That affected the design substantially: I used water-only conditions, varied amount and volume, included an independent repeat, and spent batches on scale and preparation-history comparisons. I did not search catalysts, chase high diagnostic values, or select the final reference by maximum native score.\n\nFor example, batches 6 and 11 had final equilibrium_confidence diagnostics around 0.576 and 0.572, substantially above batch 1's approximately 0.433. I did not promote them on that basis. As K1 states, equilibrium_confidence is an environment diagnostic, not my confidence or the research objective.\n\nThere was an opportunity cost to prioritizing characterization: repeated anchors and low-loading points consumed batches that could instead have explored the score landscape. However, I cannot claim a quantified sacrifice in achievable score because I did not conduct a competing optimization campaign or establish a score optimum. Likewise, I cannot reliably rank the final experiments by a reconstructed score using only selected components while ignoring other evaluator details.\n\nThe more important weakness was not score chasing but **inefficient use of the characterization budget**. Several high-loading batches produced redundant plateau information, while the low-loading transition and amount\u2013volume decoupling received too little coverage. Broad coverage helped reveal saturation, but a more informative design would have allocated more independent contrasts near the transition.\n\nThus there is no evidence that I knowingly sacrificed identifiability to increase score. There is clear evidence that the fixed design sacrificed some potential identifiability to broad range coverage and direct archival comparability. Those are scientific design tradeoffs, but they were not necessarily optimal ones.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and the weakest sealed predictions\n\nSeveral types of acquired evidence were not fully exploited.\n\n**The intermediate measurements were used mainly as corroboration, not jointly modeled.** For instance, batch 3's normalized pH was 0.33964 on the pH meter and 0.33057 on final assay. Batch 11's intermediate dissociation was 0.11296, considerably above its final value of 0.07066 after additional reagent. These differences deserve a structured observation model, but each is confounded by instrument differences, and the staged batches additionally changed chemistry. It would have been inappropriate to average all intermediate and final values indiscriminately.\n\n**Raw artifact information was incompletely examined.** I inspected the batch-1 pH artifact, including its replicate pH values and calibration information, but did not systematically inspect every acquired artifact. The final-assay artifact contents were not comprehensively analyzed. I therefore cannot claim that unexamined raw details would resolve a particular uncertainty. Their possible value is a limitation of analysis coverage, not additional evidence I can invoke after sealing.\n\n**Operational and sampling information was acknowledged but not incorporated into an exact inferential model.** The pH meter removed 0.000030 L before each original final assay, whereas the prediction recipes omit that measurement. At the campaign volumes this is a small change, but the handling of species in withdrawn liquid was not independently identified. Instrument timing and termination were also not disentangled from any underlying state evolution.\n\n**The diagnostic channels were mostly summarized rather than explained.** Batch 8's pH-meter equilibrium_residual of 0.02161 contrasted with a final value of zero. There was insufficient evidence to decide whether such differences carried mechanistic information or mainly reflected channel noise and clipping. Treating them as chemical proof would have been worse than leaving them unresolved.\n\nThe least reliable predictions are the ones most dependent on the untested low-loading map:\n\n- **Q08** extrapolates both amount and concentration below the tested range. Its point estimate of dissociation 0.469 depends strongly on the provisional saturating-pool equation and neglect of unknown background chemistry.\n- **Q09** combines an untested trace amount with an untested small volume. Its 0.177 central dissociation estimate follows concentration scaling, while its upper interval permits a much more amount-driven response. That asymmetry represents real model disagreement, not a calibrated sampling distribution.\n- **Q04** has the same total amount as batch 3 but a much smaller volume. Its precipitation central estimate of 0.143 differs sharply from batch 3's 0.05389, making it a direct test of an unresolved dependency.\n- **Q03** is less extreme but still assumes how dilution behaves below the tested concentration range.\n- **Q10** extrapolates to 6.667 mol/L nominal loading and could encounter a different regime even though the total amount was previously tested.\n\nThe most questionable intervals are not necessarily the narrowest numerical intervals. Q08's precipitation interval of 0\u20130.042 may be too restrictive because no zero-loading baseline was measured. Q09's dissociation upper bound of 0.48 and Q08's upper bound of 0.73 are judgmental; the evidence does not establish those as calibrated 90th percentiles. Q10's precipitation interval of 0.111\u20130.195 may underrepresent a regime change at extreme nominal concentration. Even Q12's relatively narrow interior intervals could undercover if unmodeled between-batch effects or correlated observation errors are larger than assumed.\n\nThere is also a tension with K1 that should be explicit. K1 said the exact saturation and precipitation functions were unidentified. The predictions nevertheless chose C_eff = 0.0008 C/(C + 0.001) and a particular exponential precipitation curve to produce central numbers. They were labeled provisional predictive conveniences, so this did not formally contradict K1. But their numerical specificity should not be mistaken for increased evidential support. The intervals were judgment-based marginal 80% assessments, not empirically calibrated coverage guarantees. Some may insufficiently represent the structural uncertainty K1 described. No truth has yet been supplied to determine which actually undercovered.\n\n## 7. Limitations of the sealed recommendation and how its scope could be tested\n\nThe sealed recommendation selected **batch 1**, with 0.001 mol reagent in 0.018 L water, as an evidentiary anchor. It was not a declaration of best chemistry or highest score. The recorded rationale emphasized its direct link to the archival condition, its intermediate and final measurements, its independent repeat in batch 12, and its connections to dilution and scale comparisons.\n\nThat is a useful reference condition, but it has clear limitations:\n\n- It lies on the plateau and is therefore less informative about the transition mechanism than batch 3 or a low-loading decoupling experiment.\n- Its apparent repeatability rests on only one independent repeat.\n- The batch-1 versus batch-12 normalized-pH difference was 0.003552, and their dissociation difference was 0.007742. Those observations support approximate, not exact, reproduction.\n- Its relationship to the archival temperature was not precisely controlled.\n- Its validation is water-only and local to this world and operation history.\n\n**Repeatability** would require additional independent executions of the exact recipe and measurement sequence, using fixed analysis rules and retaining all failures. The goal would be to estimate the distribution of final responses rather than select the most favorable repeat. Repeated readings within one batch would not substitute for independent preparation replicates.\n\n**Local robustness** would require a predeclared small neighborhood of amount, volume, and preparation order, with relevant timing and temperature conditions controlled or deliberately varied. Robustness would mean changes stayed within stated tolerances over that neighborhood, not merely that several plateau means looked similar after inspection.\n\n**Cross-material scope** would require separate validation. Changing solvent or introducing an anonymous catalyst could alter the benchmark mapping, and real solvent names do not guarantee ordinary physical-chemistry transfer. Neither K1 nor the recommendation establishes cross-material equivalence.\n\n**Cross-world scope** would require independently initialized worlds and an explicit account of what is shared across them. Parameters such as effective pKa, saturation scale, and precipitation baseline could differ. Those tests would need to assess transfer of the model structure separately from transfer of fitted numerical values.\n\nFinally, \u201csample-internal highest\u201d and \u201cproven optimal\u201d are very different claims. Even if one completed batch had the largest observed scalar score, it would only be the highest measured result among the tested conditions, with selection noise and incomplete coverage. The campaign did not prove a global optimum, a local optimum, or even that score maximization was scientifically appropriate. Batch 1 was recommended as a connected, repeat-supported reference\u2014not as either the sample-internal maximum or an established optimum."

Their precipitation proxies remain approximately 0.1549, 0.1531, and 0.1489. There is no large monotonic response over this sixfold concentration change.\n\n### 3.2 The lowest-loading batch leaves the plateau\n\nBatches 5 and 6 have the same nominal volume, 0.054 L, and differ tenfold in reagent amount. Relative to batch 5, batch 6 has:\n\n- an increase of approximately 0.02749 in normalized pH, or 0.385 pH units;\n- an increase of approximately 0.07542 in dissociation fraction;\n- a decrease of approximately 0.11552 in precipitation proxy.\n\nThese differences are much larger than the declared final-assay noise scales of 0.002, 0.006, and 0.006, respectively. They provide the clearest evidence of a genuine regime change within the tested space.\n\nThe intermediate measurements corroborate the distinction. Batch 6 gave normalized pH 0.333255, dissociation 0.162618, and precipitation 0.016237 before the final values of 0.337032, 0.171210, and 0.034198. Batch 5 gave intermediate dissociation 0.095309 and precipitation 0.172231, consistent in broad ordering with its final values of 0.095791 and 0.149721.\n\nBatch 5 also illustrates a caution: its intermediate normalized pH was 0.322989, noticeably above its final value of 0.309545. I do not hide this discrepancy or assume the instruments are interchangeable replicates. Measurement noise, instrument differences, and differences in measurement stage all remain relevant.\n\nThe data place the transition between the markedly different lowest-loading response and the higher-loading plateau somewhere in the sparsely sampled low-concentration region. They do not locate a sharp threshold. The dissociation channel already shows an intermediate response in batch 5 while the precipitation proxy is near its higher-loading plateau; a single common hard threshold is therefore not required.\n\n### 3.3 Volume and amount are not separately identified as primary chemical controls\n\nBatches 1 and 4 have the same nominal concentration, 0.05556 mol/L, but differ threefold in both reagent amount and volume. Their final normalized pH differs by 0.002853, dissociation by 0.010810, and precipitation proxy by 0.009461. These are modest differences relative to pairwise assay noise.\n\nThis comparison is compatible with concentration being a more useful coordinate than either total amount or volume alone. However, both conditions lie on the plateau, where many different models predict little change. It is consequently a weak test of concentration scaling, not proof of it. A matched-concentration comparison in the low-loading regime would have been more discriminating, but none was performed.\n\n### 3.4 Little resolved history or hold-time dependence on the plateau\n\nDirect preparation at the diluted anchor, batch 2, and staged dilution to approximately the same condition, batch 3, give final normalized pH 0.309275 versus 0.305057, dissociation 0.075539 versus 0.068781, and precipitation 0.142826 versus 0.158283. These differences do not establish a history effect, though the precipitation difference warrants caution rather than an assertion of exact equivalence.\n\nAt high loading, batch 10 underwent a nominal threefold dilution from 1.2 to 0.4 mol/L. Its intermediate-to-final responses were:\n\n- normalized pH: 0.305190 to 0.304067;\n- dissociation: 0.078053 to 0.074100;\n- precipitation proxy: 0.142335 to 0.158206.\n\nThere is no resolved large departure from the plateau. This comparison is cross-instrument and cross-stage, so it is not a clean same-sensor kinetic trajectory.\n\nBatch 11 reached approximately the same final nominal concentration as batch 8 by staged reagent addition. Their final values are similar. Batch 12 added a 3,600-second hold at the batch-8 loading and likewise remained on the plateau. Its dissociation was lower than batch 8 by about 0.00819, which is not compelling relative to the combined final-assay noise scale.\n\nThus, the observations do not require a slow kinetic or path-dependent mechanism at the tested plateau conditions. They do not exclude slow dynamics near the low-loading transition or on longer timescales.\n\n## 4. Acid-dissociation coupling and revision of the archival estimate\n\n### 4.1 An effective pH\u2013fraction relationship\n\nFor a monoprotic acid, an effective dissociation relationship can be written as\n\nalpha = 1 / [1 + 10^(pKa_eff \u2212 pH)],\n\nor equivalently\n\npKa_eff = pH \u2212 log10[alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha)].\n\nI applied this transformation to the paired final-assay pH and dissociation values. This is a conditional interpretation: it assumes that the reported fraction corresponds to the acid/base ratio relevant to the reported pH. It does not establish an acid identity or prove that nominal reagent concentration is the analytical acid concentration.\n\nThe resulting batchwise effective pKa values are approximately:\n\n| Batch | Derived pKa_eff |\n|---|---:|\n| 1 | 5.398 |\n| 2 | 5.418 |\n| 3 | 5.402 |\n| 4 | 5.365 |\n| 5 | 5.309 |\n| 6 | 5.403 |\n| 7 | 5.358 |\n| 8 | 5.383 |\n| 9 | 5.364 |\n| 10 | 5.354 |\n| 11 | 5.331 |\n| 12 | 5.407 |\n\nTheir unweighted mean is 5.374. The observed range, approximately 5.31\u20135.42, is a descriptive spread, not a calibrated confidence interval.\n\nThe relationship is especially useful because the low-loading batch and the plateau batches yield similar transformed values despite their different pH and dissociation. A working effective value around 5.37\u20135.40 therefore gives a parsimonious coupling of these two channels.\n\nThe precision should not be overstated. At alpha near 0.07, an assay noise scale of 0.006 propagates to roughly 0.04 pKa units through the fraction transformation; the pH channel adds about 0.028 pH units from its stated noise scale. Correlations, clipping, and process-related uncertainty could alter that calculation. A provisional interpretation band of roughly 5.3\u20135.5 is more defensible than treating the mean as a precise thermodynamic constant.\n\n### 4.2 The archival pKa interval is not supported under the same interpretation\n\nThe supplied archival effective-pKa interval was 4.6094\u20134.7094, with stated 80% coverage and explicitly local scope. It is far below the values derived from the new paired measurements.\n\nFor example, near plateau pH 4.27, a pKa near 4.66 would imply a dissociation fraction near 0.29 under the monoprotic relationship, whereas observed final fractions are around 0.07. The discrepancy is not a subtle assay-noise effect.\n\nI therefore reject transfer of that interval as the effective pH\u2013fraction parameter for the present observations. This conclusion is conditional on the fraction having the intended acid-dissociation meaning. If the archival model and current channels use different effective pools or normalization conventions, the discrepancy could instead reflect a mismatch of definitions. The public observations cannot resolve that provenance question.\n\n### 4.3 The archival local dilution increment is not decisively rejected\n\nThe archive also predicted that diluting 0.001 mol from 0.018 to 0.054 L at 298.15 K would increase normalized pH by 0.003494987 and dissociation by 0.008187416.\n\nThe direct final-assay contrast between batches 1 and 2 gives increases of 0.006580 and 0.010823, respectively. Both are compatible with the archived increments at the rough scale of pairwise measurement noise. Using staged-dilution batch 3 instead gives increases relative to batch 1 of 0.002362 and 0.004066, also not decisive against the archival increments.\n\nMoreover, the experiment temperature was approximately 0.945 K below the archived anchor temperature, and no temperature response was measured. This is a scope mismatch, not an explanation I can quantify.\n\nThe correct conclusion is therefore asymmetric: the archival pKa interval is inconsistent with the current pH\u2013fraction pairing, but the small local dilution increment remains unresolved and compatible with the observations. Rejecting one component does not justify rejecting the other automatically.\n\n## 5. Why a simple unbuffered weak-acid model is insufficient\n\nFor an ideal, unbuffered monoprotic acid with analytical concentration C, negligible water autoionization, and no other charged species, one would expect approximately\n\nh = alpha C,\n\nKa = h alpha/(1 \u2212 alpha),\n\nwith h approximately 10^(\u2212pH) mol/L.\n\nThe second relation gives the useful effective pKa above. The first relation fails if C is identified with all nominal reagent added per litre.\n\nFor batch 1, the final pH is approximately 4.238, implying h around 5.8 \u00d7 10^\u22125 mol/L under an ideal activity-to-concentration interpretation. But alpha C_nom is approximately 0.00360 mol/L, over sixty times larger. At still higher nominal loading the inconsistency becomes larger because pH and alpha remain nearly constant while C_nom increases.\n\nEven batch 6 does not exactly satisfy that simple balance: its pH is approximately 4.718, corresponding to h around 1.9 \u00d7 10^\u22125 mol/L, while alpha C_nom is approximately 3.17 \u00d7 10^\u22125 mol/L.\n\nConsequently, nominal reagent loading cannot simply be inserted as the total dissolved concentration in that textbook mass balance. Possible reasons include an effective concentration transformation, an unobserved acid/base reservoir, counterions, phase partitioning, activity effects, or a synthetic proxy definition. These are competing explanations, not measured facts.\n\n## 6. A useful bounded effective model\n\nA defensible working model is a family rather than a uniquely identified equation:\n\nC_nom = n_added / V_liquid,\n\nz = g(C_nom, composition, history, temperature),\n\npH = H(z),\n\nalpha \u2248 1 / [1 + 10^(pKa_eff \u2212 pH)],\n\nP = Q(z, alpha),\n\nwhere z is an unobserved effective chemical coordinate and P is the public precipitation proxy.\n\nFor the conditions tested, g followed by H is strongly compressive at higher nominal concentration. P also approaches a plateau. An empirical implementation could use smooth saturation, a piecewise cap, or a phase-constrained effective dissolved pool. The observations do not choose among those mathematical forms.\n\nThe directly supported operating description is:\n\n- At C_nom approximately 0.000185 mol/L, the observed final state is near normalized pH 0.337, alpha 0.171, and P 0.034.\n- At C_nom approximately 0.00185 mol/L, alpha is intermediate, around 0.096, while P is already around 0.150; normalized pH is around 0.310.\n- At tested conditions from approximately 0.0185 to 1.2 mol/L, the response is broadly near normalized pH 0.305, alpha 0.072, and P 0.152.\n\nThese are observed anchors and a plateau summary, not a validated interpolation formula. In particular, there are too few low-loading points to determine transition curvature, a threshold, or a power law.\n\nOne possible physical realization would cap or strongly buffer the effective dissolved acid pool as nominal loading rises, stabilizing hydrogen activity and hence dissociation. A precipitation-related process could participate in that cap. An equally possible synthetic realization would apply bounded response functions directly to concentration and then generate the reported channels. The campaign identifies the bounded response more clearly than either realization.\n\n## 7. Precipitation: what is and is not identified\n\nThe precipitation proxy is low in batch 6 and near 0.15 in all other final assays. This supports a loading-dependent transition followed by a plateau. It does not show that 15% of reagent precipitated, that a solid phase was physically present, or that the proxy measures a conserved amount.\n\nThe loading intervention changes pH, dissociation, and precipitation together. Accordingly, several causal structures are compatible with the observations:\n\n1. Increasing effective concentration directly increases precipitation propensity and independently decreases dissociation.\n2. Speciation changes mediate precipitation propensity.\n3. Precipitation or phase buffering constrains dissolved composition, producing the pH and dissociation plateau.\n4. All three channels are functions of a common bounded internal coordinate without a separately identifiable physical causal chain.\n\nThe measurements do not isolate these alternatives. There was no independent pH intervention, dissolved-species assay, solid-phase measurement, or phase-selective mass balance.\n\nThere is also no basis for a general linear anticorrelation between precipitation and dissociation. Batch 5 has a higher dissociation fraction than the main plateau but essentially the same precipitation proxy, demonstrating that the two channels need not change proportionally.\n\n## 8. Competing explanations and identifiability\n\n### A. Bounded input mapping or clipped internal state\n\nA saturating transformation of nominal concentration naturally explains the extended plateau and the stronger response at the lowest loading. This is economical for a bounded synthetic world. However, no internal mapping or exact clipping threshold was observed, so I do not claim one as established.\n\n### B. Solubility or phase buffering\n\nA constrained dissolved pool could maintain nearly fixed pH and dissociation while total loading increases. This is qualitatively compatible with the observations. The precipitation proxy's own plateau does not disprove it, since the proxy is not a solid-mass measurement. Nevertheless, no real solid or solubility constant was identified.\n\n### C. Acid/base buffering or background charge balance\n\nAn unobserved buffer, neutralization fraction, or counterion pool could preserve a nearly constant pH at higher loadings and account for the failure of h = alpha C_nom. The campaign did not measure the ionic composition needed to test this explanation.\n\n### D. Readout saturation or common proxy construction\n\nThe assay channels could become insensitive even while the underlying state changes. The plateau is not at the declared 0 or 1 channel boundaries, so simple clipping to those output limits is not sufficient by itself. However, internal response saturation remains possible. Agreement between the intermediate pH measurement and final assay supports the broad low-loading contrast but does not prove independence of their underlying synthetic chemical representation.\n\n### E. Kinetic arrest or history dependence\n\nA frozen or slowly changing state could also give flat responses. The staged preparations and the one-hour hold provide no compelling evidence for a large effect at plateau conditions, making this explanation less necessary there. It remains inadequately tested in the transition region.\n\nNone of these alternatives should be replaced by a claim about a named reagent or catalyst. The reagent is anonymous, and the mechanism-to-species mapping is not public.\n\n## 9. Uncertainty, diagnostics, and limitations\n\nThe final-assay contract lists noise scales of 0.002 for normalized pH and 0.006 for both dissociation and precipitation. Intermediate measurements have larger listed scales: 0.004, 0.015, and 0.020. Pairwise differences require combined uncertainty; a change comparable to one single-measurement noise scale is not automatically a resolved effect.\n\nThe large batch-5/batch-6 contrast is robust relative to these scales. Most within-plateau contrasts are not. There was only one intermediate and one final measurement per batch, and these are different instruments or measurement stages, not technical replicates. The design provides limited replication and sparse coverage of the most informative low-loading region.\n\nFinal equilibrium_residual values were small, ranging from 0 to approximately 0.007740, with several values exactly zero. This is compatible with a small reported residual, but the bounded channel and its noise mean that zero is not proof of exact thermodynamic equilibrium.\n\nThe equilibrium_confidence diagnostic varied substantially even while the chemical-response channels were flat. For example, its final value was approximately 0.531 in batch 7 and 0.368 in batch 8. I have not treated it as my uncertainty, as a task score, or as a reason to favor a mechanism. It is an additional environment response whose governing dependence was not identified here.\n\nThe effective pKa calculation is a post hoc explanatory analysis of public observations, not an independently validated parameter fit. Its agreement across batches is useful evidence for coupling, but does not eliminate shared construction or correlated channel errors.\n\n## 10. Supported range and prospective implications\n\nThe observed domain comprises water-only preparations with nominal reagent amounts from 0.00001 to 0.024 mol, nominal final water volumes from 0.018 to 0.060 L, and nominal concentrations from approximately 0.000185 to 1.2 mol/L, near 297.2 K. Most conditions had no explicit hold; one plateau condition was held for 3,600 seconds. No conclusion about temperature dependence, alternative solvents, catalysts, very long times, or named real chemical systems is supported.\n\nWithin the higher-loading conditions already represented, a continuation of the approximate plateau is a reasonable interpolation hypothesis, subject to assay noise and possible small unresolved trends. Below the lowest tested concentration, further increases in pH and dissociation and a decrease in precipitation are plausible extrapolations, not observations. Neutral-pH limits, complete dissociation, zero precipitation, and the exact shape of the transition remain unknown.\n\nThe most informative distinctions for a future study would be matched-concentration amount/volume contrasts in the low-loading regime, denser concentration coverage between roughly 0.000185 and 0.00185 mol/L, repeated measurements at unchanged state, and an independent measure of dissolved or solid material. These are proposals only; no additional experiments were performed for this report.\n\n## 11. Conclusion\n\nThe strongest supported mechanism-level statement is that nominal aqueous loading drives a bounded effective state: the lowest-loading condition has higher pH and dissociation and much lower precipitation proxy, whereas a broad higher-loading range shows an approximately invariant chemical response. The pH and dissociation pairing is consistent with an effective monoprotic pKa near 5.37, substantially above the supplied archival interval. The archive's small local dilution increment, however, is not decisively contradicted.\n\nA simple unbuffered weak-acid model using all nominal reagent as dissolved analytical acid is inadequate. Bounded concentration mapping, buffering, phase constraints, and saturating public response functions remain reasonable competing explanations. The campaign establishes an effective response structure and its limitations, not a uniquely identified microscopic mechanism."
```json
}
```

```json
{
  "report": "# Retrospective review\n\nI refer to the sealed mechanism report as K1 and to the sealed blind predictions by their Q identifiers. No prediction truth has been supplied. This review uses only the existing observations, does not revise K1 or Q, and does not execute any additional experiment. Where I identify a better interpretation or design now, that is retrospective reasoning rather than a claim about what I had already concluded during the campaign.\n\n## 1. Initial claims: supported, contradicted, and untested\n\nThe initial information did contain substantive scientific claims. The material-information response supplied an archival effective-pKa interval of 4.6094\u20134.7094 and a local dilution prediction: for 0.001 mol diluted from 0.018 to 0.054 L at 298.15 K, normalized pH would increase by 0.003494987 and dissociation fraction by 0.008187416. These were explicitly local effective claims, not a complete mechanism.\n\n### The effective-pKa interval encountered contrary evidence\n\nK1 concluded that the final-assay pH\u2013fraction pairs implied an effective pKa near 5.37 under a monoprotic interpretation, with batchwise values approximately 5.31\u20135.42. Batch 6, despite being outside the higher-loading plateau, gave approximately 5.403 by the same transformation. This agreement across regimes makes the disagreement with the archival interval more consequential than a discrepancy in one batch.\n\nAs K1 stated, at plateau pH near 4.27, pKa near 4.66 would imply dissociation around 0.29, rather than the observed approximately 0.07. This is actual contrary evidence under the common pH\u2013fraction interpretation, not merely failure to confirm the archive.\n\nThe timing of that revision matters. The first batch already contained information that could have raised this issue, but I did not calculate the transformed pKa during laboratory execution. My contemporary commentary kept the archive as a hypothesis and later recognized the plateau. The explicit numerical rejection of the pKa interval occurred in the post-campaign analysis for K1. Thus, contradictory evidence was available before I articulated and quantified the revision. I should not portray the campaign as having promptly updated a fitted acidity parameter after batch 1.\n\nThe rejection remains conditional: if the archive and current dissociation channel refer to different effective pools or conventions, the mismatch could concern definitions rather than a changed acid constant. K1 appropriately did not identify a real chemical species or a thermodynamic constant.\n\n### The local dilution increments were not decisively contradicted\n\nThe final batch-2 minus batch-1 changes were approximately +0.006580 in normalized pH and +0.010823 in dissociation. Both were reasonably compatible with the smaller archival increments given the measurement scales and limited replication. Batch 3, prepared through staged dilution, also did not decisively contradict those increments.\n\nK1 therefore made an important distinction: rejecting transfer of the archival pKa interval did not justify rejecting its local dilution increment. That increment remains weakly compatible, not independently validated. Only a few noisy contrasts were available, and the experimental temperature was approximately 297.2 K rather than the archived 298.15 K.\n\n### Other initial statements were contractual scope, not independently tested mechanisms\n\nThe relations pH_normalized = pH/14 and the interpretation of precipitation_signal as a bounded proxy were instrument definitions. I used them; I did not independently verify their physical calibration. Likewise, the stated measurement-noise scales informed uncertainty assessments but were not validated by a dedicated repeatability study.\n\nTemperature dependence, solvent effects, catalyst effects, real precipitate identity, and transfer to real aqueous chemistry remained untested. The initial information explicitly cautioned against inferring real material mechanisms from anonymous benchmark identities. Nothing in the campaign removes that limitation.\n\n## 2. Experiments that formed or changed the explanation\n\n### The first anchor and dilution comparisons established the local problem\n\nBatches 1 and 2 were selected primarily because the archive supplied their amount and volume contrast. Batch 3 added a staged version of the dilution. These were hypothesis-directed choices, not products of a newly identified mechanism.\n\nThe measured local changes were small. They supported retaining the archival dilution response as plausible, but their power to distinguish a shallow concentration law, saturation, and ordinary measurement variation was limited. Batch 4, at the same nominal concentration as batch 1 but threefold greater amount and volume, supplied a useful scaling check. Its similarity to batch 1 was compatible with concentration dependence, although K1 correctly called it a weak test because both conditions were on the plateau.\n\n### Batches 5 and 6 provided the most important chemical contrast\n\nThe tenfold loading reduction from batch 5 to batch 6 at fixed nominal volume produced the clearest observed regime change: normalized pH increased by approximately 0.02749, dissociation by 0.07542, and precipitation proxy decreased by 0.11552. This contrast, rather than the small anchor dilution, established that the world was not simply flat over all tested conditions.\n\nBatch 6 changed the evidentiary picture from \u201clittle response over this range\u201d to \u201ca low-loading response connected to a broad plateau.\u201d Its intermediate measurement broadly corroborated the final assay. This was the principal reason for the sealed recommendation of batch 6 as an evidentiary anchor.\n\n### Batches 7\u20139 established the breadth of the plateau\n\nThe increase from 0.2 to 0.6 to 1.2 mol/L produced final dissociation fractions of approximately 0.0742, 0.0744, and 0.0718, with precipitation proxies near 0.15. These experiments were important because they showed that the near-flat response was not confined to the narrow archived dilution contrast.\n\nAfter nine batches, my contemporary commentary explicitly described \u201ca bounded or plateauing response rather than an unrestricted concentration law.\u201d That interpretation was formed during the campaign, unlike the later numerical pKa revision.\n\n### Batches 10\u201312 were checks rather than major mechanism discoveries\n\nHigh-loading dilution in batch 10, staged reagent addition in batch 11, and the one-hour hold in batch 12 did not establish a large history or time effect. They reduced the need to invoke such effects on the plateau. They did not prove equilibrium or path independence.\n\nThe detailed 12-batch sequence was established early, before the main low-loading and high-loading outcomes were available. The last three conditions were not freshly optimized in response to batch 6. That preserved a coherent preselected block, but it also meant that the campaign did not redirect coverage toward its most informative transition.\n\n### Which assumptions drove the choices?\n\nThe anchor conditions came directly from the archive. The low-loading and high-loading sweeps depended on standard scientific expectations that dilution could reveal dissociation behavior and that broad concentration coverage could expose saturation. The matched-concentration comparison assumed concentration was a plausible organizing variable. The staged-addition and hold tests assumed history and kinetics were credible alternatives. These were reasonable design hypotheses, but they were not verified facts when chosen.\n\nIn retrospect, I devoted too many independent final conditions to the plateau relative to the low-loading regime. That is a design limitation, not evidence that the plateau experiments were useless.\n\n## 3. Most important competing explanations\n\nK1 identified the central ambiguity accurately: a bounded effective response is more strongly established than its physical realization.\n\n### Bounded concentration mapping versus a physical dissolved-pool constraint\n\nAn internal saturating transformation of nominal concentration could directly generate the plateau. Alternatively, a solubility or phase constraint could stabilize a dissolved chemical pool while additional nominal loading accumulates elsewhere.\n\nThe existing assays cannot distinguish these explanations. The precipitation channel is not a solid-mass measurement, and there is no phase-selective composition balance. A plateau in that proxy cannot establish a solubility limit or rule one out.\n\n### Buffering or background charge balance\n\nK1 showed that using all nominal reagent as dissolved analytical acid fails the simple relation h = alpha C_nom. Batch 1 is already a strong example: the hydrogen concentration inferred from pH is far smaller than alpha times nominal concentration.\n\nThat finding disfavors a simple ideal, unbuffered acid model tied directly to total added reagent. It does not identify the replacement. A background buffer, counterions, effective reagent fraction, activity corrections, or a different definition of the reported dissociation pool could each contribute.\n\n### Concentration dependence versus separate amount or volume dependence\n\nBatches 1 and 4 provide some evidence for concentration as a useful coordinate. However, because they lie on the plateau, an amount-dependent or volume-dependent mechanism that also saturates could mimic their agreement.\n\nThis is especially important for Q03, Q04, and Q09, where predictions use concentration similarity despite changes in absolute amount or volume outside the tested low-loading configurations. The present experiments do not establish low-loading scale invariance.\n\n### Physical response saturation versus readout saturation\n\nAgreement between the pH-meter and final-assay trends supports a real distinction within the public measurement system. It does not establish that the two outputs arise from independent chemical information. They could share a bounded underlying synthetic state.\n\nThe plateau is not at the public channel limits of zero or one, so simple output clipping at those limits is insufficient as an explanation. Internal saturation or calibration compression remains possible.\n\n### Kinetics and history\n\nThe staged preparations and one-hour hold make a large plateau-specific history effect less necessary. They cannot exclude slow dynamics at low loading or near the transition. Because the before-and-after comparisons generally cross instruments and measurement stages, they are weaker kinetic tests than repeated observations with the same instrument under an unchanged protocol.\n\n## 4. The one additional complete experiment I would choose\n\nIf a new authorization allowed exactly one additional complete batch, I would prioritize a low-loading matched-concentration scale test rather than another plateau measurement.\n\nThe proposed condition would be:\n\n- Add 0.018 L water.\n- Add approximately 0.000003333 mol reagent.\n- Measure once with the pH meter.\n- Explicitly terminate.\n- Perform the final assay.\n\nThis gives the same nominal concentration as batch 6, approximately 0.0001852 mol/L, but one-third its amount and volume. The operations and per-action amounts are within the published action ranges. This is a hypothetical new allocation; the completed campaign has already exhausted its vessel and assay allotment, so I would not describe it as executable within the original remaining resources.\n\nThe reason for choosing this condition is that it directly addresses a major untested assumption behind the model and several blind predictions: whether concentration organizes the low-loading response independently of total amount and volume.\n\nPossible outcomes would have different implications:\n\n- If both measurements resemble batch 6, that would strengthen a concentration-based low-loading model and weaken substantial independent amount or volume effects at this scale. It would still not distinguish physical buffering from an internal bounded mapping.\n- If the response moves materially toward the plateau despite equal concentration, that would be contrary evidence to simple concentration scaling and would motivate amount-dependent, volume-dependent, background-pool, or procedural explanations.\n- If dissociation and pH remain coupled near the effective pKa while precipitation changes, the evidence would favor a separate control or normalization of the precipitation proxy rather than a single shared concentration response.\n- If intermediate and final results disagree strongly, as the pH channel partly did in batch 5, instrument or stage effects would become more central.\n\nOne batch would not settle these alternatives. The value is that it creates a contrast missing from the original design, rather than adding another point in an already flat region.\n\n## 5. Mechanistic identifiability versus operational score\n\nThe research objective was characterization, not yield optimization or maximization of the native score. My choices largely reflected that objective: broad concentration contrasts, a matched-concentration control, staged dosing, and a hold-time check.\n\nI did not select conditions by following equilibrium_confidence upward. For example, batch 7 had a final diagnostic value near 0.531, whereas batch 8 was near 0.368 despite similar chemical channels. I did not interpret that difference as evidence that batch 7 had a more trustworthy mechanism or should determine the final recommendation.\n\nThere was therefore no clear case of deliberately sacrificing identifiability to maximize operational score. However, avoiding score optimization does not automatically make a design highly identifiable. The main identifiability cost was spending many batches on plateau conditions and only two on the low-loading transition region. The plateau checks tested robustness and history, but they could not determine transition curvature or low-loading scaling.\n\nConversely, selecting batch 6 as the recommendation favored scientific discrimination rather than a demonstrated score maximum. Its low precipitation proxy and distinct chemistry were informative, not inherently favorable under all scalar weighting schemes. I did not compute and establish the highest-scoring completed batch as a scientific objective, and I should not claim that I knowingly accepted a quantified score penalty either.\n\nThe use of one intermediate pH measurement per batch provided useful corroboration, but it also consumed the entire intermediate-measurement allowance without technical replication. A design with fewer routine plateau intermediate measurements and more targeted replication might have supported stronger uncertainty estimates, subject to how measurements and batch limits were allocated.\n\n## 6. Underused evidence and weaknesses in the blind predictions\n\n### Underused public evidence\n\nThe analysis relied mostly on processed point estimates. The first pH packet exposed electrode response, a reported pH, and replicate pH values; these could have been examined more systematically for consistency with the normalized channel and declared noise. I did not establish an independent calibration analysis from those fields.\n\nThe final-assay responses supplied artifact references, but their full signal contents were not retrieved and analyzed during the campaign. A reference is not equivalent to inspected spectral or mass-balance evidence. I cannot now claim those unexamined packets demonstrated a solid phase, an effective dissolved concentration, or measurement independence.\n\nThe intermediate measurements were used mainly as qualitative corroboration. They could have received a more systematic paired analysis separating unchanged preparations from interventions. Batch 5's normalized-pH discrepancy, 0.322989 intermediate versus 0.309545 final, deserved particular attention. K1 disclosed it, but the model did not quantitatively explain it.\n\nThe variation in equilibrium_confidence and the small, sometimes clipped equilibrium_residual values were also not modeled. Excluding equilibrium_confidence from my own uncertainty was correct. Treating it as a separate observable could nevertheless have helped identify which public controls affect diagnostics differently from chemical channels, without converting it into a research score.\n\n### Least reliable point predictions\n\nQ08 is the weakest low-loading extrapolation. Its concentration is approximately fourteen times below the lowest tested concentration, and its amount is also below the tested range. The predicted dissociation of 0.47 and normalized pH of 0.381 extend a weak-acid-like relationship beyond the domain that established it. K1 explicitly left background buffering and bounded internal mappings unresolved. Those alternatives could produce substantially different trace-loading behavior.\n\nQ10 is the weakest high-loading extrapolation. Its nominal concentration, 6.667 mol/L, is far above the tested maximum of 1.2 mol/L, while amount and volume also move outside the studied ranges. Continuing the plateau is economical, but boundary behavior was not tested.\n\nQ09 and Q04 are vulnerable for a different reason: they borrow concentration-based expectations from batches 6 and 5 while using only 0.006 L and much smaller absolute amounts. K1 specifically warned that the matched-concentration evidence came from the plateau and did not establish low-loading scale invariance.\n\n### Intervals that may be too narrow\n\nAll the sealed intervals were judgment-based marginal predictive intervals, not empirically calibrated 80% coverage intervals. Twelve batches, sparse replication, and only one clear low-loading endpoint do not establish their coverage.\n\nQ08's intervals are broad relative to assay noise, but may still be too narrow relative to structural uncertainty. In particular, a qualitatively different trace-loading regime could fall outside the stated pH or dissociation ranges. The same concern applies to Q10: its bounds allow moderate deviation from the plateau, but not every plausible unobserved boundary regime.\n\nThe precipitation intervals for Q04 and Q09 may underrepresent uncertainty about whether the proxy depends separately on total amount, volume, or a phase-related quantity. A same-concentration argument is not sufficient to rule out those alternatives.\n\nQ05\u2013Q07 received identical predictions and relatively narrow intervals. This is consistent with the preferred final-state explanation, but could be overconfident if dosing order matters through an unobserved state. The historical tests did not prove exact path independence.\n\nQ12 is the strongest interpolation case, but even its comparatively narrow interval may be optimistic if the nominal noise model omits shared process variation or instrument-stage effects. By contrast, its location within the tested plateau makes it less structurally uncertain than Q08 or Q10.\n\nThere is also a delivery limitation: the sealed predictions listed only pH_normalized, acid_dissociation_fraction, and precipitation_signal. The public task named equilibrium_residual and equilibrium_confidence as observable metrics as well. If the request for every metric is read as covering all five task metrics, the sealed prediction response was incomplete. The fact that equilibrium_confidence is not my confidence does not make it ineligible for prediction as an environment output. I cannot repair that omission by adding predictions after sealing.\n\n### Consistency with K1\n\nThe Q rationales acknowledged most scope limitations, so the predictions were not presented as established laws. Nevertheless, numerical intervals can imply more calibrated knowledge than the prose supports. K1 said the exact low-loading shape, transition threshold, and physical cause were unidentified. The trace-loading point estimates and finite interval widths necessarily introduced stronger extrapolative assumptions. They should be understood as explicit bets under a preferred model, not as consequences uniquely implied by K1.\n\n## 7. Limits of the sealed recommendation and how to evaluate it\n\nThe recommendation selected completed batch 6: 0.00001 mol reagent in 0.054 L water, with an intermediate pH measurement followed by termination and final assay. Its stated rationale was evidentiary: it most clearly anchored the low-loading departure from the plateau.\n\nThat recommendation has several limits:\n\n- It is a single batch at that condition, not a replicated operating point.\n- Its distinctiveness was judged after observing the outcomes, so selection can emphasize an unusually clear realization.\n- Intermediate and final measurements support the broad pattern but are not independent batch replications.\n- It does not establish the best operating condition for a scalar objective, the best point for every scientific question, or a globally optimal experiment.\n- It applies only to the bounded aqueous world and preparation context actually studied.\n\n### Repeatability\n\nA future repeatability study should independently recreate the same preparation and measurement sequence, not merely remeasure the same vessel. It should assess between-batch variation, retain all outcomes, and compare the resulting distributions with the batch-6 values rather than requiring exact numerical replay. A separate deterministic replay check would test execution reproducibility, but would not by itself estimate scientific variability.\n\n### Local robustness\n\nSmall changes in amount, volume, and hold time around batch 6 would test whether the selected response occupies a stable neighborhood or a sensitive transition. Matched-concentration scaling would be particularly valuable. Measurements should distinguish preparation perturbations from differences caused by adding or omitting the destructive intermediate assay.\n\n### Transfer across materials and worlds\n\nNo alternate solvent or catalyst was tested. Transfer across materials would require a new, prospectively specified study, with attention to the warning that named solvent identities do not imply physically general kinetic or equilibrium behavior in this benchmark.\n\nTransfer across independently initialized worlds or parameter settings would require external validation across those worlds, retaining failures and distinguishing recalibration from genuine transfer. The present campaign cannot establish that the effective pKa, plateau, or low-loading transition is universal.\n\n### Sample-best is not proven optimal\n\nBatch 6 was not recommended as the sample's highest scalar score, and I did not establish such a ranking as the research objective. Even a confirmed sample-highest score would only identify the best observed realization among the tested conditions. Proving an optimum would require an explicit objective, adequate search coverage, uncertainty-aware comparisons, and robustness checks.\n\nThe defensible recommendation remains narrower: batch 6 is an informative observed anchor for further characterization of the low-loading regime. Its scientific value is real, but it is not a certificate of optimality, repeatability, or transferability."
}
```
